### [1] [A Polynomial-Scaling PDE Solver with Entanglement-Basis Tensor Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02316)
*Abhijatmedhi Chotrattanapituk,Michael J. Landry,Chu-Liang Fu,Mingda Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A tensor-network FEM for PDEs: lifting finite-element equations into an augmented coefficient space gives a unified quadratic residual minimization, solved element-by-element with DMRG/MPS at polynomial cost for bounded bond dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FEM for PDEs becomes expensive when the augmented problem space is large. The authors seek a solver that leverages the entanglement-basis representation from their companion work to handle boundary, initial, and inter-element constraints uniformly and efficiently.

Method: They lift nonlinear finite-element equations into an augmented coefficient space, recasting the PDE with boundary, initial, and inter-element constraints as a unified quadratic residual minimization. The augmented space grows exponentially with elements but has tensor-product structure, represented via tensor networks. Using MPS, DMRG sweeps enable element-by-element optimization without building the full space. Time-dependent problems are handled via implicit temporal discretization.

Result: For bounded bond dimension, computational cost scales polynomially with the number of finite elements. Convergence is demonstrated under both mesh and polynomial refinement for diffusion equations.

Conclusion: The entanglement-basis tensor-network formulation yields an efficient FEM framework unifying constraints and extending to time-dependent PDEs, with polynomial scaling and demonstrated convergence.

Abstract: We develop a finite element method (FEM) for partial differential equation (PDE) solver based on the entanglement-basis representation introduced in our companion work. By lifting non-linear finite-element equations into an augmented coefficient space, the governing PDE together with boundary, initial, and inter-element constraints can be expressed through a unified quadratic residual minimization. Although this augmented space grows exponentially with the number of elements, its tensor-product structure allows it to be represented efficiently using tensor networks. Using the matrix product state (MPS) as a concrete example, we show that density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) sweeps enable element-by-element optimization without explicitly constructing the full augmented space. For bounded bond dimension, the resulting computational cost scales polynomially with the number of finite elements. We extend the framework to time-dependent problems through implicit temporal discretization and demonstrate convergence under both mesh and polynomial refinement using diffusion equations.

</details>


### [2] [Flow Matching for Fast Posterior Sampling in Bayesian Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02377)
*Jan Blechschmidt,Oliver G. Ernst,Moritz Poguntke,Björn Sprungk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper assesses conditional flow matching as an amortized alternative to MCMC for PDE-based Bayesian inverse problems with function-valued parameters, deriving computable accuracy estimates and proposing an asymptotically exact hybrid sampler via Metropolization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MCMC, the standard tool for Bayesian inverse problems, is sequential, produces correlated samples, and must be rerun for each observation, making it costly. Conditional flow matching can amortize this cost by training a transport map once that yields independent posterior samples for any observation at negligible online cost without new likelihood evaluations—yet it needs careful evaluation.

Method: The authors provide an MCMC-literate assessment of flow matching for PDE-based inverse problems with function-valued parameters. Leveraging the flow's tractable density, they derive computable accuracy estimates of the approximate posterior in total-variation distance and KL divergence, and propose a hybrid sampler made asymptotically exact by Metropolization.

Result: The accuracy estimates are validated numerically, and amortization is demonstrated on several examples, including electrical impedance tomography and a likelihood-free Lotka-Volterra model.

Conclusion: Conditional flow matching offers a practical, amortized alternative to MCMC for Bayesian inverse problems, with computable accuracy guarantees and an asymptotically exact hybrid sampler enabled by Metropolization.

Abstract: Sampling from the posterior is the central task of computational Bayesian inverse problems. The standard workhorse in Bayesian inference - Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) - is sequential, yields correlated samples, and must be rerun for each observation. Conditional flow matching offers an amortized alternative: a transport map, trained once on joint samples of parameter and data, that yields independent approximate posterior samples for any observation at negligible online cost, without new likelihood evaluations. We give a careful, MCMC-literate assessment of flow matching for PDE-based inverse problems with function-valued parameters. Exploiting the flow's tractable density, we derive computable accuracy estimates of the underlying approximate posterior in total-variation distance and Kullback-Leibler divergence and, moreover, propose a hybrid sampler that is asymptotically exact by Metropolization. We validate the accuracy estimates and demonstrate the amortization in several numerical examples, including electrical impedance tomography and a likelihood-free Lotka-Volterra model.

</details>


### [3] [Strong error analysis of a stochastic exponential integrator for SPDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion with H<1/2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02412)
*Antoine Tambue,Aurelien Junior Noupelah,Louis Aime Fono*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies semilinear stochastic PDEs driven by additive fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter H in (0, 1/2), establishing well-posedness, space-time regularity, and strong convergence of a stochastic exponential integrator, with an H-dependent temporal convergence rate up to H + 1/2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional Brownian motion models anti-persistent temporal correlations in anomalous diffusion, hydrology, finance, and geophysics. Existing numerical analysis for SPDEs driven by fBm with H < 1/2 is limited, especially for non-self-adjoint operators and low temporal regularity.

Method: The authors analyze semilinear SPDEs with additive fBm (H in (0,1/2)), allowing non-self-adjoint linear operators. They combine analytic semigroup estimates, the canonical Hilbert-space structure of fBm, and Malliavin calculus to handle low temporal regularity, and study a stochastic exponential integrator for temporal discretization.

Result: They establish well-posedness and space-time regularity of the mild solution and prove strong convergence of the exponential integrator. An H-dependent strong temporal convergence rate is derived, reaching H + 1/2 under maximal spatial regularity. Numerical experiments on a stochastic advection-diffusion-reaction problem with heterogeneous Darcy flow confirm the rates, and Monte Carlo estimates of the solution mean are reported for various H.

Conclusion: The stochastic exponential integrator is effective for semilinear SPDEs driven by additive fBm with H < 1/2, achieving the predicted H-dependent convergence rates, including for non-self-adjoint operators and a practical advection-diffusion-reaction setting.

Abstract: Fractional Brownian motion provides a useful framework for modeling random phenomena with anti-persistent temporal correlations that arise in applications such as anomalous diffusion, hydrology, finance, and geophysical processes. In this paper, we study a class of semilinear stochastic partial differential equations driven by additive fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter $H\in(0,\frac12)$. We establish the well-posedness and space-time regularity of the mild solution and investigate the strong convergence of a stochastic exponential integrator for the temporal discretization. The analysis allows the linear operator to be non-self-adjoint and combines analytic semigroup estimates with the canonical Hilbert-space structure associated with fractional Brownian motion and Malliavin calculus to handle the low temporal regularity of the noise. We derive an $H$-dependent strong temporal convergence rate, which reaches $H+\frac12$ under maximal spatial regularity. Numerical experiments for a stochastic advection--diffusion--reaction problem with heterogeneous Darcy flow confirm the theoretical temporal convergence rates. We also estimate the mean of the solution with different Hurst parameters $H\in(0,\frac12)$ using the Monte Carlo method.

</details>


### [4] [Residual Minimisation for Transport-Based Optimal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02499)
*Tristan Pryer,Nikolaos Rekatsinas*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An a posteriori error estimate is developed for an optimal control problem governed by a stationary linear transport equation, with neural-network parameterisation of state, adjoint and control, and a Monte Carlo/quadrature error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by photon transport in radiotherapy treatment planning, where the control is an inflow boundary source and the observation may track angular flux or its angular integral as a simplified dose surrogate. The goal is a continuum-level a posteriori estimate that certifies errors in state, adjoint and control without relying on discretisation, and that extends to neural-network approximations.

Method: A residual least-squares functional is formulated whose minimisers coincide with solutions of the KKT optimality system. The state, adjoint and control are parameterised by neural networks and minimised over the network space using suitable quadrature, with a Monte Carlo formulation replacing residual integrals by weighted sample means. Convergence is proved under explicit approximation, quadrature and optimisation assumptions.

Result: The functional controls the L2 errors in state and adjoint and the flux-weighted L2 error in the control, giving a continuum-level a posteriori estimate valid for every admissible approximation. It handles different boundary measures in the control penalty and transport traces without a positive lower bound on |ω·n|, and an unweighted optimality residual certifies the control in the objective norm. Total error bounds are derived in terms of approximation, quadrature and optimisation error, and numerical examples in 2D and 3D illustrate the residual–error relation.

Conclusion: The residual framework provides a flexible and rigorous a posteriori certification tool for field-tracking transport control problems, including regular manufactured solutions, and its error analysis cleanly decomposes approximation, quadrature and optimisation contributions.

Abstract: We consider an optimal control problem governed by a stationary linear transport equation in two or three spatial dimensions, motivated by photon transport in radiotherapy treatment planning. The control is an inflow boundary source, and the observation operator may track either the angular flux or its angular integral, the latter serving as a simplified dose surrogate. The associated optimality system consists of a transport--adjoint pair with angular dependence, coupled through a boundary optimality condition.
  We formulate a residual least-squares functional whose minimisers coincide with solutions of the Karush--Kuhn--Tucker system and show that the functional controls the $L^2$ errors in the state and adjoint and the flux-weighted $L^2$ error in the control. This yields a continuum-level a posteriori estimate without recourse to discretisation. An additional unweighted optimality residual certifies the control in the norm of the objective. The estimate holds for every admissible approximation and accounts for the different boundary measures in the control penalty and transport traces, without a positive lower bound on $|ω\cdot n|$. We then parameterise the state, adjoint and control using neural networks and minimise over a neural network space using appropriate quadrature. The Monte Carlo formulation replaces each residual integral by a weighted sample mean, whose quadrature error enters the a posteriori estimate.
  We prove convergence under explicit assumptions on approximation, quadrature andoptimisation and derive bounds on the total error in terms of approximation, quadrature and optimisation error. Numerical examples in two and three dimensions illustrate the residual--error relation and the flexibility of the residual framework for field-tracking control problems, including regular manufactured solutions.

</details>


### [5] [Learning Closure of Dynamical Systems with Kernel Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02564)
*Evan Habbershaw,John Harlim,Senwei Liang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) closure modeling framework that identifies missing dynamical system components, handling both difference-equation and algebraic closure problems with demonstrated accuracy and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamical systems often have unresolved or missing components (closures) that must be modeled. Existing approaches like LSTM-based closures have limitations, motivating a principled KRR-based framework for two distinct closure problem classes.

Method: KRR-based framework for two closure types: (1) difference-equation closures (ODE/PDE) with a derived error bound quantifying time integration, unresolved-scale approximation, and interpolation contributions; (2) algebraic closures for moment closure in a 1D kinetic equation, modeling flux discrepancies via global KRR (PCA coordinates) versus spatially local models.

Result: For class 1: accurate long-horizon predictions on Lorenz-63 and Kuramoto-Sivashinsky, substantially outperforming an LSTM closure model. For class 2: global KRR works for unimodal initial conditions but deteriorates for bimodal; spatially local models with suitable inputs improve robustness and predictive accuracy.

Conclusion: KRR provides an effective closure modeling framework across both difference-equation and algebraic closure problems, and spatially local models enhance robustness for complex kinetic moment closure scenarios.

Abstract: We develop a closure modeling framework for identifying missing components of dynamical systems using Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR). The framework addresses two classes of closure problems: difference-equation closures arising in ODE and PDE settings, and algebraic closures arising from moment closure in kinetic equations. For the first class, we derive an error bound in an ODE setting that quantifies contributions from time integration, approximation of unresolved scales, and interpolation required to couple unresolved-scale effects to the resolved solver. Numerical experiments on the Lorenz-63 system and the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation demonstrate accurate long-horizon predictions and substantial improvements over an LSTM-based closure model. For the second class, we consider moment closure for a one-dimensional kinetic equation by modeling discrepancies between kinetic and macroscopic fluxes as a function of the resolved macroscopic variables. We compare global KRR models based on PCA coordinates with spatially local models. While the global model performs well for unimodal initial conditions, its accuracy deteriorates for bimodal initial conditions. Spatially local models with appropriate modeling inputs improve robustness and achieve higher predictive accuracy.

</details>


### [6] [Inferring constitutive forces from noisy Allen-Cahn movies with moment equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02672)
*Soobeen Jung,Hyunju Kim*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops observation-aware, noise-adjusted moment equations to infer Allen-Cahn constitutive forces from images, coupled with a surface-tension-calibrated constitutive transformer (SCCT) using a positive Bernstein inverse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring an Allen-Cahn constitutive force from images is hard beyond just having an accurate optimizer: nonlinear observation noise shifts the mean of force features, shared noise couples instruments to the response, and temporal balance borrowed from another solver can remain biased even on clean data.

Method: The authors derive observation-aware, noise-adjusted moment equations correcting these effects before inversion. The surface-tension-calibrated constitutive transformer (SCCT) couples the resulting moments to a positive Bernstein inverse and adapts its prior and precision from images and moments. A conditional stability bound separates moment residuals from prior error.

Result: Synthetic tests show correcting the estimating equation matters more than increasing regularizer flexibility; the benefit of learned adaptation is smaller and depends on the training comparison. Forces inferred from planar movies predict unseen planar and 3D evolutions, and a ring-cage breakup demonstrates that diffuse-interface accuracy distinguishes forces even when topological predictions agree, with external surface tension setting the energy scale separately.

Conclusion: Observation-aware moment corrections are the key to accurate force inference, outweighing regularizer flexibility, and enable generalization from planar images to unseen 2D and 3D evolutions.

Abstract: Inferring an Allen-Cahn constitutive force from images requires more than an accurate optimizer: nonlinear observation noise alters the mean of force features, shared noise couples instruments to the response, and a temporal balance borrowed from another solver can remain biased even on clean data. We develop observation-aware, noise-adjusted moment equations that address these effects before inversion. The surface-tension-calibrated constitutive transformer (SCCT) couples the resulting moments to a positive Bernstein inverse and can adapt its prior and precision from images and moments. A conditional stability bound separates moment residuals from prior error. Synthetic tests show that correcting the estimating equation matters more than increasing regularizer flexibility; the benefit of learned adaptation is smaller and depends on the training comparison. Forces inferred from planar movies predict unseen planar and three-dimensional evolutions. A ring-cage breakup shows why diffuse-interface accuracy distinguishes forces even when their topological predictions agree, while external surface tension sets the energy scale separately.

</details>


### [7] [Property-Preserving Data Compression for Incompressible Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02776)
*Andrey Pluzhnik,Ramansh Sharma,Martin Burtscher,Varun Shankar*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces property-preserving lossy compression for divergence-free velocity fields, using matrix-valued kernels and lossy coefficient compression to guarantee zero divergence (and periodicity) regardless of compression tolerance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard lossy compression of incompressible flow data does not guarantee that the decompressed velocity field remains divergence-free or periodic, corrupting physical constraints important for simulation and analysis.

Method: Nodal velocities are fit with property-preserving matrix-valued kernels; coefficients are compressed with standard lossy compressors so any decompressed coefficient vector yields a field satisfying the constraints. Several fitting schemes are compared: global Matérn interpolation, two-level Nyström approximation, Nyström–Wendland residual interpolation, multilevel Wendland interpolation, and a property-preserving redundant kernel frame, with derived nodal error bounds linking compressor tolerance to velocity error.

Result: Experiments with four compressors on cavity, Taylor–Green, and species-transport flows verify the error bounds and compare storage at matched accuracy. Low-rank methods are compact when they resolve the field accurately; residual, multilevel, and frame interpolation extend attainable accuracy otherwise. Changes in turbulent statistics are also measured.

Conclusion: The approach provides an accuracy–compression tradeoff while analytically preserving incompressibility and periodicity, making it suitable for compressing incompressible flow data.

Abstract: We develop a method for lossy compression of incompressible (i.e., divergence-free) velocity fields that preserves zero divergence and, on periodic domains, periodicity. We fit the nodal velocities with property-preserving matrix-valued kernels and compress their coefficients using standard lossy compressors. Every decompressed coefficient vector defines a field with the same constraints, independently of the compression tolerance. We present several fitting schemes designed around either low-rank representation of or sparsity in the interpolation matrix. More specifically, we show results for global Matérn interpolation, a two-level Nyström approximation, Nyström--Wendland residual interpolation, multilevel Wendland interpolation and a property-preserving (redundant) kernel frame. We derive nodal error bounds that relate the compressor tolerance to the additional velocity error. Experiments with four compressors on cavity, Taylor--Green, and species-transport flows verify the bounds under their coefficient-error assumptions and compare storage at matched velocity accuracy. The low-rank methods give compact representations when they resolve the velocity field sufficiently accurately; residual, multilevel, and frame interpolation extend the attainable accuracy when they do not. We also measure changes in turbulent velocity statistics. The resulting representations support an accuracy--compression tradeoff while preserving incompressibility and periodicity analytically.

</details>


### [8] [Efficient and Accurate Lift-and-Decoupling Generalized Transferable Neural Networks for Elliptic and Moving Interface Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02891)
*Tao Cheng,Junqing Jia,Lili Ju,Xiaoping Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes LD-GTransNet, a lift-and-decoupling generalized transferable neural network for efficiently and accurately solving elliptic and moving interface problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural network and traditional numerical methods struggle with high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries in interface problems, and conventional lift approaches suffer from redundancy.

Method: LD-GTransNet uses a multi-layer GTransNet approach with a lift-and-decoupling strategy: hidden-layer neuron parameters are preset, and physical coordinates plus an auxiliary variable are processed by separate subnetworks. This gives unified global approximation of piecewise smooth solutions without domain decomposition, reduces to a linear least-squares problem, and avoids nonlinear training.

Result: Extensive 2D and 3D benchmark interface experiments show LD-GTransNet consistently outperforms existing neural network-based and traditional numerical methods, especially for high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries.

Conclusion: LD-GTransNet provides a highly efficient and accurate method for elliptic and moving interface problems, combining expressivity with computational efficiency by eliminating nonlinear training.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a highly efficient and accurate method, called ``lift-and-decoupling generalized transferable neural network" (LD-GTransNet), for numerical solution of elliptic and moving interface problems. Our method makes use of the multi-layer GTransNet approach and incorporates a novel {lift-and-decoupling} strategy, in which the hidden-layer neuron parameters are preset and the physical coordinates and the added auxiliary variable are processed by separate subnetworks. Such design enables a unified global approximation of piecewise smooth solutions without explicit domain decomposition, while preserving network expressivity and avoiding the redundancy inherent in conventional {lift} approaches. The resulting formulation reduces to a linear least-squares problem, thereby eliminating the need of nonlinear training and ensuring the high computational efficiency and accuracy. Extensive numerical experiments on benchmark interface problems in two and three dimensions demonstrate that, compared with existing neural network-based and traditional numerical methods, our proposed LD-GTransNet consistently delivers superior performance, particularly for problems with high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries.

</details>


### [9] [From Continuous to Fully Discrete Carleman Estimates: A Transfer Principle for Parabolic Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02983)
*Qi Lü,Yu Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An abstract transfer theorem derives discrete parabolic Carleman estimates for backward Euler schemes from continuous Carleman inequalities, with applications to heat equation discretizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct discrete Carleman estimates rely on operator-specific weighted identities, which are technical and hard to generalize. The authors aim to separate PDE-specific Carleman analysis from discretization-specific verification.

Method: Prove an abstract transfer theorem: for fully discrete backward Euler schemes with conforming spatial reconstructions, and for time-independent positive self-adjoint operators, assign each discrete state a shifted continuous auxiliary problem whose discrete solution matches the state. Weighted space-time error estimates uniform over the discrete load space transfer the continuous Carleman inequality while preserving residual and observation terms.

Result: Hypotheses verified for the heat equation with consistent-mass P1 finite elements on locally graded meshes in 2D/3D and standard Cartesian finite differences in any dimension. The transferred estimate holds for Carleman parameters up to scale min{h^{-4/5}, (δt)^{-2/5}}. For bounded real-valued space-time potentials, this yields relaxed observability and approximate null controls with uniformly bounded cost and exponentially small terminal errors under independent refinements; weak accumulation points of reconstructed controls are continuous null controls.

Conclusion: The abstract framework cleanly transfers continuous Carleman estimates to discrete settings, providing sharp parameter regimes and control-theoretic consequences for common discretizations of the heat equation.

Abstract: Direct approaches to discrete parabolic Carleman estimates often rely on weighted identities tailored to the discrete operator. We prove an abstract transfer theorem that derives such estimates for fully discrete backward Euler schemes with conforming spatial reconstructions from a continuous Carleman inequality together with suitable weighted approximation and compatibility properties. For a class of time-independent positive self-adjoint operators, the key construction assigns to each discrete state a shifted continuous auxiliary problem whose discrete solution is exactly the prescribed state. Weighted space-time error estimates, uniform over the full discrete load space, then transfer the inequality while preserving the residual and observation terms of the original scheme. This separates the PDE-specific Carleman analysis from the numerical verification required for each discretization. We verify the hypotheses for the heat equation discretized by consistent-mass $P_1$ finite elements on locally graded meshes in two and three dimensions and by standard Cartesian finite differences in any fixed dimension. For the heat equation, the transferred estimate is valid for Carleman parameters up to the scale $\min\{h^{-4/5},(δt)^{-2/5}\}$. For bounded real-valued space-time potentials, the resulting estimates yield relaxed observability and approximate null controls with uniformly bounded cost and exponentially small terminal errors under independent spatial and temporal refinement; weak accumulation points of the reconstructed controls are continuous null controls.

</details>


### [10] [A Physics-Driven Multiscale Method for High-Frequency Helmholtz Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03144)
*Huangxin Chen,Jianhui Chen,Shubin Fu,Zhiyu Tan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a physics-driven multiscale model reduction method for the high-frequency Helmholtz equation, using local wave responses and generalized eigenvalue problems to build a reduced space, with rigorous stability and a priori error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-frequency Helmholtz problems are computationally expensive due to oscillatory solutions; there is a need for efficient model reduction that captures wave propagation while reducing global dimension and solution cost.

Method: A mixed finite element formulation with velocity elimination; local wave responses computed from boundary-excited Helmholtz problems on oversampled regions provide snapshots; local generalized eigenvalue problems select modes to form a reduced global system with sparse interelement coupling.

Result: A multiscale inf-sup condition governs stability, yielding well-posedness and quasi-optimality in a discrete energy norm, plus an a priori error bound quantifying coarse/fine mesh sizes, oversampling depth, spectral truncation, and wavenumber. Numerical experiments in 2D and 3D show accurate wavefields with substantial reductions in dimension and cost.

Conclusion: The analysis links local space reduction to global stability and accuracy, and the method is effective for homogeneous and strongly heterogeneous media, including perfectly matched layer truncations.

Abstract: We develop and analyze a physics-driven multiscale method for model reduction of the Helmholtz equation at high frequency. The method constructs a reduced approximation space from local wave responses within a mixed finite element formulation with velocity elimination. These responses are computed by solving Helmholtz problems with boundary excitations on oversampled regions, giving snapshots that incorporate the oscillatory behavior and propagation characteristics of the underlying medium. Local generalized eigenvalue problems then select modes from this physically informed space to form a reduced global system with sparse interelement coupling. To establish stability and convergence of this reduction, we relate the local spectral approximation to approximation of the global adjoint problem. Starting from an inf--sup estimate for the fine-grid formulation, this argument yields a multiscale inf--sup condition governed by fine-grid resolution, oversampling, and the first omitted local eigenvalues. The resulting stability establishes well-posedness and quasi-optimality of the multiscale approximation in a discrete energy norm. Together with local spectral estimates, it gives an a priori error bound that explicitly quantifies the roles of the coarse and fine mesh sizes, oversampling depth, spectral truncation, and wavenumber. This analysis provides a quantitative link between local space reduction and the stability and accuracy of the global approximation. Numerical experiments in two and three dimensions demonstrate accurate wavefield approximation with substantial reductions in global dimension and solution cost for homogeneous and strongly heterogeneous media, including perfectly matched layer truncations.

</details>


### [11] [Recursive Multilevel Decomposition of Pairwise Comparison Matrices: Algorithms and Inconsistency Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03184)
*Mert Çarboğa,Waldemar W. Koczkodaj,Yusuf Yaylı*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper derives a unique top-down decomposition of the residual matrix of positive reciprocal pairwise comparison matrices into Frobenius-orthogonal layers, with an O(n^2) parameter-extraction procedure and an ordering-conditioned residual-energy profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positive reciprocal pairwise comparison (PC) matrices are central to decision analysis, but existing tools (geometric-mean priorities, Koczkodaj inconsistency indicator) capture limited structural information. The authors seek a finer, assumption-free structural decomposition of inconsistency residuals.

Method: Work in entrywise logarithmic coordinates. Starting from the consistent-residual split, for any fixed ordering, decompose the residual matrix uniquely into mutually Frobenius-orthogonal layers via an active-matrix recursion (O(n^3)); also give an equivalent cumulative parameter-extraction procedure (O(n^2)). Entrywise exponentiation reconstructs the original matrix exactly as a Hadamard product.

Result: The decomposition exists for every positive reciprocal PC matrix of size n>=3 with no spectral, sparsity, or genericity assumptions. The squared Frobenius norms of layers yield an ordering-conditioned residual-energy profile. Numerical experiments confirm agreement between both procedures, exact reconstruction, mutual orthogonality, and machine-precision accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed layer decomposition offers new structural information complementary to geometric-mean priorities and Kii, with efficient computation and exact reconstruction, providing a principled ordering-dependent diagnostic for PC matrices.

Abstract: Positive reciprocal pairwise comparison matrices are studied in entrywise logarithmic coordinates. Starting from the established consistent-residual split, we show that, for any fixed ordering, the residual matrix admits a unique top-down decomposition into mutually Frobenius-orthogonal layers. The construction applies to every positive reciprocal PC matrix of size $n \geq 3$, without additional spectral, sparsity, or genericity assumptions. An explicit active-matrix recursion and an equivalent cumulative parameter-extraction procedure have computational complexities $O(n^3)$ and $O(n^2)$, respectively. The squared Frobenius norms of the layers define an ordering-conditioned residual-energy profile that provides structural information complementary to the geometric-mean priorities and the Koczkodaj inconsistency indicator (Kii). Entrywise exponentiation yields an exact Hadamard-product reconstruction of the original reciprocal matrix. Numerical experiments confirm agreement between the two computational procedures, exact reconstruction, mutual orthogonality, and numerical accuracy to machine precision.

</details>


### [12] [Reversible Stratonovich perturbations for accelerating convergence to equilibrium of Langevin dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03191)
*Guillaume Louis,Grigorios A. Pavliotis,Gilles Vilmart*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies sampling from probability distributions via Langevin dynamics with a reversibility-preserving Stratonovich perturbation, proving optimal scaling for Gaussian targets and constructing the perturbation algorithmically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling efficiently from probability distributions is a core problem; overdamped Langevin dynamics can be slow, motivating perturbations that preserve reversibility while improving convergence.

Method: Overdamped Langevin dynamics is perturbed by a Stratonovich perturbation preserving reversibility; optimal scaling is proved for Gaussian targets and an algorithm is derived to construct the perturbation.

Result: Optimal scaling for the Stratonovich perturbation is established for Gaussian target distributions, and numerical experiments show improved efficiency versus nonreversible samplers and standard overdamped Langevin dynamics, extending beyond the quadratic case.

Conclusion: The proposed reversibility-preserving Stratonovich perturbation yields an efficient, theoretically grounded sampling method that outperforms baselines in practice.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the problem of sampling from a probability distribution using Langevin-type dynamics. We consider overdamped Langevin dynamics perturbed by adding a Stratonovich perturbation that preserves reversibility. We prove an optimal scaling for the Stratonovich perturbation applied to Gaussian target distributions and derive an algorithm to construct such perturbation. Our theoretical results are supplemented by numerical experiments in which we compare our proposed method with nonreversible samplers and overdamped Langevin dynamics. Our numerical experiments demonstrate the efficiency of the approach beyond the quadratic case.

</details>


### [13] [Simultaneous Global Convergence Failure of Newton's and Halley's Methods in Degree Four](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03246)
*Alessio Basti,Tommaso Cremaschi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper constructs an explicit quartic polynomial where both Newton's and Halley's methods fail to converge to a root on nonempty open sets, filling a degree-four gap via a reproducible computational search and an analytic contraction argument.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identified simultaneous failure of Newton's and Halley's root-finding methods at various degrees, but the degree-four case remained open. This paper fills that gap.

Method: The authors search computationally within a one-parameter family of quartics designed to exhibit a prescribed Newton two-cycle, then prove analytically that Newton's method has a superattracting two-cycle and Halley's method has a distinct attracting extraneous two-cycle using a two-interval contraction argument.

Result: An explicit quartic is found for which Newton's and Halley's methods both fail to converge to a root on nonempty open sets. Numerical evidence also identifies another quartic with simultaneous failure of Newton's, Halley's, and Schröder's methods.

Conclusion: The degree-four gap in the simultaneous Newton–Halley failure problem is resolved, and the computational approach suggests extensions to simultaneous failure of multiple root-finding methods.

Abstract: We construct an explicit quartic polynomial for which Newton's and Halley's root-finding methods both fail to converge to a root on nonempty open sets of initial values. The example can be found through a reproducible computational search within a one-parameter family of quartics designed to exhibit a prescribed Newton two-cycle. We then prove analytically that Newton's method has a superattracting two-cycle and that Halley's method has a distinct attracting extraneous two-cycle, using a two-interval contraction argument. This fills a degree-four gap in the simultaneous Newton--Halley failure problem. The computational approach also identifies another quartic with numerical evidence of simultaneous failure of Newton's, Halley's, and Schröder's methods.

</details>


### [14] [On the Choice of Continuous and Discontinuous Lagrange Multipliers in Fictitious-Domain Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03267)
*Najwa Alshehri,Shumoua F. Alrzqi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies two low-order Fictitious-Domain Distributed Lagrange Multiplier (FD-DLM) discretizations for elliptic interface problems with non-matching meshes, comparing continuous vs. discontinuous multiplier spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FD-DLM handles elliptic interface problems with separate meshes without requiring interface-background mesh matching, but the choice between continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces and its effect on coupling accuracy is unclear.

Method: Compares two stable, low-order FD-DLM discretizations using continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces, distinguishing per-element weak coupling constraint satisfaction from the discrepancy between the two discrete primal fields.

Result: Continuous formulation usually yields smaller local errors and maximum mismatch values; discontinuous formulation shows more regular mismatch decrease under mesh refinement and smaller primal errors.

Conclusion: Continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces offer distinct accuracy trade-offs, guiding the choice of FD-DLM formulation depending on whether local error control or primal accuracy and convergence regularity is prioritized.

Abstract: This article examines the Fictitious-Domain approach using Distributed Lagrange Multipliers (FD-DLM), a method that handles elliptic interface problems with separate meshes without requiring the interface to match the background mesh. It compares two stable, low-order FD-DLM discretizations when using both continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces. The study distinguishes between satisfying the weak coupling constraint per element and the size of the discrepancy between the two discrete primal fields. Numerical experiments indicate that the continuous formulation usually yields smaller local errors and maximum mismatch values, while the discontinuous formulation shows a more regular decrease in mismatch as the mesh is refined and has smaller primal errors.

</details>


### [15] [Fully mixed finite element methods for the coupling of viscoelasticity and reaction-diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03317)
*Alonso J. Bustos,Jeonghun J. Lee,Ricardo Ruiz-Baier*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes and analyzes a fully mixed finite element method for a two-way coupled mechanochemical model of calcium signaling in viscoelastic tissue, proving well-posedness and optimal a priori error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calcium signaling in viscoelastic tissue involves bidirectional coupling between mechanics and chemistry, requiring a robust numerical framework that handles mixed formulations and weak symmetry constraints.

Method: A fully mixed finite element method is developed: the mechanical response follows a Kelvin-Voigt law with elastic/viscous stresses, velocity, and spin tensor as unknowns and weak total-stress symmetry; the reaction-diffusion system for calcium and inactivated IP3 is formulated in mixed form with diffusive flux as an extra unknown. Global existence and uniqueness are proven via reduction to an integro-differential problem, and a semidiscrete scheme is analyzed with a divergence-commuting, weak-symmetry-preserving interpolation operator.

Result: The semidiscrete scheme is well posed under a single inf-sup condition, yielding optimal a priori error estimates. Explicit convergence rates are derived for three families of weakly symmetric finite elements, including superconvergent local post-processing of displacement for one family. Two- and three-dimensional numerical tests confirm predicted rates.

Conclusion: The proposed mixed finite element framework provides a rigorous and accurate approach for coupled mechanochemical calcium signaling, validated by benchmarks and mechanically driven calcium wavetrains.

Abstract: We propose and analyse a fully mixed finite element method for a two-way coupled mechanochemical model of calcium signalling in viscoelastic tissue. The mechanical response follows a Kelvin--Voigt law, with the elastic and viscous stresses, velocity, and spin tensor as unknowns, and the symmetry of the total stress imposed weakly. The reaction-diffusion system for calcium and inactivated inositol triphosphate is also formulated in mixed form, introducing the diffusive flux as an additional unknown. The coupling is bidirectional: calcium generates an active stress, while local dilation feeds back into the calcium dynamics. We establish global existence and uniqueness by reducing the coupled system to an integro-differential problem and subsequently recovering the mixed variables. The semidiscrete scheme is formulated for general conforming finite element spaces and shown to be well posed under a single inf-sup condition. An interpolation operator that commutes with the divergence and preserves the weak symmetry pairing yields optimal a priori error estimates. We specialise the analysis to three families of weakly symmetric finite elements, deriving explicit convergence rates and, for one family, a superconvergent local post-processing of the displacement. Numerical tests in two and three dimensions confirm the predicted convergence rates and illustrate the method through viscoelastic benchmarks and mechanically driven calcium wavetrains.

</details>


### [16] [Energetic Variational Deep Learning for Solving High-Dimensional Stationary Fokker-Planck Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03325)
*Fanze Kong,Zhouyuan Qin,Shuangquan Xie*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A deep learning framework solves high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations via Monte Carlo weak supervision, energy-variational physics constraints, and quasi-Newton refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations are challenging to solve, and existing methods struggle with scale anchoring, physics consistency, and general drift forms.

Method: Combines Monte Carlo weak supervision (anchoring stationary density scale), a free-energy variational loss (enforcing equilibrium physics), and quasi-Newton refinement; extends to systems with pointwise orthogonal rotational components and, via test functions, more general non-gradient drift.

Result: Accurate and robust density approximations across various high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck problems, including rough-potential and noisy-data settings.

Conclusion: The framework provides an effective, generalizable approach for high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations and broader non-gradient drift systems.

Abstract: This paper develops a deep learning framework for solving high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations by combining Monte Carlo weak supervision, energy-variational physics constraints, and quasi-Newton refinement. Monte Carlo statistics anchor the scale of the stationary density, while a free-energy variational loss enforces equilibrium physics; the formulation also applies to stationary systems with pointwise orthogonal rotational components and, using a family of test functions, to systems with more general non-gradient drift. Numerical results demonstrate accurate and robust density approximations across a range of high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck problems, including rough-potential and noisy-data settings.

</details>


### [17] [Learning the Willmore flow with a lightweight neural operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03534)
*Elie Bretin,Roland Denis,Tokuhiro Eto,Simon Masnou*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The authors extend a phase-field neural operator approach (originally for mean curvature motion) to the Willmore flow, developing a lightweight single-step architecture that enables 2D/3D curve and surface reconstruction from unoriented point clouds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper builds on Bretin et al. (2022), which proposed a phase-field neural operator for mean curvature motion, to tackle the harder Willmore flow problem with fast, learnable solvers.

Method: A single-time-step neural operator motivated by a Bence-Merriman-Osher-type expansion of the Allen–Cahn semigroup, trained on trajectories from a hybrid minimizing movement scheme. Radial convolution kernels are used to avoid anisotropic dynamics, and 3D kernels are constructed from 2D kernels via radial Fourier profiles.

Result: Unconstrained convolution kernels produce anisotropic dynamics in non-oriented interface experiments, while radial kernels improve the evolution. The transferred 2D-to-3D kernel provides effective 3D initialization, enabling reconstruction from unoriented point clouds without additional 3D training.

Conclusion: The phase-field neural operator generalizes to Willmore flow, with radial kernels and dimensional transfer yielding an efficient lightweight model applicable to curve and surface reconstruction from unoriented point clouds.

Abstract: We extend to the Willmore flow the phase-field neural operator approach proposed by Bretin, Denis, Masnou, and Terii (2022) for mean curvature motion in two and three dimensions. The resulting lightweight, single-time-step architecture is motivated by a Bence-Merriman-Osher-type expansion of the Allen-Cahn semigroup, and is trained using trajectories generated by a hybrid minimizing movement scheme. In our experiments with non-oriented interfaces, unconstrained convolution kernels produce anisotropic dynamics, whereas radial kernels enhance the evolution process. We also construct three-dimensional kernels from trained two-dimensional kernels via their radial Fourier profiles, which provides an effective initialization for the non-oriented 3D model. Finally, we show applications to curve and surface reconstruction from unoriented point clouds, including reconstruction using the transferred model without the need for additional 3D training.

</details>


### [18] [Error Estimates in Physics-Informed Neural Networks to Heat-Wave Fluid-Structure Interactive System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03575)
*Pelin G. Geredeli,Mohammad Mahabubur Rahman,Deepanshu Verma*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper provides the first error estimates for physics-informed neural network (PINN) approximation of a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system (heat-wave model). Errors are bounded by PINN residuals, and generalization error is controlled by training error, quadrature points, and network size, supported by numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs show promise for solving PDEs, but theoretical error analysis exists mainly for single PDEs. Coupled fluid-structure interaction systems (like Stokes-elastic) are more challenging, and no error estimates existed for PINN approximations of such coupled systems.

Method: The authors analyze a PINN approximation of a canonical heat-wave model coupling a parabolic and hyperbolic equation via transmission conditions on a fixed boundary interface. They bound the error between exact and PINN solutions using the associated PINN residuals, and derive generalization error bounds in terms of training error, number of quadrature points, and network size.

Result: The paper establishes theoretical error estimates for PINN approximation of a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system—claimed as the first such estimates. Numerical experiments are also conducted to support the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: This work provides a theoretical foundation for using PINNs on coupled fluid-structure interaction problems, showing that error is controlled by residuals and generalization error depends on training error, quadrature points, and network size.

Abstract: We study a physics-informed neural network (PINN) approximation of a coupled PDE system that models the interaction between fluid and elastic dynamics. The canonical heat-wave model considered here couples a parabolic and a hyperbolic equation through transmission conditions on a fixed boundary interface, and serves as a prototype for the Stokes-elastic systems that arises in real world applications. This coupling makes the error analysis more delicate than that for a single PDE. We bound the error between the exact and PINN solutions by the associated PINN residuals, and we moreover prove that the generalization error is controlled by the training error, the number of quadrature points, and the size of the network. {To the best of our knowledge}, these are the first error estimates for a PINN approximation of a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system. We also show that numerical experiments with respect to a given test problem.

</details>


### [19] [A Polynomial-Scaling PDE Solver with Entanglement-Basis Tensor Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02316)
*Abhijatmedhi Chotrattanapituk,Michael J. Landry,Chu-Liang Fu,Mingda Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A finite element PDE solver built on a tensor-network (MPS) representation of an augmented coefficient space, using DMRG sweeps for scalable element-by-element optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard FEM for nonlinear PDEs with boundary, initial, and inter-element constraints is costly; a unified and efficient representation is desirable. The augmented space from the companion work grows exponentially, motivating a compact tensor-network encoding.

Method: Lift nonlinear finite-element equations into an augmented coefficient space, expressing the PDE with all constraints as a unified quadratic residual minimization. Exploit the tensor-product structure via matrix product states and optimize with DMRG sweeps, avoiding explicit construction of the full space. Extend to time-dependent problems via implicit temporal discretization.

Result: With bounded bond dimension, computational cost scales polynomially in the number of finite elements. Convergence demonstrated under both mesh and polynomial refinement using diffusion equations.

Conclusion: Tensor-network-based FEM offers a scalable, unified framework for nonlinear and time-dependent PDEs, achieving polynomial scaling and confirmed convergence.

Abstract: We develop a finite element method (FEM) for partial differential equation (PDE) solver based on the entanglement-basis representation introduced in our companion work. By lifting non-linear finite-element equations into an augmented coefficient space, the governing PDE together with boundary, initial, and inter-element constraints can be expressed through a unified quadratic residual minimization. Although this augmented space grows exponentially with the number of elements, its tensor-product structure allows it to be represented efficiently using tensor networks. Using the matrix product state (MPS) as a concrete example, we show that density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) sweeps enable element-by-element optimization without explicitly constructing the full augmented space. For bounded bond dimension, the resulting computational cost scales polynomially with the number of finite elements. We extend the framework to time-dependent problems through implicit temporal discretization and demonstrate convergence under both mesh and polynomial refinement using diffusion equations.

</details>


### [20] [Flow Matching for Fast Posterior Sampling in Bayesian Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02377)
*Jan Blechschmidt,Oliver G. Ernst,Moritz Poguntke,Björn Sprungk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Conditional flow matching is assessed as an amortized alternative to MCMC for PDE-based Bayesian inverse problems with function-valued parameters, providing computable accuracy estimates and a hybrid exact sampler.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MCMC, the standard approach for Bayesian inverse problems, is sequential, produces correlated samples, and must be rerun for every new observation. There is a need for an amortized method that yields independent posterior samples at negligible online cost without new likelihood evaluations.

Method: The authors give an MCMC-literate assessment of conditional flow matching for PDE-based inverse problems with function-valued parameters. Exploiting the flow's tractable density, they derive computable accuracy estimates of the approximate posterior in total-variation distance and KL divergence, and propose a hybrid sampler made asymptotically exact via Metropolization.

Result: The accuracy estimates are validated and amortization is demonstrated in numerical examples, including electrical impedance tomography and a likelihood-free Lotka-Volterra model.

Conclusion: Conditional flow matching offers a viable amortized posterior sampling alternative to MCMC, with quantifiable accuracy and an asymptotically exact hybrid sampler for reliable Bayesian inference.

Abstract: Sampling from the posterior is the central task of computational Bayesian inverse problems. The standard workhorse in Bayesian inference - Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) - is sequential, yields correlated samples, and must be rerun for each observation. Conditional flow matching offers an amortized alternative: a transport map, trained once on joint samples of parameter and data, that yields independent approximate posterior samples for any observation at negligible online cost, without new likelihood evaluations. We give a careful, MCMC-literate assessment of flow matching for PDE-based inverse problems with function-valued parameters. Exploiting the flow's tractable density, we derive computable accuracy estimates of the underlying approximate posterior in total-variation distance and Kullback-Leibler divergence and, moreover, propose a hybrid sampler that is asymptotically exact by Metropolization. We validate the accuracy estimates and demonstrate the amortization in several numerical examples, including electrical impedance tomography and a likelihood-free Lotka-Volterra model.

</details>


### [21] [Strong error analysis of a stochastic exponential integrator for SPDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion with H<1/2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02412)
*Antoine Tambue,Aurelien Junior Noupelah,Louis Aime Fono*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Studies semilinear SPDEs driven by additive fractional Brownian motion with H in (0,1/2), establishing well-posedness, space-time regularity, and strong convergence of a stochastic exponential integrator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional Brownian motion models anti-persistent temporal correlations in anomalous diffusion, hydrology, finance, and geophysics. Existing numerical analysis for SPDEs with fBm is limited, especially for rough noise (H < 1/2) and non-self-adjoint operators.

Method: Mild solution framework; combines analytic semigroup estimates with the canonical Hilbert-space structure of fBm and Malliavin calculus; allows non-self-adjoint linear operators; stochastic exponential integrator for temporal discretization.

Result: Well-posedness and space-time regularity established; H-dependent strong temporal convergence rate, reaching H+1/2 under maximal spatial regularity; numerical experiments on a stochastic advection-diffusion-reaction problem with heterogeneous Darcy flow confirm the rates; Monte Carlo estimation of the solution mean for various H.

Conclusion: The proposed framework and exponential integrator are effective for semilinear SPDEs with rough fBm noise, with theory validated numerically.

Abstract: Fractional Brownian motion provides a useful framework for modeling random phenomena with anti-persistent temporal correlations that arise in applications such as anomalous diffusion, hydrology, finance, and geophysical processes. In this paper, we study a class of semilinear stochastic partial differential equations driven by additive fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter $H\in(0,\frac12)$. We establish the well-posedness and space-time regularity of the mild solution and investigate the strong convergence of a stochastic exponential integrator for the temporal discretization. The analysis allows the linear operator to be non-self-adjoint and combines analytic semigroup estimates with the canonical Hilbert-space structure associated with fractional Brownian motion and Malliavin calculus to handle the low temporal regularity of the noise. We derive an $H$-dependent strong temporal convergence rate, which reaches $H+\frac12$ under maximal spatial regularity. Numerical experiments for a stochastic advection--diffusion--reaction problem with heterogeneous Darcy flow confirm the theoretical temporal convergence rates. We also estimate the mean of the solution with different Hurst parameters $H\in(0,\frac12)$ using the Monte Carlo method.

</details>


### [22] [Residual Minimisation for Transport-Based Optimal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02499)
*Tristan Pryer,Nikolaos Rekatsinas*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a residual least-squares framework for an optimal control problem governed by a stationary linear transport equation (photon transport in radiotherapy), proves that minimizers coincide with KKT solutions and that the functional controls state/adjoint/control errors, then extends it to a neural-network (Monte Carlo) discretization with a full convergence and error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by photon transport in radiotherapy treatment planning, where the control is an inflow boundary source and one may track either the angular flux or its angular integral (a dose surrogate). The goal is to obtain a posteriori error control at the continuum level, valid for any admissible approximation and robust to the absence of a positive lower bound on |ω·n|.

Method: Formulates a residual least-squares functional whose minimizers coincide with solutions of the KKT optimality system (a transport–adjoint pair coupled via a boundary optimality condition). Estimates use different boundary measures in the control penalty and transport traces, plus an additional unweighted optimality residual. State, adjoint and control are parameterized by neural networks; Monte Carlo quadrature replaces residual integrals, and quadrature error enters the a posteriori estimate.

Result: Proves that the functional controls L² errors in state and adjoint and the flux-weighted L² error in the control, yielding a continuum-level a posteriori estimate for every admissible approximation without a positive lower bound on |ω·n|. Provides convergence proofs under explicit assumptions on approximation, quadrature and optimization, with total error bounds in these three contributions. Numerical examples in 2D and 3D confirm the residual–error relation and demonstrate flexibility for field-tracking control problems, including manufactured solutions.

Conclusion: The residual least-squares approach gives rigorous, discretization-independent a posteriori error control for transport optimal control and extends naturally to neural-network approximations with quadrature, yielding a fully certified error decomposition for radiotherapy-relevant field-tracking control problems.

Abstract: We consider an optimal control problem governed by a stationary linear transport equation in two or three spatial dimensions, motivated by photon transport in radiotherapy treatment planning. The control is an inflow boundary source, and the observation operator may track either the angular flux or its angular integral, the latter serving as a simplified dose surrogate. The associated optimality system consists of a transport--adjoint pair with angular dependence, coupled through a boundary optimality condition.
  We formulate a residual least-squares functional whose minimisers coincide with solutions of the Karush--Kuhn--Tucker system and show that the functional controls the $L^2$ errors in the state and adjoint and the flux-weighted $L^2$ error in the control. This yields a continuum-level a posteriori estimate without recourse to discretisation. An additional unweighted optimality residual certifies the control in the norm of the objective. The estimate holds for every admissible approximation and accounts for the different boundary measures in the control penalty and transport traces, without a positive lower bound on $|ω\cdot n|$. We then parameterise the state, adjoint and control using neural networks and minimise over a neural network space using appropriate quadrature. The Monte Carlo formulation replaces each residual integral by a weighted sample mean, whose quadrature error enters the a posteriori estimate.
  We prove convergence under explicit assumptions on approximation, quadrature andoptimisation and derive bounds on the total error in terms of approximation, quadrature and optimisation error. Numerical examples in two and three dimensions illustrate the residual--error relation and the flexibility of the residual framework for field-tracking control problems, including regular manufactured solutions.

</details>


### [23] [Learning Closure of Dynamical Systems with Kernel Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02564)
*Evan Habbershaw,John Harlim,Senwei Liang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A Kernel Ridge Regression framework is developed to model missing closure terms in dynamical systems, covering difference-equation closures (ODE/PDE) and algebraic moment closures in kinetic equations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolved-scale simulations of dynamical systems omit unresolved-scale effects, requiring closure models. The paper seeks a data-driven framework to identify these missing components, addressing both difference-equation and algebraic closure problems.

Method: KRR-based closure modeling. For ODE/PDE difference-equation closures, an error bound quantifying time integration, unresolved-scale approximation, and interpolation contributions. For kinetic moment closure, KRR models discrepancy between kinetic and macroscopic fluxes, comparing global PCA-coordinate models with spatially local models.

Result: Lorenz-63 and Kuramoto-Sivashinsky experiments show accurate long-horizon predictions and substantial improvement over an LSTM-based closure. For moment closure, the global model works for unimodal initial conditions but degrades for bimodal ones; spatially local models with suitable inputs are more robust and accurate.

Conclusion: KRR provides an effective closure modeling framework. Local modeling inputs improve robustness and accuracy, especially for challenging (bimodal) conditions, while the derived error bound guides the difference-equation closure approach.

Abstract: We develop a closure modeling framework for identifying missing components of dynamical systems using Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR). The framework addresses two classes of closure problems: difference-equation closures arising in ODE and PDE settings, and algebraic closures arising from moment closure in kinetic equations. For the first class, we derive an error bound in an ODE setting that quantifies contributions from time integration, approximation of unresolved scales, and interpolation required to couple unresolved-scale effects to the resolved solver. Numerical experiments on the Lorenz-63 system and the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation demonstrate accurate long-horizon predictions and substantial improvements over an LSTM-based closure model. For the second class, we consider moment closure for a one-dimensional kinetic equation by modeling discrepancies between kinetic and macroscopic fluxes as a function of the resolved macroscopic variables. We compare global KRR models based on PCA coordinates with spatially local models. While the global model performs well for unimodal initial conditions, its accuracy deteriorates for bimodal initial conditions. Spatially local models with appropriate modeling inputs improve robustness and achieve higher predictive accuracy.

</details>


### [24] [Inferring constitutive forces from noisy Allen-Cahn movies with moment equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02672)
*Soobeen Jung,Hyunju Kim*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces the surface-tension-calibrated constitutive transformer (SCCT) to infer Allen-Cahn constitutive forces from images, correcting observation and noise biases in the moment equations before inversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring an Allen-Cahn constitutive force from images is hard beyond optimizer accuracy: nonlinear observation noise shifts the mean of force features, shared noise couples instruments to the response, and a temporal balance borrowed from another solver stays biased even on clean data.

Method: The authors derive observation-aware, noise-adjusted moment equations that correct these effects before inversion. SCCT couples the resulting moments to a positive Bernstein inverse and adapts its prior and precision from images and moments. A conditional stability bound separates moment residuals from prior error.

Result: Synthetic tests show correcting the estimating equation matters more than increasing regularizer flexibility; learned adaptation yields smaller, comparison-dependent gains. Forces inferred from planar movies predict unseen planar and 3D evolutions. A ring-cage breakup illustrates that diffuse-interface accuracy distinguishes forces even when topological predictions agree.

Conclusion: Correcting biased moment equations is the dominant factor for accurate force inference, whereas the energy scale is set separately by external surface tension.

Abstract: Inferring an Allen-Cahn constitutive force from images requires more than an accurate optimizer: nonlinear observation noise alters the mean of force features, shared noise couples instruments to the response, and a temporal balance borrowed from another solver can remain biased even on clean data. We develop observation-aware, noise-adjusted moment equations that address these effects before inversion. The surface-tension-calibrated constitutive transformer (SCCT) couples the resulting moments to a positive Bernstein inverse and can adapt its prior and precision from images and moments. A conditional stability bound separates moment residuals from prior error. Synthetic tests show that correcting the estimating equation matters more than increasing regularizer flexibility; the benefit of learned adaptation is smaller and depends on the training comparison. Forces inferred from planar movies predict unseen planar and three-dimensional evolutions. A ring-cage breakup shows why diffuse-interface accuracy distinguishes forces even when their topological predictions agree, while external surface tension sets the energy scale separately.

</details>


### [25] [Property-Preserving Data Compression for Incompressible Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02776)
*Andrey Pluzhnik,Ramansh Sharma,Martin Burtscher,Varun Shankar*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A method for lossy compression of divergence-free velocity fields that analytically preserves zero divergence and periodicity, using property-preserving matrix-valued kernel fits whose coefficients are compressed lossily.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard lossy compression of incompressible flow velocity fields can break the divergence-free constraint and periodicity. The goal is a compression scheme where every decompressed field automatically satisfies these physical constraints, regardless of compression tolerance.

Method: Nodal velocities are fit with property-preserving matrix-valued kernels, and their coefficients are compressed using standard lossy compressors. Several fitting schemes are proposed based on low-rank or sparse interpolation matrices: global Matérn interpolation, two-level Nyström approximation, Nyström–Wendland residual interpolation, multilevel Wendland interpolation, and a property-preserving (redundant) kernel frame.

Result: Nodal error bounds relate compressor tolerance to additional velocity error. Experiments with four compressors on cavity, Taylor–Green, and species-transport flows verify the bounds and compare storage at matched accuracy. Low-rank methods are compact when accurate enough; residual, multilevel, and frame interpolation extend attainable accuracy otherwise. Changes in turbulent velocity statistics are also measured.

Conclusion: The representations support an accuracy–compression tradeoff while preserving incompressibility and periodicity analytically.

Abstract: We develop a method for lossy compression of incompressible (i.e., divergence-free) velocity fields that preserves zero divergence and, on periodic domains, periodicity. We fit the nodal velocities with property-preserving matrix-valued kernels and compress their coefficients using standard lossy compressors. Every decompressed coefficient vector defines a field with the same constraints, independently of the compression tolerance. We present several fitting schemes designed around either low-rank representation of or sparsity in the interpolation matrix. More specifically, we show results for global Matérn interpolation, a two-level Nyström approximation, Nyström--Wendland residual interpolation, multilevel Wendland interpolation and a property-preserving (redundant) kernel frame. We derive nodal error bounds that relate the compressor tolerance to the additional velocity error. Experiments with four compressors on cavity, Taylor--Green, and species-transport flows verify the bounds under their coefficient-error assumptions and compare storage at matched velocity accuracy. The low-rank methods give compact representations when they resolve the velocity field sufficiently accurately; residual, multilevel, and frame interpolation extend the attainable accuracy when they do not. We also measure changes in turbulent velocity statistics. The resulting representations support an accuracy--compression tradeoff while preserving incompressibility and periodicity analytically.

</details>


### [26] [Efficient and Accurate Lift-and-Decoupling Generalized Transferable Neural Networks for Elliptic and Moving Interface Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02891)
*Tao Cheng,Junqing Jia,Lili Ju,Xiaoping Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces LD-GTransNet, an efficient and accurate neural network method for solving elliptic and moving interface problems using a lift-and-decoupling strategy combined with multi-layer GTransNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical solution of elliptic and moving interface problems, especially with high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries, is challenging. Existing neural network and traditional methods often require explicit domain decomposition, suffer from redundancy in lift approaches, or need costly nonlinear training.

Method: LD-GTransNet combines multi-layer GTransNet with a novel lift-and-decoupling strategy: hidden-layer neuron parameters are preset, and physical coordinates plus an added auxiliary variable are processed by separate subnetworks. This yields a unified global approximation of piecewise smooth solutions without explicit domain decomposition and reduces to a linear least-squares problem, eliminating nonlinear training.

Result: Extensive numerical experiments on 2D and 3D benchmark interface problems show LD-GTransNet consistently outperforms existing neural network-based and traditional numerical methods, particularly for high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries.

Conclusion: LD-GTransNet provides a highly efficient and accurate approach for elliptic and moving interface problems, achieving superior performance by avoiding explicit domain decomposition and nonlinear training while maintaining network expressivity.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a highly efficient and accurate method, called ``lift-and-decoupling generalized transferable neural network" (LD-GTransNet), for numerical solution of elliptic and moving interface problems. Our method makes use of the multi-layer GTransNet approach and incorporates a novel {lift-and-decoupling} strategy, in which the hidden-layer neuron parameters are preset and the physical coordinates and the added auxiliary variable are processed by separate subnetworks. Such design enables a unified global approximation of piecewise smooth solutions without explicit domain decomposition, while preserving network expressivity and avoiding the redundancy inherent in conventional {lift} approaches. The resulting formulation reduces to a linear least-squares problem, thereby eliminating the need of nonlinear training and ensuring the high computational efficiency and accuracy. Extensive numerical experiments on benchmark interface problems in two and three dimensions demonstrate that, compared with existing neural network-based and traditional numerical methods, our proposed LD-GTransNet consistently delivers superior performance, particularly for problems with high-contrast coefficients and complex interface geometries.

</details>


### [27] [From Continuous to Fully Discrete Carleman Estimates: A Transfer Principle for Parabolic Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02983)
*Qi Lü,Yu Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves an abstract transfer theorem that derives discrete parabolic Carleman estimates for fully discrete backward Euler schemes from a continuous Carleman inequality, then applies it to finite element and finite difference discretizations of the heat equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct discrete Carleman estimates typically rely on ad hoc weighted identities tied to the specific discrete operator, making the analysis PDE- and discretization-specific and hard to reuse. The authors aim to separate the continuous PDE Carleman analysis from the numerical verification needed for each discretization.

Method: An abstract transfer theorem for fully discrete backward Euler schemes with conforming spatial reconstructions. Given a continuous Carleman inequality plus weighted approximation and compatibility properties, and for time-independent positive self-adjoint operators, each discrete state is assigned a shifted continuous auxiliary problem whose discrete solution equals the prescribed state; weighted space-time error estimates uniform over the full discrete load space transfer the inequality while preserving residual and observation terms. Hypotheses are verified for consistent-mass P1 finite elements on locally graded meshes (2D/3D) and standard Cartesian finite differences in any fixed dimension.

Result: For the heat equation the transferred estimate holds for Carleman parameters up to the scale min{h^{-4/5}, (δt)^{-2/5}}. For bounded real-valued space-time potentials, the estimates yield relaxed observability and approximate null controls with uniformly bounded cost and exponentially small terminal errors under independent spatial and temporal refinement; weak accumulation points of the reconstructed controls are continuous null controls.

Conclusion: The transfer approach cleanly decouples PDE-specific Carleman analysis from discretization-specific verification, yielding robust discrete Carleman estimates and control results for heat-equation discretizations, with parameter ranges and convergence guarantees made explicit.

Abstract: Direct approaches to discrete parabolic Carleman estimates often rely on weighted identities tailored to the discrete operator. We prove an abstract transfer theorem that derives such estimates for fully discrete backward Euler schemes with conforming spatial reconstructions from a continuous Carleman inequality together with suitable weighted approximation and compatibility properties. For a class of time-independent positive self-adjoint operators, the key construction assigns to each discrete state a shifted continuous auxiliary problem whose discrete solution is exactly the prescribed state. Weighted space-time error estimates, uniform over the full discrete load space, then transfer the inequality while preserving the residual and observation terms of the original scheme. This separates the PDE-specific Carleman analysis from the numerical verification required for each discretization.
