We verify the hypotheses for the heat equation discretized by consistent-mass $P_1$ finite elements on locally graded meshes in two and three dimensions and by standard Cartesian finite differences in any fixed dimension. For the heat equation, the transferred estimate is valid for Carleman parameters up to the scale $\min\{h^{-4/5},(δt)^{-2/5}\}$. For bounded real-valued space-time potentials, the resulting estimates yield relaxed observability and approximate null controls with uniformly bounded cost and exponentially small terminal errors under independent spatial and temporal refinement; weak accumulation points of the reconstructed controls are continuous null controls.

</details>


### [28] [A Physics-Driven Multiscale Method for High-Frequency Helmholtz Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03144)
*Huangxin Chen,Jianhui Chen,Shubin Fu,Zhiyu Tan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A physics-driven multiscale method reduces the high-frequency Helmholtz equation via local wave responses and generalized eigenvalue problems, with a rigorous inf-sup stability and a priori error analysis linking local reduction to global accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-frequency Helmholtz problems are computationally expensive due to oscillatory solutions and large fine-grid dimensions. There is a need for model reduction that retains wave propagation physics while substantially lowering global dimension and cost.

Method: The method builds a reduced space from local wave responses solved on oversampled regions with boundary excitations, using a mixed finite element formulation with velocity elimination. Local generalized eigenvalue problems select modes, forming a reduced global system with sparse interelement coupling. Stability and convergence are analyzed by relating local spectral approximation to the global adjoint problem, yielding a multiscale inf-sup condition governed by fine-grid resolution, oversampling, and the first omitted eigenvalues.

Result: The analysis establishes well-posedness and quasi-optimality in a discrete energy norm, plus an a priori error bound quantifying roles of coarse/fine mesh sizes, oversampling depth, spectral truncation, and wavenumber. Numerical experiments in 2D and 3D show accurate wavefield approximation with substantial reductions in global dimension and solution cost for homogeneous and strongly heterogeneous media, including PML truncations.

Conclusion: The work provides a quantitative link between local space reduction and global stability/accuracy, validating the physics-driven multiscale approach as an effective and theoretically grounded reduction for high-frequency Helmholtz problems.

Abstract: We develop and analyze a physics-driven multiscale method for model reduction of the Helmholtz equation at high frequency. The method constructs a reduced approximation space from local wave responses within a mixed finite element formulation with velocity elimination. These responses are computed by solving Helmholtz problems with boundary excitations on oversampled regions, giving snapshots that incorporate the oscillatory behavior and propagation characteristics of the underlying medium. Local generalized eigenvalue problems then select modes from this physically informed space to form a reduced global system with sparse interelement coupling. To establish stability and convergence of this reduction, we relate the local spectral approximation to approximation of the global adjoint problem. Starting from an inf--sup estimate for the fine-grid formulation, this argument yields a multiscale inf--sup condition governed by fine-grid resolution, oversampling, and the first omitted local eigenvalues. The resulting stability establishes well-posedness and quasi-optimality of the multiscale approximation in a discrete energy norm. Together with local spectral estimates, it gives an a priori error bound that explicitly quantifies the roles of the coarse and fine mesh sizes, oversampling depth, spectral truncation, and wavenumber. This analysis provides a quantitative link between local space reduction and the stability and accuracy of the global approximation. Numerical experiments in two and three dimensions demonstrate accurate wavefield approximation with substantial reductions in global dimension and solution cost for homogeneous and strongly heterogeneous media, including perfectly matched layer truncations.

</details>


### [29] [Recursive Multilevel Decomposition of Pairwise Comparison Matrices: Algorithms and Inconsistency Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03184)
*Mert Çarboğa,Waldemar W. Koczkodaj,Yusuf Yaylı*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops a top-down layer decomposition of residual matrices in positive reciprocal pairwise comparison matrices via entrywise logarithmic coordinates, giving a structured residual-energy profile computable in O(n^3) or O(n^2).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positive reciprocal pairwise comparison (PC) matrices are central to decision analysis, but existing tools like geometric-mean priorities and the Koczkodaj inconsistency indicator give limited structural insight into residual inconsistency. A decomposition that exposes layer-wise residual structure without restrictive assumptions is desirable.

Method: Using entrywise logarithmic coordinates and a consistent-residual split, the authors show that for any fixed ordering the residual matrix admits a unique top-down decomposition into mutually Frobenius-orthogonal layers. They provide an explicit active-matrix recursion (O(n^3)) and an equivalent cumulative parameter-extraction procedure (O(n^2)).

Result: The construction applies to every positive reciprocal PC matrix with n>=3, requiring no spectral, sparsity, or genericity assumptions. Squared Frobenius norms of layers form an ordering-conditioned residual-energy profile, complementary to geometric-mean priorities and Kii. Entrywise exponentiation yields exact Hadamard-product reconstruction. Experiments confirm procedure agreement, exact reconstruction, orthogonality, and machine-precision accuracy.

Conclusion: The layer decomposition offers a principled, assumption-free structural diagnostic for PC matrices, with efficient computation and exact reconstruction, complementing standard inconsistency measures.

Abstract: Positive reciprocal pairwise comparison matrices are studied in entrywise logarithmic coordinates. Starting from the established consistent-residual split, we show that, for any fixed ordering, the residual matrix admits a unique top-down decomposition into mutually Frobenius-orthogonal layers. The construction applies to every positive reciprocal PC matrix of size $n \geq 3$, without additional spectral, sparsity, or genericity assumptions. An explicit active-matrix recursion and an equivalent cumulative parameter-extraction procedure have computational complexities $O(n^3)$ and $O(n^2)$, respectively. The squared Frobenius norms of the layers define an ordering-conditioned residual-energy profile that provides structural information complementary to the geometric-mean priorities and the Koczkodaj inconsistency indicator (Kii). Entrywise exponentiation yields an exact Hadamard-product reconstruction of the original reciprocal matrix. Numerical experiments confirm agreement between the two computational procedures, exact reconstruction, mutual orthogonality, and numerical accuracy to machine precision.

</details>


### [30] [Reversible Stratonovich perturbations for accelerating convergence to equilibrium of Langevin dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03191)
*Guillaume Louis,Grigorios A. Pavliotis,Gilles Vilmart*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies sampling from probability distributions via Langevin dynamics, adding a reversibility-preserving Stratonovich perturbation, proving optimal scaling for Gaussian targets and validating efficiency numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard (overdamped) Langevin dynamics can be slow to converge, and the authors seek to improve sampling efficiency while preserving reversibility, investigating how an added Stratonovich perturbation can be optimally scaled.

Method: The authors consider overdamped Langevin dynamics perturbed by a reversibility-preserving Stratonovich perturbation. They prove an optimal scaling of this perturbation for Gaussian target distributions and derive an algorithm to construct such a perturbation.

Result: An optimal scaling is proven for the Stratonovich perturbation for Gaussian targets, and an algorithm to construct it is derived. Numerical experiments compare the method against nonreversible samplers and overdamped Langevin dynamics, showing improved efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed reversibility-preserving Stratonovich perturbation yields efficient sampling, demonstrated numerically to work well beyond the quadratic (Gaussian) case.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the problem of sampling from a probability distribution using Langevin-type dynamics. We consider overdamped Langevin dynamics perturbed by adding a Stratonovich perturbation that preserves reversibility. We prove an optimal scaling for the Stratonovich perturbation applied to Gaussian target distributions and derive an algorithm to construct such perturbation. Our theoretical results are supplemented by numerical experiments in which we compare our proposed method with nonreversible samplers and overdamped Langevin dynamics. Our numerical experiments demonstrate the efficiency of the approach beyond the quadratic case.

</details>


### [31] [Simultaneous Global Convergence Failure of Newton's and Halley's Methods in Degree Four](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03246)
*Alessio Basti,Tommaso Cremaschi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The authors construct an explicit quartic polynomial on which both Newton's and Halley's methods fail to converge on nonempty open sets, via a computational search plus a two-interval contraction proof.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Newton's and Halley's methods are classical root-finders, but they can fail to converge to a root for some initial values. A simultaneous failure on nonempty open sets was known for other degrees, but the degree-four case was an open gap, which this paper fills.

Method: They search a one-parameter family of quartics designed to have a prescribed Newton two-cycle, reproducibly finding an example. They then prove analytically that Newton's method has a superattracting two-cycle and Halley's method has a distinct attracting extraneous two-cycle, using a two-interval contraction argument.

Result: An explicit quartic is found where Newton's and Halley's methods both fail to converge to a root on nonempty open sets of initial values. Numerical evidence also identifies a second quartic showing simultaneous failure of Newton's, Halley's, and Schröder's methods.

Conclusion: The work closes the degree-four gap in the simultaneous Newton–Halley failure problem and suggests that simultaneous failure among several root-finding methods may occur at higher (quartic) degrees as well.

Abstract: We construct an explicit quartic polynomial for which Newton's and Halley's root-finding methods both fail to converge to a root on nonempty open sets of initial values. The example can be found through a reproducible computational search within a one-parameter family of quartics designed to exhibit a prescribed Newton two-cycle. We then prove analytically that Newton's method has a superattracting two-cycle and that Halley's method has a distinct attracting extraneous two-cycle, using a two-interval contraction argument. This fills a degree-four gap in the simultaneous Newton--Halley failure problem. The computational approach also identifies another quartic with numerical evidence of simultaneous failure of Newton's, Halley's, and Schröder's methods.

</details>


### [32] [On the Choice of Continuous and Discontinuous Lagrange Multipliers in Fictitious-Domain Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03267)
*Najwa Alshehri,Shumoua F. Alrzqi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper compares two FD-DLM discretizations for elliptic interface problems, contrasting continuous vs. discontinuous multiplier spaces, and finds trade-offs between local errors, mismatch, and primal accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handling elliptic interface problems with separate meshes without requiring interface-mesh conformity is challenging, and the choice of continuous vs. discontinuous multiplier spaces in low-order FD-DLM discretizations is unclear.

Method: Two stable, low-order FD-DLM discretizations are compared using continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces. The analysis distinguishes between satisfying the weak coupling constraint per element and the discrepancy (mismatch) between the two discrete primal fields.

Result: Numerical experiments show the continuous formulation usually yields smaller local errors and maximum mismatch values, while the discontinuous formulation shows a more regular mismatch decrease under mesh refinement and gives smaller primal errors.

Conclusion: The continuous and discontinuous multiplier formulations offer different advantages, so the choice depends on whether lower local/mismatch errors or better primal accuracy and convergence regularity is prioritized.

Abstract: This article examines the Fictitious-Domain approach using Distributed Lagrange Multipliers (FD-DLM), a method that handles elliptic interface problems with separate meshes without requiring the interface to match the background mesh. It compares two stable, low-order FD-DLM discretizations when using both continuous and discontinuous multiplier spaces. The study distinguishes between satisfying the weak coupling constraint per element and the size of the discrepancy between the two discrete primal fields. Numerical experiments indicate that the continuous formulation usually yields smaller local errors and maximum mismatch values, while the discontinuous formulation shows a more regular decrease in mismatch as the mesh is refined and has smaller primal errors.

</details>


### [33] [Fully mixed finite element methods for the coupling of viscoelasticity and reaction-diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03317)
*Alonso J. Bustos,Jeonghun J. Lee,Ricardo Ruiz-Baier*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A fully mixed finite element method for a two-way coupled mechanochemical model of calcium signalling in viscoelastic tissue, with global existence/uniqueness, optimal error estimates, and numerical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calcium signalling in viscoelastic tissue involves bidirectional mechanochemical coupling where calcium generates active stress and local dilation feeds back into calcium dynamics. A fully mixed finite element framework capturing this coupling with weak stress symmetry is needed for rigorous and accurate simulation.

Method: A fully mixed FEM is proposed: mechanical response via Kelvin-Voigt law with elastic/viscous stresses, velocity, and spin tensor as unknowns and weak total-stress symmetry; the reaction-diffusion system for calcium and inactivated IP3 is also in mixed form with diffusive flux as an extra unknown. The coupling is bidirectionally active stress and dilation feedback.

Result: Global existence and uniqueness are established by reducing to an integro-differential problem. The semidiscrete scheme is well posed under a single inf-sup condition, with a commuting interpolation operator yielding optimal a priori error estimates. Explicit convergence rates are derived for three families of weakly symmetric elements, with superconvergent local post-processing for one. 2D/3D tests confirm rates.

Conclusion: The method provides a rigorous, accurate mixed finite element treatment of coupled mechanochemical calcium signalling, validated numerically through viscoelastic benchmarks and mechanically driven calcium wavetrains.

Abstract: We propose and analyse a fully mixed finite element method for a two-way coupled mechanochemical model of calcium signalling in viscoelastic tissue. The mechanical response follows a Kelvin--Voigt law, with the elastic and viscous stresses, velocity, and spin tensor as unknowns, and the symmetry of the total stress imposed weakly. The reaction-diffusion system for calcium and inactivated inositol triphosphate is also formulated in mixed form, introducing the diffusive flux as an additional unknown. The coupling is bidirectional: calcium generates an active stress, while local dilation feeds back into the calcium dynamics. We establish global existence and uniqueness by reducing the coupled system to an integro-differential problem and subsequently recovering the mixed variables. The semidiscrete scheme is formulated for general conforming finite element spaces and shown to be well posed under a single inf-sup condition. An interpolation operator that commutes with the divergence and preserves the weak symmetry pairing yields optimal a priori error estimates. We specialise the analysis to three families of weakly symmetric finite elements, deriving explicit convergence rates and, for one family, a superconvergent local post-processing of the displacement. Numerical tests in two and three dimensions confirm the predicted convergence rates and illustrate the method through viscoelastic benchmarks and mechanically driven calcium wavetrains.

</details>


### [34] [Energetic Variational Deep Learning for Solving High-Dimensional Stationary Fokker-Planck Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03325)
*Fanze Kong,Zhouyuan Qin,Shuangquan Xie*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A deep learning framework solves high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations via Monte Carlo weak supervision, energy-variational physics constraints, and quasi-Newton refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations is challenging due to the curse of dimensionality and the difficulty of enforcing equilibrium physics accurately.

Method: Combines Monte Carlo weak supervision to anchor the density scale, a free-energy variational loss to enforce equilibrium physics, and quasi-Newton refinement. Extends to systems with pointwise orthogonal rotational components and, via a family of test functions, to more general non-gradient drift.

Result: Accurate and robust density approximations across various high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck problems, including rough-potential and noisy-data settings.

Conclusion: The proposed physics-informed deep learning approach effectively handles high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations and generalizes to non-gradient drift systems.

Abstract: This paper develops a deep learning framework for solving high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck equations by combining Monte Carlo weak supervision, energy-variational physics constraints, and quasi-Newton refinement. Monte Carlo statistics anchor the scale of the stationary density, while a free-energy variational loss enforces equilibrium physics; the formulation also applies to stationary systems with pointwise orthogonal rotational components and, using a family of test functions, to systems with more general non-gradient drift. Numerical results demonstrate accurate and robust density approximations across a range of high-dimensional stationary Fokker-Planck problems, including rough-potential and noisy-data settings.

</details>


### [35] [Learning the Willmore flow with a lightweight neural operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03534)
*Elie Bretin,Roland Denis,Tokuhiro Eto,Simon Masnou*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends a phase-field neural operator approach from mean curvature motion to the Willmore flow, using a lightweight single-step architecture trained on hybrid minimizing-movement trajectories, with emphasis on radial kernels and 2D-to-3D transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (Bretin et al. 2022) applied a phase-field neural operator to mean curvature motion, but Willmore flow (a higher-order, more complex geometric evolution) was not addressed. This paper aims to fill that gap while keeping the model lightweight and efficient.

Method: The authors extend the phase-field neural operator framework to Willmore flow, motivated by a Bence-Merriman-Osher-type expansion of the Allen-Cahn semigroup. The single-time-step architecture is trained on trajectories generated by a hybrid minimizing movement scheme. For non-oriented interfaces they compare unconstrained versus radial convolution kernels, and construct 3D kernels from trained 2D kernels via their radial Fourier profiles for effective 3D initialization.

Result: Unconstrained convolution kernels produced anisotropic dynamics for non-oriented interfaces, while radial kernels improved the evolution process. Transferring the radial Fourier profiles of 2D kernels provided an effective initialization for the non-oriented 3D model, enabling 3D reconstruction without additional 3D training.

Conclusion: The proposed approach yields a lightweight neural operator for Willmore flow, demonstrates the advantage of radial kernels, and enables curve/surface reconstruction from unoriented point clouds, including 3D transfer from 2D-trained models.

Abstract: We extend to the Willmore flow the phase-field neural operator approach proposed by Bretin, Denis, Masnou, and Terii (2022) for mean curvature motion in two and three dimensions. The resulting lightweight, single-time-step architecture is motivated by a Bence-Merriman-Osher-type expansion of the Allen-Cahn semigroup, and is trained using trajectories generated by a hybrid minimizing movement scheme. In our experiments with non-oriented interfaces, unconstrained convolution kernels produce anisotropic dynamics, whereas radial kernels enhance the evolution process. We also construct three-dimensional kernels from trained two-dimensional kernels via their radial Fourier profiles, which provides an effective initialization for the non-oriented 3D model. Finally, we show applications to curve and surface reconstruction from unoriented point clouds, including reconstruction using the transferred model without the need for additional 3D training.

</details>


### [36] [Error Estimates in Physics-Informed Neural Networks to Heat-Wave Fluid-Structure Interactive System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03575)
*Pelin G. Geredeli,Mohammad Mahabubur Rahman,Deepanshu Verma*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: First error and generalization estimates for a physics-informed neural network (PINN) approximating a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system, the heat-wave transmission problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupled fluid-elastic systems (parabolic + hyperbolic PDEs) are prototype models for real-world Stokes-elastic problems, but existing PINN error analyses only handle single PDEs, making the coupled error analysis more delicate.

Method: A physics-informed neural network approximates the coupled heat-wave system with transmission conditions on a fixed interface. The error between exact and PINN solutions is bounded by the PINN residuals, and generalization error is controlled by training error, number of quadrature points, and network size.

Result: Error estimates are proven, showing the PINN error is bounded by residuals and the generalization error depends on training error, quadrature points, and network size; numerical experiments validate the theory.

Conclusion: These are the first error estimates for a PINN approximation of a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system, supported by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We study a physics-informed neural network (PINN) approximation of a coupled PDE system that models the interaction between fluid and elastic dynamics. The canonical heat-wave model considered here couples a parabolic and a hyperbolic equation through transmission conditions on a fixed boundary interface, and serves as a prototype for the Stokes-elastic systems that arises in real world applications. This coupling makes the error analysis more delicate than that for a single PDE. We bound the error between the exact and PINN solutions by the associated PINN residuals, and we moreover prove that the generalization error is controlled by the training error, the number of quadrature points, and the size of the network. {To the best of our knowledge}, these are the first error estimates for a PINN approximation of a coupled fluid-structure interaction PDE system. We also show that numerical experiments with respect to a given test problem.

</details>


### [37] [A Two-level Filtered Subspace Iterative Method for Generalized Hermitian Interior Eigenvalue Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03804)
*Qi Li,Shi Shu,Xiaofei Xu,Juan Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a two-level filtered subspace iteration (TLFSI) method to compute all eigenpairs with eigenvalues in a target interval [a,b] from finite element discretizations of self-adjoint PDE eigenvalue problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing all eigenvalues within a specified interval for large-scale PDE eigenvalue problems is computationally challenging; a scalable method exploiting coarse-space information is needed.

Method: Combines a two-level approach with modern filtered subspace iteration, using contour integral filtering as an example. A coarse space is constructed based on the spectral location of eigenvalues of interest to approximate the target invariant subspace.

Result: The distance between the approximate and target subspaces depends only on the spectral separation between the region of interest and the remaining spectrum, plus the coarse mesh size H. Numerical experiments on benchmark elasticity problems confirm the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: TLFSI provides an efficient, theoretically grounded method for interval eigenvalue problems, with accuracy governed by spectral separation and coarse mesh size.

Abstract: We propose a two-level filtered subspace iterative (TLFSI) method for computing all eigenpairs whose eigenvalues in the interval of interest $[a,b]$ after finite element discretization of self-adjoint PDE eigenvalue problems. The method combines a two-level approach with a modern filtered subspace iteration framework. In this paper, we use contour integral filtering as an example. The two-level method constructs a coarse space according to the spectral location of the eigenvalues of interest and obtains from it an approximation to the target invariant subspace. We prove that the distance between the approximate and target subspaces depends only on the separation between the spectral region of interest and the remainder of the spectrum, together with the coarse mesh size \(H\). Numerical experiments on benchmark elasticity problems confirm the theoretical predictions.

</details>


### [38] [On the involution preserving properties of semi-discrete Active Flux methods on simplex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04349)
*Remi Abgrall,Yongle Liu,Mario Ricchiuto*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The Active Flux method on unstructured triangular meshes is shown to preserve discrete involutions (curl for linear acoustics, divergence for 2D Maxwell), with explicit discrete operators derived for general simplex meshes and any polynomial degree.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conservative formulations and involutions (curl/divergence constraints) must both hold, but it is unclear how a single discretization can satisfy the conservative form and the involution simultaneously.

Method: Within a discrete Active Flux framework on simplices, explicit formulas are derived for a discrete curl operator exactly preserved for the acoustic system and a discrete divergence operator preserved for the Maxwell system.

Result: The analysis applies to general simplex meshes and any polynomial degree, including extensions beyond third order; third-order numerical experiments confirm the theoretical results.

Conclusion: Active Flux preserves discrete involutions on unstructured triangular meshes, providing structure-preserving discretizations for acoustics and Maxwell equations.

Abstract: We study the involution-preserving properties of the Active Flux method on unstructured triangular meshes. Two model problems are considered: the two-dimensional linear acoustic system, which satisfies a curl involution on the velocity field, and the two-dimensional Maxwell equations with an in-plane magnetic field, which satisfy a divergence involution. Both systems can be written in conservative form. The question we address is how a single discretization can satisfy the conservative form and the involution at the same time. Within a discrete Active Flux framework on simplices, we derive explicit formulas for a discrete curl operator that is exactly preserved by the scheme for acoustics, and for a discrete divergence operator preserved for the Maxwell system. The analysis applies to general simplex meshes and to any polynomial degree, including extensions of the method beyond third order. Numerical experiments with the third-order scheme confirm the theoretical results.
  Highlights: 1-Active Flux on unstructured triangular meshes preserves discrete involutions, 2-Explicit discrete curl operator preserved by the scheme for linear acoustics, 3- Explicit discrete divergence operator preserved for 2D Maxwell equations, 4- Results hold on general simplex meshes,% and for any polynomial degree 5- Third-order numerical experiments confirm the theoretical results. \end{itemize}

</details>


### [39] [Inference of Hamiltonian and Lie-Poisson structures from vector field samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04350)
*Jason E. Frank,Georg A. Gottwald*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper presents a data-driven method to jointly infer Hamiltonians and Poisson structure matrices (including Lie-Poisson and affine Poisson) from vector-field samples, using random feature maps, orthogonal matching pursuit, eigenvalue problems, and constrained least-squares with Jacobi/cocycle enforcement, validated via splitting integrators and Poincaré sections on Lorenz-86 and Kirchhoff systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Hamiltonian/Lie-Poisson system inference methods typically assume the Poisson structure is known, limiting applicability to systems where the underlying geometric structure must also be discovered from data.

Method: Hamiltonian represented as a linear combination of random feature maps (fixed random internal weights), making estimation a linear regression. Three cases: known Poisson tensor; unknown constant invertible (symplectic) structure matrix, solved via an eigenvalue problem avoiding the trivial zero solution; and Lie-Poisson/affine Poisson (structure matrix linear in state), solved by alternating between a generalized eigenvalue problem for an initial Hamiltonian estimate and a constrained nonlinear least-squares fit of structure constants enforcing the Jacobi identity and cocycle condition via penalty continuation. Orthogonal matching pursuit selects compact, well-conditioned random features, and the resulting model admits an explicit Poisson/symplectic splitting integrator.

Result: On four- and five-dimensional Lorenz-86 and a six-dimensional Kirchhoff rigid-body system, the method recovers Hamiltonians, structure constants, and Casimir invariants to high accuracy, with inferred Poincaré sections converging to correct invariant-tori topology as the number of random features increases.

Conclusion: Joint inference of Hamiltonian and Poisson structure is feasible and accurate across increasing levels of structural generality, with the splitting integrator and long-time Poincaré sections providing a robust validation mechanism.

Abstract: We present a method for the data-driven inference of Hamiltonian and Lie-Poisson dynamical systems from vector-field samples, in which the Hamiltonian and the underlying Poisson tensor are estimated jointly rather than assuming the structure is known. The Hamiltonian is represented as a linear combination of random feature maps with fixed random internal weights, so its estimation is a linear regression problem for any given Poisson structure. We treat three cases of increasing generality: a known Poisson tensor, an unknown constant invertible (symplectic) structure matrix, and Lie-Poisson and affine Poisson structures, with structure matrix linear in the state. In the constant case, the Hamiltonian and structure matrix are recovered jointly from an eigenvalue problem that avoids the trivial zero solution due to the scaling symmetry of Hamiltonian systems. For Lie-Poisson and affine Poisson systems we alternate between an initial Hamiltonian estimate from a generalized eigenvalue problem for an approximate first integral, and a constrained nonlinear least-squares fit of the structure constants that enforces the Jacobi identity and, for affine structures, the cocycle condition, via continuation in a penalty parameter. Orthogonal matching pursuit selects a compact, well-conditioned subset of random features. Because the learned Hamiltonian is a sum of single-feature terms, the model admits an explicit Poisson/symplectic splitting integrator, which we use to validate the learned systems via long-time Poincare sections. We demonstrate the approach on the four- and five-dimensional Lorenz-86 model and a six-dimensional Kirchhoff rigid-body system, recovering Hamiltonians, structure constants and Casimir invariants to high accuracy, and showing the inferred Poincare sections converge to the correct invariant-tori topology as the number of random features increases.

</details>


### [40] [Operator splitting methods for non-autonomous evolution equations comprising three parts, with application to the Bloch-Torrey equation of MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04355)
*Barbara Kaltenbacher,Pablo Muñoz,Mechthild Thalhammer*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Lie-Trotter splitting schemes for non-autonomous evolution equations are devised and analyzed, with two approaches, and applied to the Bloch-Torrey equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To exploit explicit solutions of subproblems and to handle non-autonomous operators efficiently via freezing operators per time interval, motivated by first order convergence of Lie-Trotter integrators.

Method: Two schemes: (1) a fully non-autonomous Lie-Trotter splitting, and (2) a Magnus-integrator-based splitting that freezes operators at the start of each time interval; first order convergence is proven in a general setting, then applied to the Bloch-Torrey equation decomposed into three physically motivated subproblems, with numerical tests using finite element space discretization.

Result: First order convergence is established in a general setting; numerical tests on the Bloch-Torrey equation confirm the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The proposed Lie-Trotter splitting schemes are effective and theoretically justified for non-autonomous evolution equations, with demonstrated applicability to the Bloch-Torrey equation of MRI.

Abstract: In this paper we devise and analyze Lie Trotter splitting schemes for non-autonomous evolution equations, following two approaches: 1) A fully non-autonomous scheme, motivated by the potential use of explicit solutions of some subproblems. 2) A splitting scheme relying on the concept of Magnus integrators, where we freeze the operators at the beginning of each time interval as this suffices for first order convergence of the Lie-Trotter integrator. We derive first order convergence in a general setting and apply the analysis to the Bloch-Torrey equation of magnetic resonance imaging, where a decomposition into three subproblems is well-motivated by the physical role of the underlying operators. Numerical tests, complementing the time stepping scheme by a finite element space discretization, illustrate the theoretical findings.

</details>


### [41] [Conditioning and Singular Value Distribution of Jacobi Weighted Histopolation Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04383)
*Allal Guessab,Federico Nudo,Stefano Serra-Capizzano*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper identifies the singular value symbol of unscaled Jacobi weighted histopolation matrices and uses it to characterize their singular value distribution, vanishing set, and conditioning (ill-posedness vs well-posedness) for any α,β > -1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed growth slower than any positive power of log of matrix size for α,β > 1/2 and quasi-uniform meshes, but numerical behavior suggested a nontrivial singular value symbol that was not yet identified. This paper aims to explicitly determine that symbol and the resulting distribution for general parameters.

Method: Starting from the standard asymptotic formula for Jacobi polynomials, the authors derive an asymptotic representation of the weighted cell averages defining the histopolation matrix entries. They then analyze the associated Gram sequence using GLT (Generalized Locally Toeplitz) theory to identify its GLT symbol.

Result: The GLT symbol and singular value distribution of the histopolation matrices are obtained for any α,β > -1. The vanishing set Z of the symbol is characterized and its measure computed; Z depends only on the mesh while Jacobi parameters affect symbol values on the support. Positive measure of Z implies discrete asymptotic ill-posedness and suggests exponential growth of the spectral condition number; zero measure implies well-posedness with algebraic conditioning.

Conclusion: Numerical experiments support the theoretical findings. Conclusions, along with several open problems, are presented in the final section.

Abstract: In this paper, we first study the singular value distribution of the unscaled matrices arising from Jacobi weighted histopolation. For the Jacobi weight with parameters $(α,β)$, and using the Jacobi basis with parameters $(α+1,β+1)$, we previously proved that, for $α,β>1/2$ and quasi-uniform meshes, the growth of these matrices in the sense of singular value distribution is slower than any positive power of the logarithm of the matrix size. However, the numerical behavior of the unscaled matrices suggested a nontrivial singular value symbol, which we identify explicitly in this paper. Starting from the standard asymptotic formula for Jacobi polynomials, we derive an asymptotic representation of the weighted cell averages defining the entries of the histopolation matrices. This representation allows us to analyze the associated Gram sequence by means of GLT theory. We identify its GLT symbol and, consequently, we obtain the singular value distribution of the histopolation matrices for any $α,β>-1$. We also characterize the set $\mathcal Z$ where the resulting symbol vanishes and compute its measure, showing that this set depends only on the mesh, while the Jacobi parameters affect the values of the symbol on its support. When this measure is positive, the singular value distribution implies the discrete asymptotic ill-posedness of the problem, while a comparison with the Toeplitz setting suggests exponential growth of the spectral condition number. When the measure of $\mathcal Z$ is zero, the problem is well-posed and the observed conditioning is algebraic in the matrix size. Finally, numerical experiments supporting the theoretical findings are reported and discussed, while conclusions are given in the final section of the current work together with a few open problems.

</details>


### [42] [Arbitrarily High-Order Structure-Preserving Parametric Finite Element Methods for Geometric PDEs via Local Pullback Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04389)
*Weizhu Bao,Yifei Li,Dongmin Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops arbitrarily high-order structure-preserving parametric finite element methods for geometric PDEs (surface diffusion, volume-preserving mean curvature flow) using local pullback flow maps and two formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric PDEs like surface diffusion and volume-preserving mean curvature flow require numerical methods that both achieve high-order accuracy and preserve geometric structures (area/volume preservation and perimeter/surface area dissipation), which is challenging.

Method: Reformulate the geometric PDEs on an arbitrary intermediate hypersurface via a local pullback flow map. Using admissible local pullbacks, derive two formulations (conformal and equidistribution) from pullback identities for the Laplace-Beltrami operator. Discretize both with arbitrary-degree isoparametric finite elements and Runge-Kutta collocation methods with positive weights.

Result: For the conformal formulation, weighted averages of the normal-Jacobian vector ensure exact area/volume preservation, and algebraic stability of the Runge-Kutta method guarantees perimeter/surface area dissipation. For the equidistribution formulation, a path-averaged normal-Jacobian vector and a geometric discrete gradient guarantee both exact preservation and dissipation without requiring algebraic stability. Numerical experiments confirm expected high-order accuracy and structure-preserving properties.

Conclusion: The proposed arbitrarily high-order parametric finite element methods successfully combine high-order accuracy with structure-preserving properties for geometric PDEs, as confirmed by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We develop arbitrarily high-order structure-preserving parametric finite element methods for geometric PDEs including surface diffusion and volume-preserving mean curvature flow based on local pullback flow maps. The central idea is to reformulate the geometric PDEs on an arbitrary intermediate hypersurface and represent the subsequent evolution through a local pullback flow map. Using admissible local pullbacks, we derive two formulations from the corresponding pullback identities for the Laplace-Beltrami operator, referred to as the conformal and equidistribution formulations. We discretize both formulations using arbitrary-degree isoparametric finite elements and Runge-Kutta collocation methods with positive weights. For the conformal formulation, weighted averages of the normal-Jacobian vector ensure exact preservation of the enclosed area or volume, while algebraic stability of the Runge-Kutta method additionally guarantees dissipation of the perimeter or surface area. For the equidistribution formulation, a path-averaged normal-Jacobian vector and a geometric discrete gradient guarantee both exact preservation of the enclosed area or volume and dissipation of the perimeter or surface area, without requiring algebraic stability. Numerical experiments confirm the expected high-order accuracy and structure-preserving properties.

</details>


### [43] [A Locally Conservative Enriched Linearized Neural Network Approximation to Elliptic PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04524)
*Seungil Kim,Gwanghyun Jo,Young Ju Lee*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces an Enriched Linearized Neural Network (ENN) method for Darcy flow that guarantees local mass conservation by combining a linearized shallow ReLU^k network with piecewise constant enrichments, using a NIPG formulation with a GLS term to overcome the missing inverse inequality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network methods for PDEs typically lack the inverse inequality needed for standard discontinuous Galerkin stability/error analysis, and they generally do not enforce local mass conservation of the flux.

Method: A linearized shallow ReLU^k network with hidden parameters fixed on a quasi-uniform sphere set provides the approximation; piecewise constant functions on an auxiliary (possibly curved, general) subdivision ensure local conservation. A Galerkin least-squares term plus an edge identity compensate for the missing inverse inequality, and the nonsymmetric interior penalty (NIPG) form is used.

Result: An optimal-order EG-norm error estimate O(n^{-(r-1)/d}) for H^r solutions (r≥2) and O(n^{-(s-1)/d}) for H^s solutions (3/2<s≤2) is proven for any shape-regular subdivision, under conditions on the GLS parameter τ and edge scaling. A locally conservative numerical Darcy flux is constructed; numerics confirm theory.

Conclusion: The ENN method achieves optimal H^1-order accuracy and exact local mass conservation independent of element size/number; a fixed 4×4 subdivision matches the accuracy of a network-refined one, and mass loss stays at round-off.

Abstract: This paper presents a locally conservative Enriched Linearized Neural Network (ENN) method for the Darcy flow model. A linearized shallow ReLU$^k$ network, whose hidden-layer parameters are fixed on a quasi-uniform set of the sphere, carries the approximation, and piecewise constant functions on an auxiliary subdivision of the domain are added to ensure local mass conservation. The subdivision may consist of general, possibly curved, elements. Since neural network functions do not satisfy the inverse inequality, the stability and error analysis of the standard discontinuous Galerkin interior penalty formulation do not apply. The missing inverse inequality is remedied by an edge identity combined with a Galerkin least-squares (GLS) term, together with a single neural approximant that is optimal in the $L^2$, $H^1$ and $H^2$ norms simultaneously. We use the nonsymmetric interior penalty (NIPG) form, which is coercive for every positive penalty parameter. With $n$, the width of the shallow neural network, used in ENN, we prove an error estimate in the EG norm that is of the optimal $H^1$ order, i.e., $O(n^{-(r-1)/d})$ for solutions in $H^r$, $r\ge2$, and $O(n^{-(s-1)/d})$ for solutions in $H^s$, $\frac32<s\le2$, for any shape-regular subdivision, when the GLS parameter satisfies $\min\{h,n^{-1/d}\}^2\lesssimτ\lesssim n^{-2/d}$ and the edge terms are scaled with $\min\{h_e,n^{-1/d}\}$, i.e., by the resolution of the network rather than of the mesh. A numerical Darcy flux is constructed, which is locally conservative, regardless of the size or the number of the elements. Sample numerical results demonstrate the correctness of the theory. In particular, a fixed $4\times4$ subdivision gives the same accuracy as a subdivision refined with the network, and the mass loss stays at round-off unlike a non-conservative neural network method.

</details>


### [44] [Pressure Monotonicity for a Rotation-Based Biot Discretization with Circumcentric Mass Lumping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04535)
*Huipeng Gu,Mingchao Cai,Jingzhi Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves cellwise pressure monotonicity (a discrete maximum principle) for a mixed finite element discretization of the rotation-based Biot system using compatible discrete de Rham complexes and backward Euler time stepping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed finite element discretizations of poroelastic Biot systems can exhibit spurious cellwise pressure oscillations, and monotonicity of the pressure solution is desirable for physical fidelity but not guaranteed by standard schemes.

Method: Spatial discretization via compatible discrete de Rham complexes with lowest-order elements for rotation, displacement, Darcy flux, and pressure; backward Euler in time. Complex exactness reduces the mechanics contribution to a scaled pressure mass matrix (positive diagonal capacity); Darcy elimination with positive circumcentric facet weights gives a weighted cell graph Laplacian, with mass-lumped product uniformly equivalent to the standard Darcy product.

Result: The condensed pressure matrix is Stieltjes, so the pressure propagator is entrywise nonnegative, yielding a cellwise discrete maximum principle. Numerical experiments confirm predicted convergence rates and suppression of spurious cellwise pressure oscillations.

Conclusion: The proposed discretization provably enforces cellwise pressure monotonicity and avoids spurious oscillations, validated numerically.

Abstract: We establish cellwise pressure monotonicity for a mixed finite element discretization of the rotation-based Biot system. The spatial discretization is based on compatible discrete de Rham complexes, using standard lowest-order elements for rotation, displacement, Darcy flux, and pressure. For the temporal discretization, we employ the backward Euler method. Exactness of the discrete complexes reduces the mechanics contribution to the pressure Schur complement to a scaled pressure mass matrix, yielding a positive diagonal mechanics capacity. For the Darcy part, positive circumcentric facet weights yield a weighted cell graph Laplacian after flux elimination, while the mass-lumped product remains uniformly equivalent to the standard Darcy product. The diagonal mechanics capacity and the weighted cell graph Laplacian make the condensed pressure matrix Stieltjes. Consequently, the pressure propagator is entrywise nonnegative, which yields a cellwise discrete maximum principle. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rates and show that the proposed discretization suppresses spurious cellwise pressure oscillations.

</details>


### [45] [Quasi-Optimal Randomized Recovery of $\mathcal{H}^2$ Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04574)
*Anna Yesypenko*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces two randomized algorithms (carried and resketched recovery) for constructing explicit, strongly admissible H^2 matrix approximations using only matrix-vector products with the operator and its adjoint, on an adaptive geometric hierarchy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructing explicit H^2 approximations typically requires access to matrix entries or hierarchy-specific probing. The authors aim to build such approximations using only black-box products with the matrix and its adjoint, enabling use on implicitly defined operators.

Method: Carried recovery uses a single pair of dense Gaussian test matrices and updates forward-adjoint sketches across levels (all samples batched in one pass). Resketched recovery re-draws fresh Gaussian test matrices at each level, enabling a global quasi-optimal expected Frobenius error bound relative to the best approximation with the prescribed hierarchy, ranks, and near-neighbor pattern.

Result: Carried recovery needs O(k+p) operator applications (independent of tree depth); resketched needs O((k+p)log(N/k)) on logarithmic-depth trees (N = dimension, k = max box rank, p = oversampling). Experiments on Laplace, Dirichlet-to-Neumann, and Helmholtz operators in 2D/3D with up to 1.28M unknowns show carried recovery achieves nearly refinement-independent error and accuracy comparable to resketched recovery with far fewer operator applications; compressed application cost scales linearly for fixed rank.

Conclusion: Carried recovery offers a communication- and query-efficient alternative to resketched recovery, matching its accuracy under sufficient oversampling while substantially reducing operator applications.

Abstract: An $\mathcal H^2$ matrix represents well-separated interactions using nested low-rank bases while retaining nearby interactions explicitly. We consider the problem of constructing an explicit, strongly admissible $\mathcal H^2$ approximation using only products with the matrix and its adjoint, and develop two randomized recovery algorithms on an adaptive geometric hierarchy.
  Carried recovery begins with a single pair of dense Gaussian test matrices, without hierarchy-specific probing, and updates the resulting forward--adjoint sketches across levels. All operator samples can therefore be acquired in one batch. Resketched recovery applies the same recovery procedure using fresh Gaussian test matrices at each level. For this variant, we prove a global quasi-optimal bound on the expected Frobenius error relative to the best approximation with the prescribed hierarchy, rank bounds, and retained near-neighbor pattern.
  Carried recovery requires only $O(k+p)$ applications of the matrix and its adjoint, independent of tree depth, whereas resketched recovery requires $O((k+p)\log(N/k))$ on trees of logarithmic depth. Here $N$ is the matrix dimension, $k$ the maximum prescribed box rank, and $p$ the oversampling parameter. These bounds assume bounded near-neighbor counts and $O(k)$ local dimensions. Experiments on Laplace, Dirichlet-to-Neumann, and Helmholtz operators in two and three dimensions with up to $1.28$ million unknowns show that, with sufficient oversampling, carried recovery achieves nearly refinement-independent error and accuracy comparable to resketched recovery while using many fewer operator applications. Its compressed application cost scales linearly for a fixed rank.

</details>


### [46] [Instability of Gaussian elimination is exponentially rare (proof of partial result)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04761)
*Lloyd N. Trefethen*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the probability of large growth factors in Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting, proving that the corner entry of the upper-triangular factor is unlikely to be large for random matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting is the standard dense linear solver, but its theoretical worst-case growth factor is 2^{n-1}, implying instability. In practice it is stable, and experiments show large growth factors are exponentially rare for random matrices. This paper aims to theoretically confirm this observation.

Method: The authors prove the result not for the full growth factor (which depends on all entries of U) but for the corner entry u_{nn} of the upper-triangular factor for random matrices with independent normally distributed entries.

Result: They prove that large values of the corner entry u_{nn} are exponentially rare, providing a step toward confirming the observed rarity of large growth factors.

Conclusion: This work partially confirms the practical stability of Gaussian elimination by showing the corner entry is unlikely to be large, though the full growth factor remains open.

Abstract: Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting is the standard algorithm for solving a dense $n\times n$ system of linear equations $Ax=b$. The ``growth factor'' $ρ\,$ for this process may be as large as $2^{n-1}$, and when $ρ$ is large, the algorithm is unstable. Nevertheless, long experience has shown that Gaussian elimination is resoundingly stable in practice. Experiments indicate that among random matrices with independent normally distributed entries, growth factors $ρ\gg n^{1/2}$ are in a precise sense exponentially rare. Here we take a step toward confirming this observation by proving it not for the full growth factor, which depends on all the entries of the upper-triangular factor $U$, but for the corner entry $u_{nn}^{}$.

</details>


### [47] [A general synthetic iterative scheme with high-order spatial discretization for rarefied gas mixture flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04880)
*Jianan Zeng,Wei Su*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A high-order discontinuous Galerkin formulation of the general synthetic iterative scheme (GSIS-DG) is developed for steady rarefied gas mixture flows, achieving high-order accuracy, continuum-limit recovery, and large speedups over conventional kinetic iteration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional kinetic iteration for steady rarefied gas flows is computationally expensive, especially in the near-continuum regime, and existing finite-volume GSIS is limited to second-order accuracy. There is a need for a high-order scheme that keeps fast convergence while avoiding the need to resolve the molecular mean free path.

Method: GSIS-DG discretizes the mesoscopic kinetic equations for monatomic gas mixtures and the macroscopic synthetic equations with the same polynomial degree, solving them alternately. The kinetic solution provides nonequilibrium corrections to stress and heat flux, and the synthetic equations accelerate convergence to steady state. Asymptotic analysis is used to verify the continuum-limit behavior.

Result: Numerical tests across a wide range of Knudsen numbers show high-order spatial accuracy and rapid steady-state convergence, with over an order-of-magnitude reduction in computational cost versus conventional kinetic iteration near the continuum regime. Compared with second-order finite-volume GSIS, GSIS-DG gives comparable or better accuracy on coarser meshes with fewer total spatial degrees of freedom.

Conclusion: GSIS-DG is an efficient, high-order method for steady rarefied gas mixture flows, recovering the continuum limit without resolving the mean free path and outperforming both conventional kinetic iteration and second-order finite-volume GSIS in accuracy and cost.

Abstract: We develop a high-order discontinuous Galerkin formulation of the general synthetic iterative scheme (GSIS) for steady rarefied gas mixture flows. The resulting GSIS-DG discretizes the mesoscopic kinetic equations for monatomic gas mixtures and the macroscopic synthetic equations with the same polynomial degree and solves them alternately. The kinetic solution supplies nonequilibrium corrections to the stress and heat flux, while the synthetic equations accelerate the convergence toward the steady state. Asymptotic analysis shows that GSIS-DG recovers the continuum limit without requiring the spatial mesh to resolve the molecular mean free path. Numerical results over a wide range of Knudsen numbers demonstrate high-order spatial accuracy and rapid steady-state convergence, with more than an order-of-magnitude reduction in computational cost relative to conventional kinetic iteration in the near-continuum regime. Comparisons with second-order finite-volume GSIS further demonstrate that GSIS-DG achieves comparable or better accuracy on coarser physical meshes with fewer total spatial degrees of freedom.

</details>


### [48] [A second-order energy-stable low-regularity integrator for the Navier--Stokes equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05045)
*Shu Ma*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A second-order exponential low-regularity integrator for incompressible Navier–Stokes is proposed; it uses only linear solves, satisfies discrete energy decay, achieves second-order temporal convergence robust as viscosity vanishes, and optimal spatial convergence for Fourier–Galerkin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard integrators for Navier–Stokes often require nonlinear solves and lose robustness as viscosity tends to zero; there is a need for a low-regularity, efficient scheme with good stability properties.

Method: A second-order exponential low-regularity integrator requiring only linear solves. Analysis under a uniform H^4 solution bound; extension to conforming finite element methods while preserving discrete energy-decay.

Result: Second-order temporal convergence with error constant bounded uniformly as viscosity → 0; optimal spatial convergence for Fourier–Galerkin at fixed viscosity; numerical results confirm convergence orders and viscosity robustness.

Conclusion: The scheme offers an efficient, stable, and viscosity-robust second-order method for incompressible Navier–Stokes, extensible to finite elements.

Abstract: A second-order exponential low-regularity integrator is proposed for the incompressible Navier--Stokes equations. The method requires only linear solves and satisfies a discrete energy-decay property. Second-order temporal convergence is established under a uniform $H^4$ solution bound, with an error constant that remains bounded as the viscosity tends to zero. For the Fourier--Galerkin discretization, the optimal spatial convergence rate is established for fixed viscosity. The method can also be extended to conforming finite element methods while retaining the discrete energy-decay structure. Numerical results confirm the convergence orders and robustness with respect to the viscosity.

</details>


### [49] [Oriented point-cloud varifolds for estimating the perimeter and computing its Wasserstein gradient flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05064)
*Tokuhiro Eto,Noboru Isobe*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A mesh-free method for one-phase Mullins-Sekerka flow using an oriented point cloud (varifold) representation, with a perimeter estimator that handles hidden boundaries and provably converges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing Mullins-Sekerka flow (Wasserstein gradient flow of perimeter) is hard because when components merge, two interface pieces with opposite normals create a "hidden boundary" that standard methods cannot handle.

Method: Represent the interface as an oriented point cloud treated as an oriented varifold. Introduce a quantity measuring cancellation of nearby normals to detect hidden boundaries, build a perimeter estimator from it, and use it in a minimizing movement scheme in 2D.

Result: The expected error of the perimeter estimator decays at an algebraic rate in the number of points, even with a hidden boundary. Numerical experiments show mergers of components are computed through the hidden boundary, perimeter decreases each step, and area is nearly conserved.

Conclusion: The oriented representation handles hidden boundaries and is expected to apply to other perimeter-driven interface problems.

Abstract: This paper develops a mesh-free numerical method for the one-phase Mullins-Sekerka flow, in which the interface bounding a set moves as the Wasserstein gradient flow of its perimeter. One difficulty in computing this flow is that two pieces of the interface with opposite normals form a hidden boundary when components merge. Our approach represents the interface by an oriented point cloud, which we treat as an oriented varifold. We introduce a quantity that measures how much nearby normals cancel each other and use it to detect the hidden boundary. Based on this quantity, we construct a perimeter estimator, prove that its expected error decays at an algebraic rate in the number of points, also in the presence of a hidden boundary, and use it in a minimizing movement scheme for the flow in two dimensions. Numerical experiments show that the scheme computes mergers of components through the hidden boundary, while the perimeter term in the scheme decreases at every step and the area is nearly conserved. As hidden boundaries also appear in other perimeter-driven interface problems, we expect our oriented representation to be applicable to them as well.

</details>


### [50] [Dispersion-corrected compact finite difference discretizations for the elastic Helmholtz equation in two and three dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05110)
*Rachel Yovel,Eli Turkel,Eran Treister*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A generalized MAC (GMAC) framework enables compact finite-difference discretization of the elastic Helmholtz equation, with an elastic dispersion correction (EDC) that substantially reduces dispersion error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dispersion correction is well established for scalar Helmholtz problems, but few approaches exist for elastic problems, and most rely on non-compact stencils.

Method: Introduces a generalized MAC (GMAC) framework for compact discretization of the elastic Helmholtz equation, plus an elastic dispersion correction (EDC) process extending real-shifted-wavenumber methods from acoustic to elastic problems.

Result: GMAC is non-symmetric but produces no numerical dissipation or shear-mode separation; for certain weights it has lower polarization error than standard MAC. GMAC is proven and shown numerically to be second-order accurate. EDC greatly reduces dispersion for standard MAC and adds correction in some GMAC cases. The method outperforms existing compact schemes in dispersion accuracy and is competitive with some non-compact schemes, with improved phase alignment in mildly heterogeneous media.

Conclusion: The GMAC+EDC discretization provides an accurate, compact, dispersion-corrected scheme for elastic Helmholtz problems.

Abstract: We develop a family of compact, dispersion-corrected marker-and-cell (MAC)-based finite-difference discretizations for the elastic Helmholtz equation. While dispersion correction is well established for scalar Helmholtz problems, fewer approaches exist for the elastic problems, and most use non-compact stencils. To this end, we introduce a generalized MAC (GMAC) framework that enables compact discretization of the elastic Helmholtz equation. We prove that even though GMAC is non-symmetric, this discretization produces no numerical dissipation and no shear-mode separation. For certain weights, GMAC has lower polarization error than standard MAC. We also introduce an elastic dispersion correction (EDC) process, extending real-shifted-wavenumber dispersion correction methods from acoustic to elastic Helmholtz problems. We prove and demonstrate numerically that GMAC is second-order accurate. Furthermore, we show that EDC substantially reduces dispersion for standard MAC and can provide additional correction in some instances of GMAC. Dispersion analysis shows that the resulting method outperforms existing compact finite-difference discretizations in dispersion accuracy and is competitive with some non-compact schemes. We give numerical evidence for improved phase alignment in mildly heterogeneous media.

</details>


### [51] [Convergence and error analysis for numerical approximations of stochastic Leray-Lions equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05171)
*Ngan Le,Huateng Zhu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper analyzes a divergence-form stochastic differential equation with a Leray-Lions flux, covering multiplicative Lipschitz and transport noise, and develops a gradient discretisation method to prove convergence and, for Lipschitz noise, convergence rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic divergence-form equations with Leray-Lions type fluxes are challenging due to nonlinear monotone operators. A generic numerical framework is needed to handle various discretisation schemes under different noise types.

Method: Gradient discretisation method (GDM), a generic framework covering conforming/nonconforming FEM, hybrid mimetic mixed methods, and hybrid high order methods. Stable generic schemes are proposed for both multiplicative Lipschitz continuous noise and transport noise.

Result: For both noise types, scheme solutions converge to a strong solution in the probabilistic sense. For multiplicative Lipschitz continuous noise, the rate of convergence is established.

Conclusion: The GDM provides a unified, stable framework for numerically solving stochastic Leray-Lions equations, yielding strong convergence for Lipschitz and transport noise, plus convergence rates for the Lipschitz case.

Abstract: We analyse a divergence form stochastic differential equation featuring a Leray-Lions flux function that satisfies standard Carathéodory, growth, monotonicity, and coercivity conditions. We consider two types of noise: multiplicative Lipschitz continuous noise and a transport noise. Our approach utilises the gradient discretisation method, a generic framework encompassing various numerical schemes such as conforming and nonconforming finite element methods, hybrid mimetic mixed methods, and hybrid high order methods. For both types of noise, we propose a stable generic scheme, prove that scheme solutions converge to a strong solution in the probabilistic sense. For the multiplicative Lipschitz continuous noise, we establish the rate of convergence.

</details>


### [52] [Multiresolution Sobolev Trained Fourier Neural Operator for Cross-Resolution Surrogate Modelling of Parametric Two-Phase Darcy Flows in Porous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05188)
*Zhao Zhang,Chengjin Guo,Zhenglong Chen,Kai Zhang,Piyang Liu,Xia Yan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A multiresolution Sobolev learning method is proposed to train Fourier neural operators (FNO) for accurate cross-resolution surrogate modeling of two-phase transient Darcy flows with random parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Surrogate modeling improves computational efficiency of uncertainty quantification for physical processes with random parameters. FNO's key advantage over conventional surrogates is cross-resolution prediction, but achieving high cross-resolution accuracy remains a challenge.

Method: An FNO-based cross-resolution surrogate is built for two-phase transient Darcy flows in porous media. A multiresolution Sobolev learning method augments the FNO loss with first- and second-order gradients, approximated by resolution-related finite difference schemes compatible with the simulation grid, using training samples at different resolutions.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in training FNO for building cross-resolution surrogates.

Conclusion: Incorporating multiresolution Sobolev gradient information enhances FNO cross-resolution prediction accuracy, offering an effective surrogate for uncertainty quantification in porous media flows.

Abstract: Surrogate modelling is important to enhance the computational efficiency of uncertainty quantification for physical processes involving random parameters. The advantage of Fourier neural operator (FNO) compared to conventional surrogates is its cross-resolution prediction capability. In the current study, a cross-resolution surrogate based on FNO is built for two-phase transient Darcy flows in porous media with random parameters. A multiresolution Sobolev learning method is proposed for FNO to enhance the cross-resolution prediction accuracy by incorporating first and second-order gradients in the loss function using training samples of different resolutions. The gradients are approximated by resolution-related finite difference schemes compatible with the grid for numerical simulation. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the new method in training FNO for building cross-resolution surrogates.

</details>


### [53] [On finite elements for geometrically-exact planar beams with hyperelastic material models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05231)
*Abhishek Ghosh,Chennakesava Kadapa,Djordje Peric,Mokarram Hossain*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A geometrically-exact planar beam finite-element framework integrating hyperelastic models, using an added thickness-deformation field with independent plane-stress/plane-strain treatment of out-of-plane behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing planar geometrically-exact beam models often rely on plane-strain assumptions that couple stiffness to Poisson's ratio, and lack a simple, consistent way to embed hyperelastic constitutive laws. A framework is needed to decouple cross-sectional deformation assumptions from constitutive behavior.

Method: A conventional three-field beam model is formulated with full geometrical nonlinearity and a linear elastic law. Kinematics are enriched with an additional global field allowing in-plane thickness deformation. The out-of-plane direction is handled separately via plane-strain and plane-stress constitutive reductions, so cross-sectional deformation assumptions and constitutive behaviour are formulated independently. Saint Venant-Kirchhoff and Neo-Hookean hyperelastic models are compared.

Result: Plane-stress conditions yield essentially coincident centreline deformations across Poisson's ratios, whereas the widely used plane-strain condition makes the formulation sensitive to Poisson coupling, with the beam becoming significantly stiffer at higher Poisson's ratios. Saint Venant-Kirchhoff and Neo-Hookean models agree closely in global deformation and in-plane thickness response, with small differences appearing in local out-of-plane stretch.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a simple and consistent framework for integrating hyperelastic material models into planar geometrically-exact beam models, and highlights the importance of the out-of-plane constitutive reduction choice.

Abstract: A geometrically-exact planar beam finite-element framework is developed for the analysis of beams with hyperelastic constitutive models. A conventional three-field beam model is first formulated with full geometrical nonlinearity and a linear elastic material law. The kinematics are then enriched by an additional global field that permits deformation through the in-plane thickness. The out-of-plane direction is incorporated separately through plane-strain and plane-stress constitutive reductions, allowing the cross-sectional deformation assumptions and constitutive behaviour to be formulated independently. Numerical results for the present benchmarks show that the plane-stress condition gives essentially coincident centreline deformations for the Poisson's ratio values considered. In contrast, the plane-strain condition, which is widely employed in the literature, makes the formulation sensitive to Poisson's ratio coupling, with the beam becoming significantly stiffer at higher Poisson's ratios. Comparisons between Saint Venant-Kirchhoff and Neo-Hookean hyperelastic models further demonstrate close agreement in the global deformation and in-plane thickness response, with small constitutive differences becoming more apparent in the local out-of-plane stretch. The proposed approach offers a simple and consistent framework to integrate hyperelastic material models into planar geometrically-exact beam models.

</details>


### [54] [A Finite Element Method for the Grad--Mercier Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05313)
*Ben Ashby,Tristan Pryer*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A conforming piecewise affine FEM is developed and analyzed for the Grad–Mercier nonlocal elliptic equation, handling the rank-based source via a clipped law and mass-lumped nodal measure to guarantee a unique discrete equilibrium and convergence to the continuum solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Grad–Mercier equation models magnetic equilibria with a source depending on the global rank of a flux function. Its unique continuum equilibrium has a maximum plateau of prescribed positive area, so the solution only samples the rank law above the plateau mass. Standard discretizations that split equal values across several numerical ranks alter the non-strict rank source, making naive FEM approaches problematic.

Method: Conforming piecewise affine finite element method. The rank law is replaced by an equivalent clipped, nonnegative law; Lebesgue measure is replaced by a mass-lumped nodal measure. Equal nodal values are assigned a common rank interval, retaining the full lumped mass of each tied level. This weighted rearrangement yields a strictly convex discrete energy and a unique FE equilibrium. A Poisson-preconditioned rearrangement iteration is used with computable a posteriori energy and energy-norm bounds.

Result: Unique discrete equilibrium with strictly convex energy. On meshes satisfying a discrete comparison principle, the discrete equilibrium is nonnegative and bounded above by a computable barrier. The rearrangement iteration converges on each fixed mesh. As polygonal FE domains converge to the physical cross-section, discrete equilibria converge strongly in H^1_0(Ω) and uniformly on the closure to the unique Grad–Mercier equilibrium. Reconstructed sources converge weak-* in L^∞(Ω) to the original nonlocal source.

Conclusion: The method successfully captures the plateau structure and non-strict rank source of the Grad–Mercier equilibrium, providing a well-posed, convergent finite element discretization with a posteriori error control and rigorous convergence to the continuum solution.

Abstract: We develop and analyse a conforming piecewise affine finite element method for the Grad--Mercier equation, a nonlocal elliptic model for magnetic equilibria whose source depends on the global rank of a flux function. The principal difficulty is that the unique continuum equilibrium has a maximum plateau of prescribed positive area. The solution therefore samples the rank law only above the plateau mass, while perturbations that separate equal values may distribute this mass over several numerical ranks and thereby alter the non-strict rank source.
  We exploit the continuum well-posedness structure to replace the rank law by an equivalent clipped, nonnegative law, and replace Lebesgue measure by a mass-lumped nodal measure. Equal nodal values are assigned a common rank interval, thereby retaining the full lumped mass of each tied level.
  The resulting weighted rearrangement gives a strictly convex discrete energy and hence a unique finite element equilibrium. On meshes satisfying a discrete comparison principle, the discrete equilibrium is nonnegative and bounded above by a computable barrier. A Poisson-preconditioned rearrangement iteration converges on each fixed mesh and admits computable a posteriori energy and energy-norm bounds. As polygonal finite element domains converge to the physical cross-section, the discrete equilibria converge strongly in $H^1_0(Ω)$ and uniformly on $\overlineΩ$ to the unique Grad--Mercier equilibrium. Reconstructed equilibrium sources converge weak-* in $L^{\infty}(Ω)$ to the original nonlocal source.

</details>


### [55] [Structure Preserving High-Order FDTD Methods for the Nonlinear Kerr-Debye Model with Lorentz Dispersion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05507)
*Emmanuel E. Oguadimma,Vrushali A. Bokil,Nathan L. Gibson*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Development and analysis of fully discrete, high-order FDTD methods for a nonlinear dispersive electromagnetic model of ultrashort pulse propagation, achieving asymptotic-preserving, positivity-preserving, and energy-stable schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating electromagnetic wave propagation in nonlinear dispersive optical media requires efficient, robust numerical tools; existing AP-PP and energy-stable approaches within DG frameworks are extended to the more computationally efficient FDTD setting.

Method: Combine staggered spatial discretizations of 2M-th order with second-order explicit leapfrog or implicit trapezoidal time integrators; use a modified exponential integrator for stiff Kerr-Debye relaxation to ensure AP behavior; prove discrete energy identity and positivity; achieve high-order temporal accuracy via Richardson extrapolation.

Result: Schemes satisfy a discrete energy identity, preserve positivity of nonlinear susceptibility, are energy stable (CFL-conditioned for leapfrog, unconditional for trapezoidal), and attain 2M-th order accuracy in space and time; numerical experiments confirm convergence rates and capture soliton propagation, self-steepening, and odd harmonic generation.

Conclusion: Proposed FDTD methods offer a computationally efficient, flexible, and robust tool for simulating electromagnetic wave propagation in nonlinear dispersive optical media, extending prior AP-PP and energy-stable DG approaches.

Abstract: We develop and analyze several families of fully discrete, high-order methods in the finite difference time domain (FDTD) framework for a nonlinear dispersive electromagnetic model for ultrashort pulse propagation in a nonlinear optical medium.
