# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [209] [AI-Assisted GPU optimization of the Stochastic Variational Method for Few-Body Boson System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02745)
*Noritaka Shimizu,Shigeyoshi Aoyama,Yusuke Tsunoda,Akira Nukada*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A GPU-optimized, AI-agent-written stochastic variational method (SVM) code for few-body ⁴He clusters achieves up to 16x speedup on NVIDIA GH200 and AMD MI300A over CPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SVM is a powerful method for quantum few-body systems but no GPU-optimized SVM code has been reported, leaving performance gains untapped.

Method: Developed a parallel GPU SVM code (written entirely by an AI coding agent), introducing the secular-equation method with Gu–Eisenstat prescription for the generalized eigenvalue problem. Tuning batched many small matrices and used coalesced memory layouts.

Result: For a five-body system with 4800 basis states: 16.2x (14.7x) faster on NVIDIA GH200 (AMD MI300A) than tuned CPU Intel Xeon Max, and 168x (153x) faster than first CPU implementation.

Conclusion: The achieved performance enables 6-body and larger cluster systems within reach.

Abstract: The stochastic variational method (SVM) is one of the most powerful methods to solve quantum few-body systems precisely in various fields, such as nuclear and atomic physics. To the best of our knowledge, no SVM code optimized for GPUs has been reported. We developed a parallel SVM code for few-body clusters of $^4$He atoms and optimized it for two GPU architectures, NVIDIA GH200 and AMD Instinct MI300A, with the entire code written by an AI coding agent. On the algorithmic side, we introduce the secular-equation method with the Gu--Eisenstat prescription for solving the generalized eigenvalue problem within the SVM framework. The main part of the code tuning is to batch the many small matrices so that the GPUs are used efficiently, together with an array layout in memory optimized for coalesced addressing. For the five-body system with 4800 SVM basis states, the tuned SVM code runs 16.2 (14.7) times faster on the NVIDIA GH200 (AMD MI300A) than the same tuned code on the Intel Xeon Max CPU system, and 168 (153) times faster than the first working CPU implementation. The achieved performance brings 6-body and larger cluster systems within reach.

</details>


### [210] [Experimental Bench Validation of a Computational Fluid-Structure Interaction Model of Transcatheter Aortic Valve Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03297)
*Jae H. Lee,Paul Ahern,Anh D. Nguyen,Masod Sadipour,Robert Hunt,Wyatt Kmetz,Boyce E. Griffith*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This study extends an FSI framework for surgical bioprosthetic valves to a TAVR device in a pulse duplicator, validating hemodynamics and leaflet kinematics across 60/70/80 bpm with parameters calibrated only at 70 bpm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Surgical bioprosthetic valve FSI frameworks need extension to TAVR devices, and the transferability of calibrated boundary-model parameters across pulse rates remains unverified.

Method: Simulated vs. experimental comparison of pressure, volumetric flow rate, PDVA, and leaflet kinematics at 60, 70, and 80 bpm. Reduced-order boundary-model parameters were calibrated only at 70 bpm and held fixed; condition-specific pump-pressure waveforms were built from measured upstream pressure and flow.

Result: Normalized RMSE: 2.88-3.08% (aortic flow), 7.64-8.07% (upstream ventricular pressure), 3.50-6.21% (downstream aortic pressure), 3.07-4.70% (PDVA). Principal pressure, flow, opening, and closure features reproduced; 60/80 bpm downstream pressure and PDVA serve as out-of-calibration validation.

Conclusion: Conditional bench validation for downstream hemodynamics and valve dynamics under measurement-informed upstream forcing; supports boundary-parameter transfer across pulse rates without recalibration, advancing an experimentally supported high-fidelity FSI framework for TAVR evaluation.

Abstract: Purpose: This study extends our fluid-structure interaction (FSI) framework for surgical bioprosthetic heart valves in an experimental pulse duplicator to a transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) device. We assess hemodynamic and leaflet kinematic agreement across pulse rates and the transferability of calibrated boundary-model parameters.
  Methods: We compared simulated and experimental pressure, volumetric flow rate, projected dynamic valve area (PDVA), and leaflet kinematics at 60, 70, and 80 beats per minute (bpm). Reduced-order boundary-model parameters were calibrated using only data acquired at 70 bpm and were subsequently held fixed. At each pulse rate, a condition-specific pump-pressure waveform was constructed from the measured upstream pressure and flow rate. The 60 and 80 bpm cases therefore evaluate parameter transfer under measurement-informed upstream forcing.
  Results: Across the three conditions, normalized root-mean-square errors are 2.88-3.08% for aortic flow rate, 7.64-8.07% for upstream ventricular pressure, 3.50-6.21% for downstream aortic pressure, and 3.07-4.70% for PDVA. The simulations reproduce the principal pressure, flow, valve-opening, and valve-closure features. The downstream aortic pressure and PDVA measurements at 60 and 80 bpm were not used to construct the upstream forcing or recalibrate the model parameters and therefore provide the principal out-of-calibration validation evidence.
  Conclusion: The results provide conditional experimental bench validation evidence for downstream hemodynamics and valve dynamics under measurement-informed upstream forcing and support boundary-parameter transfer across pulse rates without recalibration. This work advances an experimentally supported, high-fidelity FSI framework for evaluating TAVR device performance.

</details>


### [211] [AI-Assisted GPU optimization of the Stochastic Variational Method for Few-Body Boson System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02745)
*Noritaka Shimizu,Shigeyoshi Aoyama,Yusuke Tsunoda,Akira Nukada*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A GPU-optimized SVM code for few-body 4He clusters, written entirely by an AI coding agent, achieves large speedups on NVIDIA GH200 and AMD MI300A.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stochastic variational method (SVM) is powerful for solving quantum few-body systems, but no GPU-optimized SVM code had been reported, limiting performance for larger systems.

Method: Developed a parallel SVM code for 4He atom clusters, using the secular-equation method with the Gu-Eisenstat prescription for the generalized eigenvalue problem. Code was written by an AI coding agent. Tuning batched many small matrices for GPU efficiency with a coalesced-addressing memory layout optimized for NVIDIA GH200 and AMD MI300A.

Result: For a five-body system with 4800 SVM basis states, the tuned code ran 16.2x (14.7x) faster on NVIDIA GH200 (AMD MI300A) than tuned code on Intel Xeon Max CPU, and 168x (153x) faster than the first working CPU implementation.

Conclusion: The achieved performance makes 6-body and larger cluster systems feasible.

Abstract: The stochastic variational method (SVM) is one of the most powerful methods to solve quantum few-body systems precisely in various fields, such as nuclear and atomic physics. To the best of our knowledge, no SVM code optimized for GPUs has been reported. We developed a parallel SVM code for few-body clusters of $^4$He atoms and optimized it for two GPU architectures, NVIDIA GH200 and AMD Instinct MI300A, with the entire code written by an AI coding agent. On the algorithmic side, we introduce the secular-equation method with the Gu--Eisenstat prescription for solving the generalized eigenvalue problem within the SVM framework. The main part of the code tuning is to batch the many small matrices so that the GPUs are used efficiently, together with an array layout in memory optimized for coalesced addressing. For the five-body system with 4800 SVM basis states, the tuned SVM code runs 16.2 (14.7) times faster on the NVIDIA GH200 (AMD MI300A) than the same tuned code on the Intel Xeon Max CPU system, and 168 (153) times faster than the first working CPU implementation. The achieved performance brings 6-body and larger cluster systems within reach.

</details>


### [212] [Experimental Bench Validation of a Computational Fluid-Structure Interaction Model of Transcatheter Aortic Valve Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03297)
*Jae H. Lee,Paul Ahern,Anh D. Nguyen,Masod Sadipour,Robert Hunt,Wyatt Kmetz,Boyce E. Griffith*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This study extends an FSI framework for surgical bioprosthetic valves to a TAVR device, validating hemodynamics and leaflet kinematics across pulse rates (60/70/80 bpm) with parameters calibrated only at 70 bpm and held fixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior FSI work targeted surgical bioprosthetic heart valves; it remained unclear whether such a framework and its calibrated boundary-model parameters could transfer to a TAVR device and across different pulse rates without recalibration.

Method: Simulated vs. experimental comparison of pressure, volumetric flow rate, PDVA, and leaflet kinematics at 60, 70, and 80 bpm in a pulse duplicator. Reduced-order boundary-model parameters were calibrated using only 70 bpm data and held fixed. Condition-specific pump-pressure waveforms were built from measured upstream pressure and flow, so 60 and 80 bpm cases test parameter transfer under measurement-informed upstream forcing.

Result: Normalized RMSE: 2.88-3.08% (aortic flow rate), 7.64-8.07% (upstream ventricular pressure), 3.50-6.21% (downstream aortic pressure), 3.07-4.70% (PDVA). Simulations reproduced main pressure, flow, and valve opening/closure features. Downstream aortic pressure and PDVA at 60 and 80 bpm served as out-of-calibration validation evidence.

Conclusion: Provides conditional experimental bench validation for downstream hemodynamics and valve dynamics under measurement-informed upstream forcing, and supports boundary-parameter transfer across pulse rates without recalibration, advancing an experimentally supported high-fidelity FSI framework for TAVR performance evaluation.

Abstract: Purpose: This study extends our fluid-structure interaction (FSI) framework for surgical bioprosthetic heart valves in an experimental pulse duplicator to a transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) device. We assess hemodynamic and leaflet kinematic agreement across pulse rates and the transferability of calibrated boundary-model parameters.
  Methods: We compared simulated and experimental pressure, volumetric flow rate, projected dynamic valve area (PDVA), and leaflet kinematics at 60, 70, and 80 beats per minute (bpm). Reduced-order boundary-model parameters were calibrated using only data acquired at 70 bpm and were subsequently held fixed. At each pulse rate, a condition-specific pump-pressure waveform was constructed from the measured upstream pressure and flow rate. The 60 and 80 bpm cases therefore evaluate parameter transfer under measurement-informed upstream forcing.
  Results: Across the three conditions, normalized root-mean-square errors are 2.88-3.08% for aortic flow rate, 7.64-8.07% for upstream ventricular pressure, 3.50-6.21% for downstream aortic pressure, and 3.07-4.70% for PDVA. The simulations reproduce the principal pressure, flow, valve-opening, and valve-closure features. The downstream aortic pressure and PDVA measurements at 60 and 80 bpm were not used to construct the upstream forcing or recalibrate the model parameters and therefore provide the principal out-of-calibration validation evidence.
  Conclusion: The results provide conditional experimental bench validation evidence for downstream hemodynamics and valve dynamics under measurement-informed upstream forcing and support boundary-parameter transfer across pulse rates without recalibration. This work advances an experimentally supported, high-fidelity FSI framework for evaluating TAVR device performance.

</details>


### [213] [Spectral properties of the Riemann zeta function and their physical applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04571)
*Kostadin G Gaminchev*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A numerical study of the Riemann ζ function emphasizing three physical manifestations: zero spectral statistics, Bose-Einstein condensation, and Casimir effect regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect the Riemann ζ function to physical phenomena, examining its role in spectral statistics of non-trivial zeros, BEC critical temperature, and Casimir effect regularization.

Method: Numerical investigation in three areas: (i) comparing non-trivial zero spectral statistics with Gaussian Unitary Ensemble predictions via χ²/dof; (ii) evaluating ζ(3/2)'s role in the BEC critical temperature for an ideal gas; (iii) applying ζ-function regularization to the Casimir effect.

Result: Spectral statistics are consistent with GUE (χ²/dof = 1.2); BEC critical temperature T_c = 0.13 K; Casimir energy density -π²ħc/720a³, giving ~-4.33×10⁻⁴ J m⁻² for a = 1 nm. Results align with the Montgomery-Odlyzko conjecture and Hilbert-Pólya-inspired spectral approaches.

Conclusion: The numerical results are consistent with the Montgomery-Odlyzko conjecture and spectral approaches motivated by the Hilbert-Pólya conjecture, within statistical limitations.

Abstract: This work studies the Riemann $ζ$ numerically with emphasis on its physical manifestations. Three areas are examined: (i) the spectral statistics of the non-trivial zeros, which we compare with the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble predictions, obtaining $χ^2/\text{dof} = 1.2$; (ii) the role of $ζ(\frac{3}{2})$ in determining the critical temperature for Bose-Einstein condensation, where we find $T_c = 0.13$ K for an ideal gas; and (iii) the application of $ζ$-function regularisation to the Casimir effect, yielding the standard Casimir energy density $-\frac{π^2 \hbar c}{720 a^3}$, which for $a=1\,\mathrm{nm}$ gives $\sim -4.33\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{J\,m^{-2}}$. The numerical results are consistent with the Montgomery-Odlyzko conjecture and with spectral approaches motivated by the Hilbert-Pólya conjecture, within the statistical limitations of the analysis.

</details>


### [214] [HFS-TransNet: A Hybrid Fixed-Stress Transferable Neural Network for Quasi-Static Biot Poroelasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04879)
*Zhequan Shen,Liyong Zhu,Mingchao Cai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Proposes HFS-TransNet, a hybrid fixed-stress transferable neural network for solving the quasi-static Biot poroelastic system, combining data-driven initialization with physics-informed fixed-stress iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quasi-static Biot system for coupled fluid-solid interactions in poroelastic media is computationally challenging due to strong coupling, multiscale nature, and locking instability.

Method: HFS-TransNet uses a data-driven TransNet module to approximate data on an initialization domain, providing a good starting point for a physics-informed stage that drives fixed-stress splitting iterations via TransNet with an error bound. A greedy update on a validation domain prevents degradation, and a relative tolerance governs convergence.

Result: Ablation studies and comparisons with FS-FEM and FS-PINN show HFS-TransNet's superior performance in capturing multiscale coupled physics across varying physical parameters and boundary conditions, demonstrated on brain edema simulations.

Conclusion: HFS-TransNet bridges classical decoupled iterative schemes with hybrid scientific machine learning, inheriting robustness, flexibility, and transferability, offering a novel perspective for complex poroelastic simulations.

Abstract: The quasi-static Biot system, which governs coupled fluid-solid interactions in poroelastic media, poses significant computational challenges due to its strong coupling and multiscale nature. To address these, we propose a hybrid fixed-stress transferable neural network (HFS-TransNet) method. In HFS-TransNet, a data-driven TransNet module first approximates observed data on an initialization domain to supply a high-quality starting point for the subsequent physics-informed stage, which then drives fixed-stress splitting iterations via TransNet and admits an error bound. After each iteration, a greedy update strategy on a validation domain prevents model degradation and a relative tolerance criterion governs convergence termination. HFS-TransNet thereby inherits the robustness of the fixed-stress splitting scheme, the flexibility of data-driven modeling, and the transferability of TransNet, effectively overcoming the strong coupling and locking instability inherent in the Biot system. Ablation studies and comparisons with FS-FEM and FS-PINN confirm HFS-TransNet's superior performance in capturing multiscale coupled physics across varying physical parameters and boundary conditions. By bridging classical decoupled iterative schemes with hybrid scientific machine learning, HFS-TransNet offers a novel perspective for complex poroelastic simulations, as demonstrated by its successful application to brain edema simulations.

</details>


### [215] [A Contrast-Source Inversion Scheme Based on Stochastic Optimization and Plug-and-Play Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05130)
*Lingqi Gao,Hakan Bagci*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes STO-PNP-CSI, an electromagnetic inversion scheme combining stochastic optimization with plug-and-play regularization (SCUNet denoiser) into contrast-source inversion, achieving accurate reconstructions at lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CSI solves for every transmitter's contrast source vector at each iteration, which is computationally expensive in multi-transmitter configurations. A cheaper approach is needed that also avoids poor local minima and saddle points.

Method: STO-PNP-CSI integrates stochastic optimization (STO) and plug-and-play (PNP) regularization into contrast-source inversion (CSI). STO solves only one randomly selected contrast source vector per iteration, reducing per-iteration cost and helping escape poor local minima/saddle points. To counter the resulting information loss and increased ill-posedness, the Swin-Conv-UNet (SCUNet) denoiser is plugged in as an implicit regularizer, providing a learned prior stronger than hand-crafted ones.

Result: Applied to both synthetic and experimental data, STO-PNP-CSI yields accurate reconstructions at substantially lower computational cost than CSI, including under strong nonlinearity and measurement noise.

Conclusion: STO-PNP-CSI offers an efficient and robust electromagnetic inversion method, combining stochastic optimization with learned plug-and-play regularization to outperform standard CSI in cost and accuracy.

Abstract: An electromagnetic inversion scheme that integrates stochastic optimization (STO) and plug-and-play (PNP) regularization into contrast-source inversion (CSI), termed STO-PNP-CSI, is developed. Standard CSI solves for the contrast source vector of every transmitter at each iteration, which is expensive in a multi-transmitter configuration. STO instead solves for only one randomly selected contrast source vector per iteration, which reduces the per-iteration cost and can help the inversion escape poor local minima and saddle points. The resulting loss of information, however, increases the ill-posedness of the inversion. To counter this, the Swin-Conv-UNet (SCUNet) denoiser is plugged into the CSI scheme as an implicit regularizer, supplying a learned prior that is stronger than conventional hand-crafted ones and stabilizes the reconstruction. The proposed STO-PNP-CSI is applied to both synthetic and experimental data. The results show that it yields accurate reconstructions at substantially lower computational cost than CSI, including under strong nonlinearity and measurement noise.

</details>


### [216] [Mechanism-resolved phase-field fracture of composite shells with a certified admissible constitutive operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06705)
*Altaf Ahmad Lone,R. T. Durai Prabhakaran,Timon Rabczuk,Xiaoying Zhuang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A phase-field fracture framework for fibre-reinforced shells where the constitutive law is a replaceable operator, formulated via orthogonal invariants and validated on notched IM7/8552 laminates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing phase-field models for fibre-reinforced shells assume a single closed-form stored energy, requiring stress, tangent and plane-stress condensation to be re-derived per expression. This limits constitutive flexibility and the ability to use learned operators.

Method: The Green-Lagrange strain of a geometrically exact Reissner-Mindlin shell is pulled back through the Cholesky factor of the reference metric and resolved onto four orthogonal invariants with an exact closure identity, turning fibre/inter-fibre/interaction separation into a change of basis. Thickness variation is captured via the shifter I - zeta b. Tension-compression split, degradation, plane-stress condensation and consistent tangent are expressed in channel potentials; the interaction energy is degraded by the product of coupled fields. Six admissibility conditions are defined, convexity and regularity are proved, and a three-tier certification protocol is introduced.

Result: The two linear invariants are proved convex in the deformation gradient. The geometric tangent is shown independent of the material tangent, and plane-stress condensation is regular wherever residual stiffness is positive. Numerical studies on notched IM7/8552 shells verify discretisation in thin and curved regimes; fibre orientation governs the damage envelope and load capacity, while curvature drives a through-thickness fracture asymmetry not representable by a single midsurface field.

Conclusion: A general, operator-based phase-field shell fracture formulation is established that supports closed-form or learned constitutive laws, with rigorous admissibility proofs and a certification protocol, and demonstrated to capture orientation- and curvature-driven fracture behaviour.

Abstract: Phase-field models of fracture in fibre-reinforced shells are usually formulated against a single closed-form stored energy, so that the stress, consistent tangent and plane-stress condensation are derived and verified for that expression alone. Here the constitutive law enters instead as a replaceable operator. The Green-Lagrange strain of a geometrically exact Reissner-Mindlin shell is pulled back through the Cholesky factor of the reference metric and resolved on four mutually orthogonal invariants with an exact closure identity, making the separation into fibre, inter-fibre and interaction channels a change of basis rather than a modelling assumption. On a curved midsurface, the metric, Cholesky factor and fibre direction vary through the thickness through the shifter I - zeta b, and the invariants inherit that dependence. The tension-compression split, channel degradation, plane-stress condensation, consistent tangent and finite-element assembly are all expressed in the channel potentials and their derivatives, allowing either closed-form or learned operators. The fibre-transverse interaction energy is stored once and degraded by the product of the fields whose mechanisms it couples. Six conditions define admissible operators; the two linear invariants are proved convex in the deformation gradient, the geometric tangent is shown independent of the material tangent, and the plane-stress condensation is regular wherever the residual stiffness is positive. A three-tier protocol certifies the algebra, solver state and admission of a state as training data. Numerical studies on notched IM7/8552 shells verify the discretisation in thin and curved regimes and show that fibre orientation governs the damage envelope and load capacity, while curvature drives a through-thickness fracture asymmetry that a single midsurface field cannot represent.

</details>
