### [217] [IGNITE Tokamak World Model Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02515)
*Peter Steiner,Azarakhsh Jalalvand,Nathaniel Chen,Kouroche Bouchiat,Ricardo Shousha,SangKyeun Kim,Egemen Kolemen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: IGNITE is a self-supervised generative world foundation model for fusion plasma simulation, trained on a decade of unlabeled DIII-D data, capable of predicting entire discharges from actuator trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion require models that can simulate plasma behavior, but labeled fusion data is scarce while large volumes of unlabeled experimental data remain underused.

Method: IGNITE uses spatio-temporal tokenizers to embed multiple input modalities (time-series measurements, image sequences, spectrograms) collected at different time scales, with an auto-regressive dynamics backbone that predicts entire discharges from initial latent plasma states and actuator trajectories over a theoretical infinite horizon. Trajectories can be supplied or generated on-the-fly via textual prompts or desired outcomes.

Result: The model can simulate DIII-D discharges from given actuator trajectories and supports prompt- or outcome-driven trajectory generation.

Conclusion: IGNITE paves the way toward efficient AI-driven experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion.

Abstract: We introduce IGNITE, a generative world foundation model for fusion plasma behavior simulation trained in a self-supervised manner from over a decade of unlabeled experimental data at the DIII-D National Fusion Facility. The core of IGNITE is a dynamics model that can simulate DIII-D discharges from a given set of actuator trajectories. These trajectories can be supplied or generated on-the-fly from a textual prompt or from desired experimental outcomes. The model architecture consists of several spatio-temporal tokenizers that embed the different input modalities, including time-series like spatio-temporal measurement data, image sequences, and high-resolution spectrograms, each of which collected at vastly different time scales. The backbone is composed of an auto-regressive dynamics model that has the capacity to predict entire DIII-D discharges given initial latent plasma states and actuator trajectories over a theoretical infinite horizon. IGNITE paves the way towards efficient AI-driven experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion.

</details>


### [218] [Numerical and diagnostic extensions to the gyrokinetic $δf$ code stella](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02958)
*Sven Stroteich,Georgia Acton,Michael Barnes,Pavlos Xanthopoulos,Ralf Schneider*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses free-energy diagnostics in stella gyrokinetic simulations of W7-X to characterize turbulence and numerical stability, and benchmarks explicit time-integration schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding turbulence in magnetised plasmas requires robust numerical tools that capture complex free-energy transfer across scales, motivating the need for reliable diagnostics and well-chosen numerical schemes.

Method: Applied free-energy diagnostics to W7-X high-mirror configuration gyrokinetic simulations with the stella code; systematically compared explicit time-integration schemes.

Result: Free-energy diagnostics reveal power-law cascade behavior, detect numerical instabilities missed by conventional diagnostics, and expose localized real-space structures not visible in electrostatic potential alone. SSP22 is cheapest for linear ITG; SSP33 is most efficient in the nonlinear saturated state; nonlinear runs cost two to three orders of magnitude more than linear ones.

Conclusion: Free-energy diagnostics are a valuable tool for interpreting plasma turbulence and provide practical guidance for scheme selection in stella simulations.

Abstract: Understanding turbulence in magnetised plasmas requires robust numerical tools capable of capturing complex free-energy transfer processes across scales. We demonstrate the utility of free energy as a diagnostic for both physical turbulence characteristics and numerical stability in gyrokinetic simulations with the stella code. Applied to the W7-X high-mirror configuration, free-energy diagnostics reveal characteristic power-law behaviour in the turbulent cascade, act as sensitive indicators of numerical instabilities undetected by conventional diagnostics, and expose localised structures in real space not apparent in the electrostatic potential alone. To support these analyses, we present a systematic comparison of explicit time-integration schemes, showing that the optimal choice depends critically on the regime: SSP22 achieves the lowest computational cost in linear ITG simulations, while SSP33 is most efficient in the nonlinear saturated state, with nonlinear simulations requiring two to three orders of magnitude more computational cost than linear cases. These results establish free-energy diagnostics as a valuable tool for interpreting plasma turbulence and provide practical guidance for scheme selection in stella simulations.

</details>


### [219] [Dynamics-aware bandwidth selection for smoothed charge deposition in 2D electrostatic particle-in-cell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03230)
*Ayushi Agrawal,Shan-Chang Lin,Ezhilmathi Krishnasamy,Pascal Bouvry*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a dynamics-aware bandwidth-selection procedure for charge deposition in PIC simulations, balancing density-error minimization against constraints that preserve specified linear mode dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charge deposition in PIC acts as kernel density estimation, so bandwidth choice affects both statistical error and the electric field driving particle dynamics, potentially altering physical mode growth/damping. Standard statistical bandwidth selection ignores these dynamics.

Method: Combines a density-error objective with constraints on specified linear modes derived from plasma equilibrium and the combined transfer of deposition, filtering, and field gathering within a model rate-tolerance boundary. Validated via a controlled two-stream experiment, applied to 85 Gaussian candidates, and checked with projected-linear calculations and finite-marker/ensemble-averaging analysis.

Result: Across four resolved equilibria the filter reduces measured growth rate by 0.7%-9.1%, with equilibrium-dependent settings meeting the 10% rate tolerance. The constrained rule lowers density and grid-field MSE by 7.40% and 3.70%. Projected-linear ratio of mean growth rates is 0.9556, 95% interval [0.9453, 0.9640], matching prediction 0.9513.

Conclusion: The proposed filter-design procedure achieves statistical error reduction while subsequently validating preservation of selected linear dynamics, enabling principled, equilibrium-aware bandwidth selection in PIC.

Abstract: Charge deposition in the particle-in-cell (PIC) method can be viewed as kernel density estimation on a spatial mesh. This allows the smoothing bandwidth to be selected through statistical criteria such as mean integrated squared error or cross-validation, but it also changes the electric field acting on the particles. Therefore, a selected bandwidth can alter the growth or damping of physical modes. We develop a dynamics-aware bandwidth-selection procedure that combines a density-error objective with constraints on specified linear modes. These constraints follow from the plasma equilibrium and the combined transfer of deposition, filtering and field gathering within the model rate-tolerance boundary.
  We demonstrate the need for equilibrium information through a controlled two-stream experiment. The controlled two-stream scan varies the beam drift while holding the initial spatial density, thermal samples, wavevector and fixed-filter transfer unchanged. Across four resolved equilibria approaching marginal stability, this filter reduces the measured growth rate by $0.7\%$ to $9.1\%$. Equilibrium-dependent interior settings satisfy the prescribed $10\%$ rate tolerance. We then apply the constrained selection rule to 85 Gaussian candidates, that reduces density and grid-field mean-square errors by $7.40\%$ and $3.70\%$ on independently evaluated snapshots. A separate projected-linear calculation at that exact width gives a ratio of mean individual growth rates of $0.9556$, with paired $95\%$ interval $[0.9453,0.9640]$, consistent with the prediction $0.9513$. Analysis of finite-marker loading and ensemble averaging explains how the measured rates relate to the response model. Together, these results demonstrate a filter-design procedure that combines statistical error reduction with subsequent validation of the selected linear dynamics.

</details>


### [220] [Quantum approaches for particle-in-cell codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03254)
*Ryan J. J. Connor,Andrew J. Daley,Callum W. Duncan,Preetma Soin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A quantum-inspired tensor-network approach is proposed to overcome computational bottlenecks in plasma simulations relative to standard particle-in-cell codes, and extended to support electromagnetic field insertion at arbitrary locations, demonstrated via laser-driven plasma acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving high-dimensional, nonlinear PDEs describing plasma physics is computationally challenging. Tensor-network methods (quantum-inspired) have shown promise in fluid dynamics for data compression and quantum computing pathways, and may help overcome bottlenecks in plasma dynamics, especially for costly industry-relevant scenarios.

Method: The authors develop tensor-network methods and compare them with standard particle-in-cell codes. They extend the tensor-network formulation to allow insertion of electromagnetic fields at arbitrary spatial locations.

Result: The tensor-network methods are shown to overcome bottlenecks of conventional approaches, and the extension is demonstrated through injection of a laser field to accelerate a plasma.

Conclusion: This provides a key step towards tensor-network-based simulation of externally driven plasma systems.

Abstract: Solving the high-dimensional, nonlinear partial differential equations that govern the rich physics of plasmas remains a major computational challenge. In fluid dynamics, quantum-inspired methods based on tensor-network techniques have recently emerged as a promising new paradigm, offering large degrees of data compression on conventional hardware and a natural pathway towards solving exponentially large problems on quantum computers. Early studies suggest that tensor-network methods may also help overcome key bottlenecks in computational plasma dynamics, particularly for industry-relevant scenarios where conventional simulations remain costly. In this work, we present recent progress in developing tensor-network methods compared to standard particle-in-cell codes and discuss their capability to overcome the bottlenecks of conventional approaches. We further extend the tensor-network formulation to support the insertion of electromagnetic fields at arbitrary spatial locations. This capability is demonstrated through the injection of a laser field to accelerate a plasma, providing a key step towards tensor-network-based simulation of externally driven plasma systems.

</details>


### [221] [Parity-Time Symmetry of Plasma Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03433)
*Hong Qin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that the susceptibility tensor of a homogeneous collisionless magnetized plasma is PT-symmetric because the underlying linearized Vlasov-Maxwell kinetic system itself is PT-symmetric, and that many kinetic instabilities arise from spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking via Krein collisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The susceptibility tensor of a homogeneous collisionless magnetized plasma is known to possess parity-time (PT) symmetry, but it was unclear whether this is a mere mathematical coincidence of the reduced response or a manifestation of a deeper structural property of the kinetic system. Understanding this could unify and explain the origin of many kinetic instabilities.

Method: The authors identify parity P and time reversal T operators on the electromagnetic kinetic eigenmode space and show that the Hamiltonian operator of the linearized Vlasov-Maxwell system is PT-symmetric, satisfying PT H_VM (PT)^{-1} = H_VM. They also establish PT symmetry of the homogeneous magnetized Vlasov-Poisson eigenproblem. Instabilities are analyzed as spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking occurring through Krein collisions, illustrated with three examples.

Result: Definite-sign cyclotron residues protect thermal Bernstein modes from spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking. The nonmonotonic Dory-Guest-Harris distribution allows sign-indefinite residues and Krein collisions, yielding an analytical instability threshold and growth rate. The recently discovered mode-pole resonance instability is shown to be a distinct realization of spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking.

Conclusion: PT symmetry is established as a structural principle of plasma waves and instabilities, opening new directions for studying spectral topology in kinetic plasmas, with applications to fusion energy.

Abstract: The susceptibility tensor of a homogeneous collisionless magnetized plasma possesses parity-time (PT) symmetry. For a stationary gyrotropic equilibrium, with the wave vector $\mathbf{k}$ and the equilibrium magnetic field $\mathbf{B}_0$ in the $x$--$z$ plane, the kinetic response satisfies $χ(ω^*,\mathbf{k})=Rχ(ω,\mathbf{k})^*R$, where $R=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,1)$. We show that the susceptibility PT-symmetry is a reduced-response-space manifestation of PT symmetry of the underpinning kinetic system. We identify parity $P$ and time reversal $T$ on the electromagnetic kinetic eigenmode space and show that the Hamiltonian operator $\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}$ of the linearized Vlasov--Maxwell system is PT-symmetric, satisfying $PT\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}(PT)^{-1}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}$. The PT symmetry of the homogeneous magnetized Vlasov--Poisson eigenproblem is also established. Many kinetic instabilities associated with nonmonotonic distributions, rings, loss cones, and resonant energetic-particle populations are fundamentally spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking, which is achieved through and only through Krein collisions. Three examples demonstrate distinct utilities of this analysis. Definite-sign cyclotron residues protect thermal Bernstein modes from spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking. The nonmonotonic Dory--Guest--Harris distribution permits sign-indefinite residues and Krein collisions, for which we derive an analytical instability threshold and growth rate. The recently discovered mode-pole resonance instability provides a distinct realization of spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking and illustrates how PT symmetry can guide the discovery of kinetic instabilities with applications to fusion energy. These results establish PT symmetry as a structural principle of plasma waves and instabilities and open new directions for studying spectral topology in kinetic plasmas.

</details>


### [222] [X-ray spectroscopy of hot dense plasmas using LLNL's upgraded Titan 2$ω$ short-pulse laser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03552)
*A. J. Fairchild,D. T. Bishel,T. Cordova,H. Flores-Alimboyoguen,M. F. Gu,P. Hamilton,N. Hell,M. J. MacDonald,E. W. Magee,R. C. Mancini,E. V. Marley,R. Shepherd,G. V. Brown*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper reports development of an X-ray emission platform at LLNL's Jupiter Laser Facility using the upgraded frequency-doubled Titan laser to benchmark high-density atomic physics effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High electron density strongly affects atomic structure (Stark broadening, ionization potential depression, line shifts, dielectronic recombination suppression), but experimental benchmarks in the high-density, high-temperature regime are scarce, limiting spectral modeling for HED and astrophysics communities.

Method: Uses the upgraded frequency-doubled short-pulse Titan laser to create high-density (n_e > 1e23 cm^-3), high-temperature (T_e ~ 1 keV) plasmas; records calibrated K-shell spectra from five time-integrated X-ray channels and compares with MERL line shape calculations and AWE Orion spectra.

Result: First experiments produced calibrated K-shell spectra; comparisons with MERL calculations and Orion spectra show the upgraded Titan laser reliably produces the required plasma conditions.

Conclusion: The platform is established and validated, providing a foundation for systematic benchmarking of high-density atomic physics in future campaigns.

Abstract: We are developing an experimental platform at Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory's Jupiter Laser Facility to characterize X-ray emission from highly charged ions in the high-density ($n_e > 10^{23}$ cm$^{-3}$), high-temperature ($T_e \sim 1$ keV) regime, leveraging the recently upgraded frequency-doubled short-pulse Titan laser. These measurements will provide experimental benchmarks for high electron density effects on atomic structure, including Stark broadening, ionization potential depression, density-dependent line shifts, and suppression of dielectronic recombination, effects that are relevant to spectral modeling packages used by both the high-energy-density and astrophysics communities. Here, we describe the current status of the platform and present results from first experiments using the upgraded frequency-doubling capability, including calibrated K-shell spectra acquired from five time-integrated X-ray channels. Comparisons with MERL line shape calculations and with spectra recorded at AWE's Orion Laser Facility indicate that the upgraded Titan laser reliably produces the necessary plasma conditions, establishing a foundation for systematic benchmarking of high-density atomic physics in future campaigns.

</details>


### [223] [IGNITE Tokamak World Model Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02515)
*Peter Steiner,Azarakhsh Jalalvand,Nathaniel Chen,Kouroche Bouchiat,Ricardo Shousha,SangKyeun Kim,Egemen Kolemen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: IGNITE is a self-supervised generative world foundation model that simulates fusion plasma behavior, trained on over a decade of unlabeled DIII-D experimental data, enabling whole-discharge simulation from actuator trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fusion plasma experiments like DIII-D generate massive unlabeled data, and there is a need for efficient AI-driven experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion.

Method: IGNITE uses spatio-temporal tokenizers to embed diverse input modalities (time-series spatio-temporal measurements, image sequences, high-resolution spectrograms at different time scales), combined with an auto-regressive dynamics model backbone that predicts entire discharges from initial latent plasma states and actuator trajectories over a theoretical infinite horizon. Trajectories can be supplied or generated on-the-fly from a text prompt or desired outcomes.

Result: The model can simulate entire DIII-D discharges given actuator trajectories and initial latent plasma states, with capacity for infinite-horizon prediction, and can generate trajectories from textual prompts or desired experimental outcomes.

Conclusion: IGNITE paves the way towards efficient AI-driven experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion.

Abstract: We introduce IGNITE, a generative world foundation model for fusion plasma behavior simulation trained in a self-supervised manner from over a decade of unlabeled experimental data at the DIII-D National Fusion Facility. The core of IGNITE is a dynamics model that can simulate DIII-D discharges from a given set of actuator trajectories. These trajectories can be supplied or generated on-the-fly from a textual prompt or from desired experimental outcomes. The model architecture consists of several spatio-temporal tokenizers that embed the different input modalities, including time-series like spatio-temporal measurement data, image sequences, and high-resolution spectrograms, each of which collected at vastly different time scales. The backbone is composed of an auto-regressive dynamics model that has the capacity to predict entire DIII-D discharges given initial latent plasma states and actuator trajectories over a theoretical infinite horizon. IGNITE paves the way towards efficient AI-driven experimental planning and world modeling for nuclear fusion.

</details>


### [224] [Numerical and diagnostic extensions to the gyrokinetic $δf$ code stella](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02958)
*Sven Stroteich,Georgia Acton,Michael Barnes,Pavlos Xanthopoulos,Ralf Schneider*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that free energy is a valuable diagnostic in gyrokinetic stella simulations of W7-X turbulence, revealing cascade power laws, numerical instabilities, and real-space structures, while comparing explicit time-integration schemes (SSP22 vs SSP33) whose efficiency depends on the regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding turbulence in magnetised plasmas requires robust numerical tools capturing complex free-energy transfer across scales, motivating better diagnostics and time-integration guidance for gyrokinetic simulations.

Method: The authors use free energy as a diagnostic in stella gyrokinetic simulations applied to the W7-X high-mirror configuration, and systematically compare explicit time-integration schemes (SSP22, SSP33) across linear and nonlinear regimes.

Result: Free-energy diagnostics reveal characteristic power-law behaviour in the turbulent cascade, detect numerical instabilities missed by conventional diagnostics, and expose localised real-space structures invisible in the electrostatic potential alone. SSP22 is cheapest for linear ITG simulations, while SSP33 is most efficient in the nonlinear saturated state; nonlinear runs cost two to three orders of magnitude more than linear cases.

Conclusion: Free-energy diagnostics are a valuable tool for interpreting plasma turbulence, and the paper provides practical guidance for time-integration scheme selection in stella simulations.

Abstract: Understanding turbulence in magnetised plasmas requires robust numerical tools capable of capturing complex free-energy transfer processes across scales. We demonstrate the utility of free energy as a diagnostic for both physical turbulence characteristics and numerical stability in gyrokinetic simulations with the stella code. Applied to the W7-X high-mirror configuration, free-energy diagnostics reveal characteristic power-law behaviour in the turbulent cascade, act as sensitive indicators of numerical instabilities undetected by conventional diagnostics, and expose localised structures in real space not apparent in the electrostatic potential alone. To support these analyses, we present a systematic comparison of explicit time-integration schemes, showing that the optimal choice depends critically on the regime: SSP22 achieves the lowest computational cost in linear ITG simulations, while SSP33 is most efficient in the nonlinear saturated state, with nonlinear simulations requiring two to three orders of magnitude more computational cost than linear cases. These results establish free-energy diagnostics as a valuable tool for interpreting plasma turbulence and provide practical guidance for scheme selection in stella simulations.

</details>


### [225] [Dynamics-aware bandwidth selection for smoothed charge deposition in 2D electrostatic particle-in-cell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03230)
*Ayushi Agrawal,Shan-Chang Lin,Ezhilmathi Krishnasamy,Pascal Bouvry*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a dynamics-aware bandwidth-selection procedure for charge deposition in PIC methods, combining density-error minimization with constraints on linear mode growth rates. It validates that the selected smoothing bandwidth reduces density and field errors while keeping physical mode growth within a prescribed rate tolerance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charge deposition in PIC can be treated as kernel density estimation, allowing statistically optimal bandwidth selection. However, changing the bandwidth also alters the electric field felt by particles, which can modify the growth or damping of physical modes. Thus a purely statistical criterion may inadvertently perturb the physics being simulated, requiring a dynamics-aware selection approach.

Method: The authors formulate a bandwidth-selection rule that combines a density-error objective with constraints derived from plasma equilibrium and the combined transfer of deposition, filtering, and field gathering, within a model rate-tolerance boundary. They demonstrate the need for equilibrium information via a controlled two-stream experiment, then apply the constrained selection rule to 85 Gaussian candidate kernels with independent snapshot evaluation and a projected-linear growth-rate calculation.

Result: In the controlled two-stream scan across four resolved equilibria approaching marginal stability, the filter reduces the measured growth rate by 0.7% to 9.1%, with equilibrium-dependent interior settings satisfying the 10% rate tolerance. Applying the constrained rule across 85 Gaussian candidates reduces density and grid-field mean-square errors by 7.40% and 3.70% on independent snapshots. The projected-linear calculation gives a mean growth-rate ratio of 0.9556 with 95% interval [0.9453, 0.9640], consistent with the predicted 0.9513.

Conclusion: The paper presents a filter-design procedure that combines statistical error reduction with subsequent validation of the selected linear dynamics. It shows that equilibrium-aware bandwidth selection can improve density/field accuracy while respecting prescribed mode-growth tolerances, with measured rates relating to the response model through analysis of finite-marker loading and ensemble averaging.

Abstract: Charge deposition in the particle-in-cell (PIC) method can be viewed as kernel density estimation on a spatial mesh. This allows the smoothing bandwidth to be selected through statistical criteria such as mean integrated squared error or cross-validation, but it also changes the electric field acting on the particles. Therefore, a selected bandwidth can alter the growth or damping of physical modes. We develop a dynamics-aware bandwidth-selection procedure that combines a density-error objective with constraints on specified linear modes. These constraints follow from the plasma equilibrium and the combined transfer of deposition, filtering and field gathering within the model rate-tolerance boundary.
  We demonstrate the need for equilibrium information through a controlled two-stream experiment. The controlled two-stream scan varies the beam drift while holding the initial spatial density, thermal samples, wavevector and fixed-filter transfer unchanged. Across four resolved equilibria approaching marginal stability, this filter reduces the measured growth rate by $0.7\%$ to $9.1\%$. Equilibrium-dependent interior settings satisfy the prescribed $10\%$ rate tolerance. We then apply the constrained selection rule to 85 Gaussian candidates, that reduces density and grid-field mean-square errors by $7.40\%$ and $3.70\%$ on independently evaluated snapshots. A separate projected-linear calculation at that exact width gives a ratio of mean individual growth rates of $0.9556$, with paired $95\%$ interval $[0.9453,0.9640]$, consistent with the prediction $0.9513$. Analysis of finite-marker loading and ensemble averaging explains how the measured rates relate to the response model. Together, these results demonstrate a filter-design procedure that combines statistical error reduction with subsequent validation of the selected linear dynamics.

</details>


### [226] [Quantum approaches for particle-in-cell codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03254)
*Ryan J. J. Connor,Andrew J. Daley,Callum W. Duncan,Preetma Soin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This work advances tensor-network methods for plasma simulation as an alternative to conventional particle-in-cell codes, and extends the formulation to allow electromagnetic field insertion at arbitrary spatial locations, demonstrated via laser-driven plasma acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving high-dimensional, nonlinear PDEs governing plasma physics is a major computational challenge. Conventional simulations remain costly for industry-relevant scenarios, and tensor-network techniques offer large data compression on classical hardware plus a pathway to quantum computation.

Method: Tensor-network methods are developed and compared against standard particle-in-cell codes. The tensor-network formulation is extended to support insertion of electromagnetic fields at arbitrary spatial locations.

Result: The tensor-network approach is benchmarked against PIC codes, showing capability to overcome bottlenecks of conventional approaches. Field-insertion capability is demonstrated by injecting a laser field to accelerate a plasma.

Conclusion: Tensor-network methods are a promising path for externally driven plasma system simulation, with the arbitrary field-insertion capability representing a key step toward this goal.

Abstract: Solving the high-dimensional, nonlinear partial differential equations that govern the rich physics of plasmas remains a major computational challenge. In fluid dynamics, quantum-inspired methods based on tensor-network techniques have recently emerged as a promising new paradigm, offering large degrees of data compression on conventional hardware and a natural pathway towards solving exponentially large problems on quantum computers. Early studies suggest that tensor-network methods may also help overcome key bottlenecks in computational plasma dynamics, particularly for industry-relevant scenarios where conventional simulations remain costly. In this work, we present recent progress in developing tensor-network methods compared to standard particle-in-cell codes and discuss their capability to overcome the bottlenecks of conventional approaches. We further extend the tensor-network formulation to support the insertion of electromagnetic fields at arbitrary spatial locations. This capability is demonstrated through the injection of a laser field to accelerate a plasma, providing a key step towards tensor-network-based simulation of externally driven plasma systems.

</details>


### [227] [Parity-Time Symmetry of Plasma Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03433)
*Hong Qin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that the susceptibility parity-time (PT) symmetry of a magnetized collisionless plasma is a manifestation of PT symmetry of the underlying kinetic system, and that many kinetic instabilities are spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking via Krein collisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish PT symmetry as a structural principle of plasma waves and instabilities, and to provide a unifying framework for understanding and predicting kinetic instabilities.

Method: Identifies parity P and time reversal T operators on the electromagnetic kinetic eigenmode space; shows the linearized Vlasov-Maxwell Hamiltonian operator is PT-symmetric; establishes PT symmetry of the Vlasov-Poisson eigenproblem; analyzes instabilities through Krein collisions and residue signs.

Result: Definite-sign cyclotron residues protect thermal Bernstein modes; the nonmonotonic Dory-Guest-Harris distribution permits sign-indefinite residues and Krein collisions, yielding an analytical instability threshold and growth rate; the mode-pole resonance instability is shown as a distinct realization of spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking.

Conclusion: PT symmetry is a structural principle of plasma waves and instabilities, opening new directions for studying spectral topology in kinetic plasmas and guiding discovery of instabilities with fusion-energy applications.

Abstract: The susceptibility tensor of a homogeneous collisionless magnetized plasma possesses parity-time (PT) symmetry. For a stationary gyrotropic equilibrium, with the wave vector $\mathbf{k}$ and the equilibrium magnetic field $\mathbf{B}_0$ in the $x$--$z$ plane, the kinetic response satisfies $χ(ω^*,\mathbf{k})=Rχ(ω,\mathbf{k})^*R$, where $R=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,1)$. We show that the susceptibility PT-symmetry is a reduced-response-space manifestation of PT symmetry of the underpinning kinetic system. We identify parity $P$ and time reversal $T$ on the electromagnetic kinetic eigenmode space and show that the Hamiltonian operator $\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}$ of the linearized Vlasov--Maxwell system is PT-symmetric, satisfying $PT\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}(PT)^{-1}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{VM}}$. The PT symmetry of the homogeneous magnetized Vlasov--Poisson eigenproblem is also established. Many kinetic instabilities associated with nonmonotonic distributions, rings, loss cones, and resonant energetic-particle populations are fundamentally spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking, which is achieved through and only through Krein collisions. Three examples demonstrate distinct utilities of this analysis. Definite-sign cyclotron residues protect thermal Bernstein modes from spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking. The nonmonotonic Dory--Guest--Harris distribution permits sign-indefinite residues and Krein collisions, for which we derive an analytical instability threshold and growth rate. The recently discovered mode-pole resonance instability provides a distinct realization of spontaneous PT-symmetry breaking and illustrates how PT symmetry can guide the discovery of kinetic instabilities with applications to fusion energy. These results establish PT symmetry as a structural principle of plasma waves and instabilities and open new directions for studying spectral topology in kinetic plasmas.

</details>


### [228] [X-ray spectroscopy of hot dense plasmas using LLNL's upgraded Titan 2$ω$ short-pulse laser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03552)
*A. J. Fairchild,D. T. Bishel,T. Cordova,H. Flores-Alimboyoguen,M. F. Gu,P. Hamilton,N. Hell,M. J. MacDonald,E. W. Magee,R. C. Mancini,E. V. Marley,R. Shepherd,G. V. Brown*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: An experimental platform at LLNL's Jupiter Laser Facility uses the frequency-doubled Titan laser to benchmark X-ray emission from highly charged ions in high-density, high-temperature plasmas for spectral modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High electron density effects (Stark broadening, ionization potential depression, density-dependent line shifts, suppression of dielectronic recombination) lack experimental benchmarks, which are needed by high-energy-density and astrophysics spectral modeling communities.

Method: Developed an experimental platform at LLNL's Jupiter Laser Facility using the recently upgraded frequency-doubled short-pulse Titan laser; acquired calibrated K-shell spectra from five time-integrated X-ray channels; compared results with MERL line shape calculations and AWE Orion Laser spectra.

Result: Calibrated K-shell spectra were acquired and comparisons with MERL calculations and Orion Laser data indicate the upgraded Titan laser reliably produces the required plasma conditions.

Conclusion: The platform establishes a foundation for systematic benchmarking of high-density atomic physics in future campaigns.

Abstract: We are developing an experimental platform at Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory's Jupiter Laser Facility to characterize X-ray emission from highly charged ions in the high-density ($n_e > 10^{23}$ cm$^{-3}$), high-temperature ($T_e \sim 1$ keV) regime, leveraging the recently upgraded frequency-doubled short-pulse Titan laser. These measurements will provide experimental benchmarks for high electron density effects on atomic structure, including Stark broadening, ionization potential depression, density-dependent line shifts, and suppression of dielectronic recombination, effects that are relevant to spectral modeling packages used by both the high-energy-density and astrophysics communities. Here, we describe the current status of the platform and present results from first experiments using the upgraded frequency-doubling capability, including calibrated K-shell spectra acquired from five time-integrated X-ray channels. Comparisons with MERL line shape calculations and with spectra recorded at AWE's Orion Laser Facility indicate that the upgraded Titan laser reliably produces the necessary plasma conditions, establishing a foundation for systematic benchmarking of high-density atomic physics in future campaigns.

</details>


### [229] [Stellarators Linking Axisymmetric Mirrors Part 1: Coil Design, MHD Equilibrium, and Physics Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06085)
*Rahul Gaur,Djin Patch,Robert Babin,Florian Hindenlang,Xu Chu,Tony Qian*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This study computationally designs a stellarator-mirror hybrid (SLAM) by bridging optimized omnigenous stellarator sections with axisymmetric mirror coils, then uses field-line tracing and MHD/gyrokinetic analysis to verify plasma confinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combining stellarator rotational transform with simple axisymmetric magnetic mirrors could yield a more practical confinement concept, but such hybrids need first-principles evaluation with modern stellarator analysis tools.

Method: Starting from an OP precursor from the DESC omnigenity database, hybrid coil sets for nFP=2 SLAMs were built by splitting OP modular coils along the midplane and inserting axisymmetric planar mirror coils into the gap. Field-line tracing mapped surface shapes; the GVEC fixed-boundary solver computed ideal MHD equilibria and metrics including eps_eff, vacuum magnetic well W, and gyrokinetic heat fluxes Q.

Result: Nested vacuum flux surfaces survive the extrusion across a range of mirror-section lengths, with cross-section qualitatively preserved at the mirror insertion point.

Conclusion: The SLAM hybrid concept is feasible at the coil and equilibrium level, and modern stellarator tools enable first-principles assessment of its confinement and transport properties.

Abstract: We present a computational design study of a stellarator-mirror hybrid concept, Stellarators Linking Axisymmetric Mirrors (SLAMs), in which an optimized poloidally omnigenous (OP or QI) stellarator serves as the rotational-transform-generating element that links long, axisymmetric (circular cross-section) magnetic mirrors. Starting from an OP precursor configuration obtained from the DESC omnigenity database, we construct hybrid coil sets for field period nFP = 2 SLAMs by separating the OP modular coils along the device midplane and inserting axisymmetric planar mirror coils into the resulting gap. Field-line tracing shows that nested vacuum flux surfaces survive this extrusion across a range of mirror-section lengths, with the cross-section qualitatively preserved at the point where the mirror is inserted. We use the field-line tracing data to map the shape of a surface and use the GVEC fixed-boundary solver to calculate an ideal magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) equilibrium and evaluate physics metrics such as the neoclassical transport proxy (eps_eff), the vacuum magnetic well (W), and the gyrokinetic heat fluxes (Q), providing a first-principles analysis of such concepts using modern stellarator analysis tools.

</details>


### [230] [Turbulent Origin of the Edge Radial Electric Field Asymmetry between Favourable and Unfavourable Drift Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06144)
*B. J. Frei,R. Bilato,W. Zholobenko,O. Grover,C. Angioni,M. Bergmann,P. Ulbl,F. Jenko,S. Rienäcker,L. Vermare,P. Hennequin,B. Labit*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses full-f gyrokinetic GENE-X simulations validated on ASDEX Upgrade and TCV to show how ion magnetic-drift direction relative to the X-point sets edge turbulence, E_r, and L-H access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The direction of the ion magnetic drift relative to the active X-point (favourable vs unfavourable configurations) is known to affect confinement and L-H transition access, but the underlying edge turbulence and radial-electric-field physics lacked a first-principles, validated explanation.

Method: Spectral full-f gyrokinetic simulations of the edge and scrape-off layer in diverted X-point geometry with GENE-X, applied to two matched pairs of L-mode discharges in ASDEX Upgrade and TCV, validated against measurements. Radial force-balance analysis, a derived exact mean poloidal-flow acceleration equation from the gyrokinetic vorticity equation, and flux-driven TCV runs at high power.

Result: Simulations match measurements, especially E_r profiles. The deeper E_r well in the favourable configuration comes from larger poloidal ion flow near the separatrix, turbulence-driven and not neoclassical. Asymmetry stems from stronger nonlinear energy transfer into the mean flow (enhanced nonlinear E x B vorticity advection near separatrix/X-point). Persists at >6x L-mode power; predicts ~1.7 unfavourable/favourable L-H power threshold ratio vs experimental ~2. Unfavourable configuration shows larger fluctuations, weaker E_r well, stronger GAMs, shorter energy confinement.

Conclusion: The X-point-relative drift direction asymmetry is explained by turbulence-driven mean poloidal flow, offering a validated first-principles account of differential L-H access.

Abstract: We study the influence of the ion magnetic-drift direction relative to the active X-point (favourable and unfavourable configurations) on edge turbulence, transport, and the radial electric field $E_r$ in ASDEX Upgrade and TCV. First-principles gyrokinetic simulations of the edge and scrape-off layer are performed in diverted X-point geometry using the spectral full-$f$ GENE-X code. Two matched pairs of L-mode discharges are simulated and validated against the available measurements in all four cases, showing good agreement, in particular for the $E_r$ profiles. A radial force-balance analysis shows that the deeper $E_r$ well in the favourable configuration is determined by the larger poloidal ion flow near the separatrix, which cannot be reproduced by neoclassical solvers and is identified in axisymmetric simulations as turbulence-driven. We then derive an exact evolution equation for the mean poloidal-flow acceleration from the full-$f$ gyrokinetic vorticity equation, which identifies the radial gradient of the total velocity stress as its nonlinear drive. The observed asymmetry is found to arise from stronger nonlinear energy transfer into the mean flow in the favourable configuration, associated with enhanced nonlinear $E\times B$ advection of vorticity near the separatrix and X-point. Flux-driven TCV simulations further show that the asymmetry persists at matched power more than six times the L-mode value. Assuming a critical $E_r$ at the L-H transition, this yields an estimated ratio of approximately 1.7 between the unfavourable and favourable L-H power thresholds, close to the experimental factor of two. Overall, the unfavourable configuration exhibits larger fluctuation amplitudes, a weaker $E_r$ well, stronger geodesic acoustic modes, and shorter energy confinement times for the same profiles, which may explain the observed difference in access to high-confinement regimes.

</details>


### [231] [Eliminating Tokamak Disruptions with Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06189)
*H. R. Strauss*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Magnetic feedback can prevent tokamak disruptions caused by tearing modes; a key criterion is that the q=2 rational surface must be close to the resistive wall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokamak disruptions caused by tearing modes are a major problem. Magnetic feedback is proposed as an attractive alternative to disruption mitigation by impurity injection.

Method: A database of DIII-D locked mode disruptions provides criteria for major disruptions. Simulations of a sequence of model equilibria derived from this database are used, and a model of MST is also simulated to test feedback stabilization.

Result: The main criterion is that the q=2 rational surface must be sufficiently close to the resistive wall to interact with it. With an ideally conducting wall, major disruptions do not occur. Magnetic feedback emulates a conducting wall and can prevent disruptions, but feedback coils must be designed appropriately. Simulations of MST also show the q=2 criterion and feedback stabilization.

Conclusion: Magnetic feedback, emulating a conducting wall, can prevent major disruptions if the q=2 surface is close enough to the wall and coils are properly designed, offering an attractive alternative to impurity injection mitigation.

Abstract: Tokamak disruptions caused by tearing modes can be prevented with magnetic feedback. A database of DIII-D locked mode disruptions provides criteria for major disruptions. The main criterion is that the
  q = 2 rational surface must be sufficiently close to the resistive wall surrounding the plasma to interact with it. This is shown in simulations of a sequence of model equilibria derived from the database. If the wall is ideally conducting, major disruptions do not occur. Magnetic feedback emulates a conducting wall, and can prevent major disruptions. Feedback coils must be designed appropriately, otherwise they are not effective in disruption prevention. Simulations of a model of MST also show the q = 2 criterion for major disruptions, as well as feedback stabilization. Feedback offers an attractive alternative to disruption mitigation by impurity injection.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.HE'></div>

# astro-ph.HE [[Back]](#toc)

### [232] [Suppression of spiral density waves in collisionless accretion flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02475)
*Stephen Majeski,Matthew W. Kunz,Jason Dexter*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: This paper models spiral density waves in low-luminosity black-hole accretion flows using coupled kinetic and fluid models in a collisionless shearing sheet, going beyond the standard LTE assumption, and finds that micro-physical instabilities' induced viscosity strongly damps the waves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spiral density waves shape emission-region morphology and variability in optically thin, low-luminosity black-hole accretion flows, but all prior studies assumed local thermodynamic equilibrium (LTE). Real hot, diffuse accretion plasmas deviate far from LTE, so more accurate collisionless modeling is needed.

Method: A combination of kinetic and fluid models is used to describe the propagation, damping, and excitation of spiral density waves in a collisionless shearing sheet, more faithfully representing low-luminosity accretion-flow plasma conditions.

Result: Micro-physical plasma instabilities play a key role in determining spiral density wave behavior; the weak particle scattering they induce causes appreciable viscous damping of the waves.

Conclusion: Non-LTE effects imply reduced emission-region variability (potentially improving agreement between EHT observations and simulation/theory) and lower emission-region pattern speeds than LTE simulations, affecting interpretation of future EHT movies.

Abstract: Spiral density waves are ubiquitous phenomena in differentially rotating flows. They are non-modal, non-axisymmetric perturbations that, due to shear in the background flow, possess time-evolving wavenumbers, frequencies, and amplitudes. In global simulations of optically thin, low-luminosity black-hole accretion flows, the excitation of such waves leads to spiral patterns in the plasma density that influence the morphology of the emission region. As such, the observed variabilities of such accretion flows are expected to be affected by the behavior of spiral density waves. Thus far, investigations of spiral density waves in accretion flows have exclusively used the assumption that the accretion-flow plasmas are in local thermodynamic equilibrium (LTE) at all times. In reality, hot and diffuse accretion-flow plasmas are expected to deviate far from LTE, and should be modeled accordingly. Here, we employ a combination of kinetic and fluid models to describe spiral density wave propagation, damping, and excitation in a collisionless shearing sheet, which more accurately represents the plasma conditions of low-luminosity accretion flows than do models that assume LTE. We find that micro-physical plasma instabilities are especially important in determining how spiral density waves behave, because the weak particle scattering that they induce leads to appreciable viscous damping. Observational implications of these results include reduced emission-region variability, which may improve agreement between Event Horizon Telescope (EHT) observations and simulation/theory, as well as a reduction in emission-region pattern speeds compared to simulations employing LTE, which bears on the interpretation of eventual EHT movies.

</details>


### [233] [Guide field effects on particle acceleration in three-dimensional relativistic magnetic reconnection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03427)
*Despina Karavola,Lorenzo Sironi,Maria Petropoulou*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: 3D PIC simulations show relativistic pair-plasma reconnection's fast particle acceleration channel (absent in 2D) persists for all guide fields studied, but weakens as guide field increases, yielding steeper spectra and lower maximum Lorentz factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relativistic magnetic reconnection is a key particle acceleration mechanism. 3D simulations showed particles accelerate near-maximally by escaping reconnected plasma and gaining energy from the upstream electric field for weak guide fields. It was unclear whether this fast channel survives when the guide field becomes comparable to the reconnecting field.

Method: 3D particle-in-cell simulations of relativistic pair-plasma reconnection across a range of guide field strengths, complemented by 2D runs, using open boundaries along the reconnection outflows to establish a quasi-steady state.

Result: Increasing guide field strength reduces the reconnection rate and aligns energetic particles more closely with the local magnetic field. The fast acceleration channel, absent in 2D, persists in 3D across the full guide field range but involves a progressively smaller particle fraction. Stronger guide fields produce steeper energy spectra and lower maximum Lorentz factors.

Conclusion: The fast acceleration channel is fundamentally 3D and robust, but its efficiency diminishes with increasing guide field strength.

Abstract: Relativistic magnetic reconnection is a key mechanism for particle acceleration in astrophysical plasmas. Recent 3D particle-in-cell simulations have shown that, for weak guide fields, particles can be accelerated at nearly the maximal rate by escaping the reconnected plasma and gaining energy from the large-scale upstream electric field. Here, we investigate whether this fast acceleration channel persists as the guide field becomes comparable to the reconnecting field. We perform 3D simulations of relativistic pair-plasma reconnection, complemented by corresponding 2D runs, using open boundaries along the reconnection outflows to establish a quasi-steady state. We find that increasing the guide field strength reduces the reconnection rate and causes energetic particles to become more closely aligned with the local magnetic field. In 3D, the fast acceleration channel---absent in 2D---persists across the full range of guide fields investigated, but involves a progressively smaller fraction of particles as the guide field increases. Consequently, stronger guide fields produce steeper particle energy spectra and lower maximum Lorentz factors.

</details>


### [234] [Suppression of spiral density waves in collisionless accretion flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02475)
*Stephen Majeski,Matthew W. Kunz,Jason Dexter*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: This paper models spiral density waves in a collisionless shearing sheet using combined kinetic and fluid descriptions, finding that micro-physical plasma instabilities induce strong viscous damping, affecting accretion-flow emission variability and pattern speeds relevant to EHT observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spiral density waves are ubiquitous in differentially rotating flows and shape the emission morphology and variability of optically thin, low-luminosity black-hole accretion flows. Prior studies assumed local thermodynamic equilibrium (LTE), but hot, diffuse accretion plasmas actually deviate far from LTE, so more accurate collisionless modeling is needed.

Method: Combines kinetic and fluid models to describe the propagation, damping, and excitation of spiral density waves in a collisionless shearing sheet, representing low-luminosity accretion-flow plasma conditions more accurately than LTE models.

Result: Micro-physical plasma instabilities are especially important because the weak particle scattering they induce leads to appreciable viscous damping of the spiral density waves. This implies reduced emission-region variability and lower emission-region pattern speeds compared to LTE simulations.

Conclusion: Viscous damping from plasma instabilities lowers emission variability, potentially improving agreement between EHT observations and theory/simulation, and reduces pattern speeds, which matters for interpreting future EHT movies.

Abstract: Spiral density waves are ubiquitous phenomena in differentially rotating flows. They are non-modal, non-axisymmetric perturbations that, due to shear in the background flow, possess time-evolving wavenumbers, frequencies, and amplitudes. In global simulations of optically thin, low-luminosity black-hole accretion flows, the excitation of such waves leads to spiral patterns in the plasma density that influence the morphology of the emission region. As such, the observed variabilities of such accretion flows are expected to be affected by the behavior of spiral density waves. Thus far, investigations of spiral density waves in accretion flows have exclusively used the assumption that the accretion-flow plasmas are in local thermodynamic equilibrium (LTE) at all times. In reality, hot and diffuse accretion-flow plasmas are expected to deviate far from LTE, and should be modeled accordingly. Here, we employ a combination of kinetic and fluid models to describe spiral density wave propagation, damping, and excitation in a collisionless shearing sheet, which more accurately represents the plasma conditions of low-luminosity accretion flows than do models that assume LTE. We find that micro-physical plasma instabilities are especially important in determining how spiral density waves behave, because the weak particle scattering that they induce leads to appreciable viscous damping. Observational implications of these results include reduced emission-region variability, which may improve agreement between Event Horizon Telescope (EHT) observations and simulation/theory, as well as a reduction in emission-region pattern speeds compared to simulations employing LTE, which bears on the interpretation of eventual EHT movies.

</details>


### [235] [Guide field effects on particle acceleration in three-dimensional relativistic magnetic reconnection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03427)
*Despina Karavola,Lorenzo Sironi,Maria Petropoulou*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: This paper studies how the guide field strength affects fast particle acceleration in relativistic magnetic reconnection using 3D particle-in-cell simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent 3D PIC simulations showed particles can be accelerated at nearly maximal rates by escaping reconnected plasma and gaining energy from the upstream electric field for weak guide fields. Whether this fast channel persists for strong guide fields was unknown.

Method: 3D simulations of relativistic pair-plasma reconnection, complemented by 2D runs, using open boundaries along reconnection outflows to establish a quasi-steady state.

Result: Increasing guide field reduces the reconnection rate and aligns energetic particles more closely with the local magnetic field. The fast acceleration channel (absent in 2D) persists across all guide fields in 3D but involves a smaller particle fraction as guide field rises. Stronger guide fields yield steeper energy spectra and lower maximum Lorentz factors.

Conclusion: The fast acceleration channel remains operational in 3D for all guide field strengths, but strong guide fields diminish its efficiency, producing steeper spectra and lower maximum energies.

Abstract: Relativistic magnetic reconnection is a key mechanism for particle acceleration in astrophysical plasmas. Recent 3D particle-in-cell simulations have shown that, for weak guide fields, particles can be accelerated at nearly the maximal rate by escaping the reconnected plasma and gaining energy from the large-scale upstream electric field. Here, we investigate whether this fast acceleration channel persists as the guide field becomes comparable to the reconnecting field. We perform 3D simulations of relativistic pair-plasma reconnection, complemented by corresponding 2D runs, using open boundaries along the reconnection outflows to establish a quasi-steady state. We find that increasing the guide field strength reduces the reconnection rate and causes energetic particles to become more closely aligned with the local magnetic field. In 3D, the fast acceleration channel---absent in 2D---persists across the full range of guide fields investigated, but involves a progressively smaller fraction of particles as the guide field increases. Consequently, stronger guide fields produce steeper particle energy spectra and lower maximum Lorentz factors.

</details>


### [236] [Jets with Streaks: the Global Dynamics and Structure of Striped Poynting flux dominated Jets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04796)
*Ho-Sang Chan,Anna Chashkina,Omer Bromberg,Gregory R. Werner,Hayk Hakobyan*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: First global axisymmetric GRMHD simulations track striped jets from launch (via accretion of alternating-polarity poloidal loops onto a spinning BH) to energy dissipation at large distances, showing efficient dissipation in current sheets between mini-jets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Striped jets—magnetically dominated regions with alternating polarity—can drive fast magnetic dissipation in global current sheets, potentially powering high-energy emission in blazars and GRB prompt emission. A self-consistent global simulation from launch to dissipation was lacking.

Method: Global axisymmetric GRMHD simulations following striped jet evolution: accretion of poloidal magnetic loops with alternating polarity onto a spinning black hole launches Poynting-flux-dominated mini-jets with inherited helical field polarity; these form the relativistic jet core surrounded by a non-relativistic sheath of returning fieldlines.

Result: Efficient mini-jet launching requires poloidal loops forming close to the BH (r ≲ 50 r_g), else turbulence destroys coherency. Mini-jets accelerate electromagnetically, with Lorentz factor rising linearly with cross-sectional radius while lateral causal contact persists. Current sheets form when trailing oppositely-directed mini-jets meet, aided by longitudinal expansion from internal pressure pre-super-FMS. Dissipation rate is a few tens of percent of jet Poynting power at 10^4 r_g.

Conclusion: The results can account for bright superluminal features observed in BL Lac type blazars, validating striped jets as a dissipation mechanism.

Abstract: Striped jets, characterized by magnetically dominated regions with alternating polarity, can enable fast magnetic energy dissipation in global current sheets formed in between the stripes, and are thus considered as a viable source for the high-energy emission observed in blazars and gamma-ray burst prompt emission. We perform the first global axisymmetric GRMHD simulations that follow the evolution of striped jets from their launching, by accretion of poloidal magnetic loops with alternating polarity onto a spinning black-hole, to their energy dissipation at large distances. The accretion of each loop launches a Poynting-flux-dominated mini-jet carrying a helical magnetic field whose polarity is inherited from the loop, and the collection of which forms the jet core. The relativistic core is surrounded by a non-relativistic sheath composed of returning fieldlines from previously launched mini-jets in a nested configuration. Efficient mini-jet launching requires poloidal loops in the accretion disk to form close to the black hole ($r \lesssim 50\,r_g$); otherwise, they lose coherency due to turbulence. Once launched, the mini-jets accelerate electromagnetically, similar to steady-state jets, with the Lorentz factor increasing linearly with the jet cross-sectional radius as long as lateral causal contact with the jet axis is maintained. Current sheets form when two trailing mini-jets carrying oppositely directed magnetic fields come into contact. Early sheet formation is facilitated by the longitudinal expansion of the mini-jets, driven by internal pressure before the flow becomes super-fast-magnetosonic. We measure an energy dissipation rate of a few tens of percent of the jet Poynting power in the observer frame, within our simulation box of $10^4\,r_g$. Our results can account for the bright superluminal features observed in BL Lac type blazars.

</details>


### [237] [Radiation Reaction in Relativistic Magnetized Shocks of Neutron Star Magnetospheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05386)
*Arno Vanthieghem,Amir Levinson*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: This paper studies how radiation reaction affects monster shocks in magnetar magnetospheres and their role in producing fast radio bursts, showing that radiative cooling does not quench precursor emission but shifts its spectral peak to higher frequencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Particles crossing a sufficiently bright magnetar shock radiate most of their energy within one gyration period, raising the question of whether radiation reaction alters the shock structure and suppresses the coherent precursor emission thought to power FRBs.

Method: The authors use a stationary multifluid shock model extended with the Landau-Lifshitz radiation-reaction force, complemented by particle-in-cell simulations of monster-shock dynamics in the strongly cooled, relativistically magnetized regime.

Result: The kinetic-scale shock transition depends on only two dimensionless parameters: the Alfvénic Mach number and a cooling parameter τ (radiative losses per gyration period). Coherent precursor emission is triggered above a critical Mach number that rises with τ, where beam dynamics becomes chaotic. Radiative cooling shifts the precursor peak frequency upward, scaling as τ^{2/7}; monster shocks stay above the emission threshold even under strong cooling.

Conclusion: Radiation reaction does not quench precursor emission but mainly shifts its spectral peak into the kinetic regime. The precursor luminosity is set solely by the driving kHz-wave luminosity and a conversion efficiency of ε_X ~ 10^{-3}–10^{-2}.

Abstract: The steepening of fast magnetosonic waves in the inner magnetospheres of magnetars can produce monster shocks, whose precursor emission may account for fast radio bursts (FRBs). For sufficiently bright bursts, particles crossing the shock radiate their energy within a single gyration period, raising the question of how radiation reaction affects the shock structure and its coherent emission. We investigate strongly cooled, relativistically magnetized shocks using a stationary multifluid model generalized to include the Landau-Lifshitz radiation-reaction force, together with particle-in-cell simulations of monster-shock dynamics. We show that the kinetic-scale shock transition is governed by only two dimensionless parameters: the Alfvénic Mach number and a cooling parameter $τ$ that measures radiative losses over a gyration period. Coherent precursor emission is triggered above a critical Mach number that increases with $τ$, at which the beam dynamics becomes chaotic. Above this threshold, radiative cooling shifts the peak frequency of the precursor to higher values, scaling as $τ^{2/7}$. Remarkably, monster shocks remain far above the emission threshold even under strong radiative cooling. Radiation reaction therefore does not quench the precursor emission; instead, it primarily shifts the spectral peak into the kinetic regime. The luminosity of the precursor wave is determined solely by the luminosity of the driving kHz wave and the conversion efficiency, $ε_X\sim 10^{-3} - 10^{-2}$.

</details>


### [238] [Fast Bayesian Updating of the Neutron-Star Equation of State with Neural Posterior and Evidence Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05121)
*Prashant Thakur*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: Two neural methods (TSNPE+MIS and A-NET+IS) and a Green-function Evidence Network enable fast, accurate neutron-star EOS inference, matching UltraNest while being far faster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microscopic neutron-star EOS inference with full Bayesian methods is computationally expensive, especially when reusing analyses across many nuclear inputs and NICER sources.

Method: TSNPE+MIS (single-observation sequential NPE with mixture importance sampling) and A-NET+IS (amortized NPE with importance sampling) are corrected against the original prior and full likelihood using effective sample size. A Green-function Evidence Network (regression-based Evidence Network variant) computes absolute evidences. Tested on nucleonic and hyperonic RMF models with nuclear data, NICER, GW170817, and pQCD.

Result: Both neural routes agree with UltraNest in posteriors and 90% M-R/tidal bands (band-edge differences 0.024-0.033 km and 1.02-1.03% for TSNPE+MIS; 0.019-0.050 km and 0.86-1.13% for A-NET+IS). TSNPE+MIS is 1.6-2.3x faster; frozen A-NET is 15-240x faster per configuration and generalizes to unseen NICER sources (R1.4 within 0.010/0.009 km of UltraNest). EN queries return evidences in ~0.03 s (nuclear data) and 3.5 s (NICER); all 16 differences below 1σ, and evidence favors nucleonic matter by Bayes factors ~22:1-27:1.

Conclusion: Neural posterior and evidence estimation provide fast, accurate, amortized alternatives to traditional samplers for neutron-star EOS inference, also enabling efficient model comparison.

Abstract: We present two complementary neural routes for microscopic neutron-star EOS inference: truncated sequential neural posterior estimation with mixture importance sampling (TSNPE+MIS), which targets one observation, and amortized neural posterior estimation with importance sampling (A-NET+IS), which is reused across nuclear inputs and NICER sources. Both are corrected against the original prior and full likelihood, with the effective sample size as a diagnostic. We test nucleonic and hyperonic relativistic mean-field models with nuclear data, NICER, GW170817, and pQCD. For the fiducial analyses, both agree with UltraNest in the parameter posteriors and 90% mass-radius and tidal-deformability bands. TSNPE+MIS band-edge differences are only 0.024-0.033 km and 1.02-1.03%, while A-NET+IS differences are 0.019-0.050 km and 0.86-1.13%. TSNPE+MIS reduces the computing time by factors of 1.6-2.3; after training, A-NET+IS is 15-240 times faster per configuration. The frozen A-NET also analyzes three NICER sources absent from training, including the newly reported high-mass, compact PSR J1614-2230 posterior. For this source, the inferred $R_{1.4}$ agrees with UltraNest within 0.010 km for nucleonic matter and 0.009 km for hyperonic matter. We also introduce a Green-function Evidence Network (EN), a regression-based variant of Evidence Networks and, to our knowledge, the first Evidence Network applied to neutron-star EOS inference. After a one-time cost, its network queries return absolute evidences in about 0.03 s for new nuclear data and 3.5 s for a new NICER source; all 16 differences from UltraNest are below $1σ$, and independent importance-sampling evidences agree within the quoted EN uncertainties. Under the adopted likelihood, both evidence calculations favor nucleonic matter by Bayes factors of about 22:1-27:1.

</details>

### [239] [Latent Score-Based Bayesian Cramér-Rao Bound Estimation for High-Dimensional Imaging Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03956)
*Evan Scope Crafts,Thomas Wynn,Seonyeong Park,Mark Anastasio,Umberto Villa*

Main category: stat.ML

TL;DR: A data-driven framework estimates the Bayesian Cramér-Rao bound in high-dimensional imaging by working in a VAE latent space and using a new Bochner-space sliced score matching objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct Bayesian CRB computation is hard in high dimensions with intractable priors, requiring the prior score and inversion of a huge Bayesian Fisher information matrix.

Method: Reformulate the inverse problem in a pre-trained VAE latent space, form the Bayesian CRB there, and map it back via change-of-variables. Learn the latent prior score with a Bochner-space sliced score matching objective using an H^1(Ω) Sobolev norm that penalizes spatial gradient errors.

Result: Validated on a qPACT breast imaging problem with over one million unknowns using a Stable Diffusion foundation-model autoencoder; the method gives stable, artifact-free CRB estimates reflecting the non-Gaussian prior and revealing the prior's impact on competing qPACT designs.

Conclusion: The framework enables tractable, stable Bayesian CRB estimation for high-dimensional imaging with complex priors, capturing non-Gaussian structure and informing imaging system design.

Abstract: We propose a data-driven framework for estimating the Bayesian Cramér-Rao bound (CRB) in high-dimensional imaging systems with complex, analytically intractable priors. Direct CRB computation is challenging in this setting due to the need to model the prior score and to form and invert the Bayesian Fisher information matrix in very high dimensions. To address these issues, we first reformulate the inverse problem in the latent space of a pre-trained variational autoencoder, thereby dramatically reducing the dimensionality of the bound estimation problem while preserving the spatial structure of the images. The Bayesian CRB is formed in this latent space and mapped back to the native parameter space using a change-of-variables formula. Second, to learn the latent prior score, we introduce a new sliced score matching objective defined in a Bochner space endowed with an $H^1(Ω)$ Sobolev norm in space. This "Bochner-space sliced score matching" objective is consistent with standard sliced score matching, but penalizes errors in the spatial gradients of the score, suppressing high-frequency artifacts that otherwise contaminate the resulting CRB estimates. We validate the approach on a stylized quantitative photoacoustic computed tomography (qPACT) breast imaging problem with over one million unknown parameters, using a foundation-model autoencoder derived from Stable Diffusion. The proposed method yields stable, artifact-free Bayesian CRB estimates that reflect the highly non-Gaussian structure of the learned prior and reveal the substantial impact of the prior on the relative performance of competing qPACT design schemes.

</details>


### [240] [On the Sequentially Semiseparable Structure of SDE-Induced Kernels and Efficient Algorithms for Kernel-Based Estimation Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05856)
*Mikkel Paltorp,Luigi Caglio,Tianshi Chen,Evangelos Katsanos,Martin S. Andersen*

Main category: stat.ML

TL;DR: The paper studies kernels induced by LTI SDEs and develops linear-complexity recursive algorithms operating directly on the SSS representation of the covariance matrix for GP regression and kernel-based regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SDE-induced kernels (e.g., Matérn, spline) are important across statistics, ML, and system identification, but existing linear-time GP methods rely on Kalman filtering and RTS smoothing on the latent state; a direct covariance-level treatment is missing.

Method: Embed prior knowledge into the state-space description of an LTI SDE to design kernels; instead of state-space filtering/smoothing, work directly with the SSS (semiseparable) representation of the observed covariance matrix to derive recursions.

Result: Linear-complexity recursive algorithms for factorization, linear solves, log determinants, posterior covariance, log-marginal-likelihood gradients, and posterior sampling; efficient recursions for analytic marginal-likelihood gradients in three cases, plus new recursions for posterior computations and Matheron-type sampling. Experiments confirm accuracy and illustrate inference/sampling.

Conclusion: The framework unifies kernel design and computation via SDE-induced kernels, and the generator-level recursions extend with minor modifications to kernel-based regularization in system identification.

Abstract: We study kernel functions induced by a linear time-invariant (LTI) stochastic differential equation (SDE), an approach that embeds prior knowledge about the function to be estimated directly into the state-space description of the SDE. This kernel-design perspective is relevant across the many settings where kernels play a central role, including kernel methods in statistics, Gaussian process (GP) regression in machine learning, and kernel-based regularization methods in system identification. The resulting class of SDE-induced kernels includes several kernels of practical interest, such as Matérn kernels with half-integer smoothness, spline kernels, and related kernels used in system identification. In contrast to existing linear-time GP regression methods for these kernels, which are typically based on Kalman filtering and Rauch--Tung--Striebel smoothing on the latent state, we work directly with the SSS representation of the observed covariance matrix. This yields linear-complexity recursive algorithms for the matrix operations required in GP regression, including factorization, linear solves, log determinants, posterior covariance, log-marginal-likelihood gradients, and posterior sampling; the same generator-level recursions extend, with only minor modifications, to the analogous computations in kernel-based regularization methods for system identification. Compared with previous uses of SSS representations in GP regression, we derive efficient recursive algorithms for analytic marginal-likelihood gradients in three application-relevant cases, and we develop new recursions for posterior computations and Matheron-type sampling. Numerical experiments verify the accuracy of the proposed recursions and illustrate posterior inference and sampling for representative SDE-induced kernels.

</details>


<div id='cs.SC'></div>

# cs.SC [[Back]](#toc)

### [241] [Root isolation for analytic functions using cubic hermite interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02934)
*Christophe Raffalli*

Main category: cs.SC

TL;DR: The paper presents a heuristic root isolation algorithm for analytic functions combining interval subdivision with a cubic Hermite interpolation test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical root isolation methods are either numerically efficient but unrigorous or rigorous but computationally expensive. The authors seek a fast, practically reliable approach for real root isolation.

Method: The algorithm combines interval subdivision with a heuristic test based on local cubic Hermite interpolation. If the function is sufficiently close to its cubic Hermite interpolant at a few sample points, the critical points of the interpolant are assumed to give valid root isolation of the original function.

Result: The method cannot generally give rigorous certification, but extensive numerical experiments show it is highly efficient and almost always produces correct results.

Conclusion: The approach is a promising heuristic, and the authors hope a probabilistic or exact algorithm can be derived from it.

Abstract: We present a root isolation algorithm for an analytic function. The method combines interval subdivision with a heuristic test based on local cubic Hermite interpolation. Whenever the function is sufficiently close to its cubic Hermite interpolant at a few sample points, we assume that the critical points of the cubic interpolant give a valid root isolation for the original function. Such an algorithm cannot, in general, provide a rigorous certification of the computed intervals. Nevertheless, extensive numerical experiments show that it is highly efficient and almost always produces the correct results. We hope that a probabilistic or exact algorithm can be derived from this work.

</details>


### [242] [Root isolation for analytic functions using cubic hermite interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02934)
*Christophe Raffalli*

Main category: cs.SC

TL;DR: A root isolation algorithm for analytic functions combining interval subdivision with a heuristic test based on local cubic Hermite interpolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing rigorous root isolation methods can be costly; the authors seek a fast heuristic that works well in practice and may later lead to probabilistic or exact algorithms.

Method: Interval subdivision is combined with a heuristic test. When the function is sufficiently close to its cubic Hermite interpolant at a few sample points, the critical points of the cubic interpolant are taken as a valid root isolation for the original function.

Result: The method cannot in general certify the computed intervals rigorously. Extensive numerical experiments show it is highly efficient and almost always produces correct results.

Conclusion: The approach is a fast, reliable-in-practice heuristic for root isolation; the authors hope a probabilistic or exact algorithm can be derived from this work.

Abstract: We present a root isolation algorithm for an analytic function. The method combines interval subdivision with a heuristic test based on local cubic Hermite interpolation. Whenever the function is sufficiently close to its cubic Hermite interpolant at a few sample points, we assume that the critical points of the cubic interpolant give a valid root isolation for the original function. Such an algorithm cannot, in general, provide a rigorous certification of the computed intervals. Nevertheless, extensive numerical experiments show that it is highly efficient and almost always produces the correct results. We hope that a probabilistic or exact algorithm can be derived from this work.

</details>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)
