The results demonstrate that MCoPINN can account for temperature-dependent thermophysical properties in full-field AFSD prediction while requiring significantly less computation than the FVM benchmark.

</details>

### [270] [Linear stability of electroconvection in a viscoelastic electrolyte: polymeric-work pathways and eigenmode selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02474)
*Minyoung Kim*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Viscoelasticity modulates electroconvective instability non-monotonically with Weissenberg number, peaking near Wi ~ O(1), governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EC instability near ion-selective surfaces is key to electrochemical transport beyond diffusion-limited regime. While Newtonian EC instability is well studied, the influence of viscoelasticity on the instability mechanism remains poorly understood.

Method: Linear stability analysis, eigenspectrum analysis, mechanical-work balance (separating electric, solvent-viscous, and polymeric contributions, with polymeric work decomposed into normal-stress-difference and shear-stress pathways), supported by asymptotic pathway ordering.

Result: Non-monotonic dependence on Wi with maximum growth rate and minimum critical voltage near Wi ~ O(1). For Wi << 1, polymer stress recovers Newtonian viscous dissipation. Near Wi ~ 1, phase efficiencies of Newtonian-like polymeric pathways drop, collapsing negative polymeric work and intensifying instability. At intermediate Wi, an extra critical-voltage minimum appears at low wavenumbers due to a slowly propagating mode retaining negative polymeric work. At large Wi, this mode no longer sets the threshold; the low-wavenumber minimum vanishes and normal-stress-difference and shear-stress work nearly cancel, with stronger ESC-localized shear deformation and solvent-viscous dissipation.

Conclusion: The non-monotonic EC instability response is governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection rather than stress magnitude or any single polymeric contribution.

Abstract: Electroconvective (EC) instability near ion-selective surfaces plays a key role in electro-chemical transport beyond the diffusion-limited regime. While EC instability in Newtonian electrolytes has been extensively investigated, the influence of viscoelasticity on the instability mechanism remains less understood. In the present study, we investigate the viscoelastic modulation of EC instability using linear stability, eigenspectrum, and mechanical-work analyses, supported by asymptotic pathway ordering. The analysis reveals a non-monotonic dependence on the Weissenberg number Wi, with the maximum growth rate and minimum critical voltage occurring near Wi ~ O(1). The mechanical-work balance separates electric, solvent-viscous, and polymeric contributions, and further decomposes the polymeric work into normal-stress-difference and shear-stress pathways according to their constitutive origins. For Wi << 1, the polymer stress recovers Newtonian viscous dissipation at leading order. As Wi approaches unity, the phase efficiencies of the Newtonian-like polymeric pathways decrease substantially, causing the negative polymeric work to collapse and the instability to intensify. At intermediate Wi, an additional minimum in the critical voltage appears at low wavenumbers. Eigenspectrum analysis attributes this feature to the selection of a slowly propagating mode that retains substantial negative polymeric work. At large Wi, this mode no longer determines the instability threshold, and the low-wavenumber minimum disappears. The normal-stress-difference and shear-stress work then nearly cancel, accompanied by stronger ESC-localized shear deformation and predominantly solvent-viscous dissipation. Thus, the non-monotonic response is governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection rather than stress magnitude or a single polymeric contribution.

</details>


### [271] [Linear stability of electroconvection in a viscoelastic electrolyte: polymeric-work pathways and eigenmode selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02474)
*Minyoung Kim*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Viscoelasticity modulates electroconvective instability non-monotonically with Weissenberg number, peaking near Wi ~ O(1), governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EC instability near ion-selective surfaces is key for electrochemical transport beyond diffusion-limited regime. Prior work focuses on Newtonian electrolytes; the effect of viscoelasticity on the instability mechanism is poorly understood.

Method: Linear stability analysis, eigenspectrum analysis, mechanical-work balance (separating electric, solvent-viscous, polymeric contributions; decomposing polymeric work into normal-stress-difference and shear-stress pathways), supported by asymptotic pathway ordering.

Result: Non-monotonic dependence on Weissenberg number Wi, with maximum growth rate and minimum critical voltage near Wi ~ O(1). For Wi << 1, polymer stress recovers Newtonian viscous dissipation. Near Wi ~ 1, phase efficiencies of Newtonian-like polymeric pathways drop, negative polymeric work collapses, intensifying instability. At intermediate Wi, an extra low-wavenumber minimum in critical voltage arises from a slowly propagating mode with substantial negative polymeric work. At large Wi, this mode no longer sets the threshold, low-wavenumber minimum disappears, normal-stress-difference and shear-stress work nearly cancel, with stronger ESC-localized shear and solvent-viscous dissipation.

Conclusion: The non-monotonic viscoelastic response is governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection rather than stress magnitude or a single polymeric contribution.

Abstract: Electroconvective (EC) instability near ion-selective surfaces plays a key role in electro-chemical transport beyond the diffusion-limited regime. While EC instability in Newtonian electrolytes has been extensively investigated, the influence of viscoelasticity on the instability mechanism remains less understood. In the present study, we investigate the viscoelastic modulation of EC instability using linear stability, eigenspectrum, and mechanical-work analyses, supported by asymptotic pathway ordering. The analysis reveals a non-monotonic dependence on the Weissenberg number Wi, with the maximum growth rate and minimum critical voltage occurring near Wi ~ O(1). The mechanical-work balance separates electric, solvent-viscous, and polymeric contributions, and further decomposes the polymeric work into normal-stress-difference and shear-stress pathways according to their constitutive origins. For Wi << 1, the polymer stress recovers Newtonian viscous dissipation at leading order. As Wi approaches unity, the phase efficiencies of the Newtonian-like polymeric pathways decrease substantially, causing the negative polymeric work to collapse and the instability to intensify. At intermediate Wi, an additional minimum in the critical voltage appears at low wavenumbers. Eigenspectrum analysis attributes this feature to the selection of a slowly propagating mode that retains substantial negative polymeric work. At large Wi, this mode no longer determines the instability threshold, and the low-wavenumber minimum disappears. The normal-stress-difference and shear-stress work then nearly cancel, accompanied by stronger ESC-localized shear deformation and predominantly solvent-viscous dissipation. Thus, the non-monotonic response is governed by stress-deformation phase relations, solvent dissipation, and eigenmode selection rather than stress magnitude or a single polymeric contribution.

</details>


### [272] [Landau damping and mode-selective instability of a vortex gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04841)
*H. Terças,J. L. Figueiredo,J. T,Mendonça*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Collisionless point-vortex gas is shown to behave as a genuine Vlasov system, yielding Landau damping, mode selection, and a beam-plasma-like instability for vortices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a kinetic/Vlasov framework for collective vortex dynamics directly on the physical plane, avoiding auxiliary momentum variables and connecting vortex flows to known plasma and self-gravitating kinetic phenomena.

Method: Start from the non-canonical Hamiltonian structure of point-vortex dynamics; derive a reduced Klimontovich equation and corresponding Vlasov description on the physical plane; obtain a dielectric response operator to analyze collective modes, resonances, and instabilities.

Result: Predicts vortex Rossby-like waves, global oscillations, resonant critical layers, and a plasma-like dielectric response. Differential rotation causes vortex Landau damping (energy/angular momentum transfer to resonant annuli without collisions) and strong mode selection: for a Gaussian vortex cloud only one quadrupolar quasi-mode survives, higher multipoles phase-mix away. A vortex beam-plasma instability occurs: a thin ring orbiting an Onsager patch excites Kelvin modes with azimuthal symmetry set by ring population.

Conclusion: Kinetic theory is a natural framework for collective vortex dynamics, suggesting new mechanisms for vortex transport, vortex avalanches, and rotational glitches in neutron-star superfluids.

Abstract: We show that a collisionless point-vortex gas behaves as a genuine Vlasov system. Starting from the non-canonical Hamiltonian structure of point-vortex dynamics, we derive a reduced Klimontovich equation and the corresponding Vlasov description directly on the physical plane, without introducing auxiliary momentum variables. The theory predicts a rich spectrum of collective phenomena, including vortex Rossby-like waves, global oscillations, resonant critical layers, and a dielectric response operator analogous to that of plasmas and self-gravitating systems. For smooth vortex distributions, differential rotation gives rise to a vortex analogue of Landau damping, whereby collective modes transfer energy and angular momentum to resonant vortex annuli in the absence of collisions or dissipation. Remarkably, differential rotation acts as a stringent mode-selection mechanism: for a Gaussian vortex cloud, only a single quadrupolar quasi-mode survives, while all higher multipoles are destroyed by phase mixing. We further predict a vortex counterpart of the beam--plasma instability, in which a thin vortex ring orbiting an Onsager patch selectively excites Kelvin modes with an azimuthal symmetry controlled by the ring population. These results establish kinetic theory as a natural framework for collective vortex dynamics and suggest new mechanisms for vortex transport, vortex avalanches, and rotational glitches in neutron-star superfluids.

</details>


### [273] [Accurate contact discontinuity resolving Boltzmann schemes based on peculiar velocity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06649)
*Balwinder Singh,S. V. Raghurama Rao*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper proposes new variants of the peculiar velocity based upwind (PVU) Boltzmann scheme that capture contact discontinuities exactly by redesigning the peculiar velocity discretization to make numerical diffusion depend only on bulk velocity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original PVU Boltzmann scheme is robust but fails to recognize contact discontinuities. Its numerical diffusion coefficient for the peculiar velocity part does not vanish as Mach number goes to zero, causing excessive smearing at discontinuities.

Method: A modified partial differential equation (MPDE) analysis reveals the diffusion issue in the original scheme. The numerical discretization of the peculiar velocity term is then redesigned so numerical diffusion depends only on bulk fluid velocity, enabling exact capture of stationary contact discontinuities in 1D and slip surfaces in 2D.

Result: The new variants achieve low numerical diffusion while preserving the robustness of the original scheme, verified on several 1D and 2D benchmark compressible flow test cases including exact capture of stationary contacts and slip surfaces.

Conclusion: Redesigning the peculiar velocity discretization yields low-diffusion, robust PVU variants that accurately capture contact discontinuities in gas dynamics simulations.

Abstract: Starting from the robust foundation of the peculiar velocity based upwind (PVU) Boltzmann scheme, which fails to recognize contact discontinuities, this paper introduces new variants for the numerical solutions of equations of gas dynamics. A modified partial differential equation (MPDE) analysis of the original scheme reveals that the numerical diffusion coefficient corresponding to the peculiar velocity part does not vanish as the Mach number goes to zero. The numerical discretization of the peculiar velocity term is redesigned so that the numerical diffusion depends only on the bulk fluid velocity across the stationary contact discontinuities, enabling its exact capture in 1D and exact capture of slip surfaces in 2D. The resulting variants achieve low numerical diffusion while preserving the robustness of the original scheme, as demonstrated through several 1D and 2D benchmark compressible flow test cases.

</details>

# cond-mat.soft [[Back]](#toc)

### [274] [Efficient, Geometry-only Prediction of Advection-dominated Particle Transport through Saturated Soils](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03767)
*Hao Liu,Rizwan Khaleel,Markus Rolf,Martin G. J. Löder,Christina Bogner,Stephan Gekle*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: minD-PNM is an efficient pore-network model that predicts microplastic breakthrough curves in water-saturated soils using μCT pore geometries as sole input, validated against lattice Boltzmann/immersed boundary simulations and column experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding microplastic transport in soils is important due to ecological and human-health impacts, but soil's complex, non-transparent porous structure limits experimental knowledge, and direct particle-resolved simulations remain computationally infeasible for realistic macroscopic soil samples.

Method: The authors introduce minD-PNM, which uses experimental X-ray micro-computed tomography (μCT) pore geometries as its only input. It combines an approximately t^-3 pore-scale transit time distribution with a minimum-dissipation partition of multi-inlet-multi-outlet pores, propagating these distributions through the pore network to predict breakthrough curves.

Result: Benchmarking against lattice Boltzmann and immersed boundary simulations for small systems and against microplastic column experiments in quartz sediments for macroscopic sizes showed good agreement in predicted breakthrough curves across both cases.

Conclusion: Because minD-PNM requires only geometric information, it scales to macroscopic sample volumes, offering a practical tool for contaminant-hydrology studies of particulate pollutants.

Abstract: The environmental transport of microplastic particles has raised concerns because of their potential ecological and human-health impacts. A particular challenge for experiments on microplastic transport in soils is the fact that soil is a highly complex and non-transparent porous medium. Thus, our knowledge of transport mechanisms of microplastics in soils and related ecological implications is still limited and computational simulations offer an additional possibility to obtain important insights into the physical transport behaviour of microplastics. Yet, direct particle-resolved simulations in realistic soil structures for macroscopic sample sizes remain a computational challenge. We introduce an efficient pore-network model, minD-PNM, that predicts particle breakthrough curves in water-saturated soils using experimental X-ray micro-computed tomography ($μ$CT) pore geometries as the only input. The model combines an approximately $t^{-3}$ pore-scale transit time distribution with a minimum-dissipation partition of multi-inlet-multi-outlet pores, and propagates these distributions through the pore network to predict breakthrough curves. We benchmark minD-PNM against lattice Boltzmann and immersed boundary simulations for small systems, and against microplastic column experiments in quartz sediments for macroscopic sample sizes. In both cases, the predicted breakthrough curves agree well with the corresponding reference data. Because it requires only geometric information, minD-PNM scales to macroscopic sample volumes offering a practical tool for contaminant-hydrology studies of particulate pollutants.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [275] [Ground-state properties of the two-dimensional SWAP spin-glass ensemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05046)
*Alexander K. Hartmann,Leticia F. Cugliandolo,Marco Tarzia*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper studies a SWAP ensemble for Ising spin-glasses with variable spin lengths, proving SWAP annealing finds ground states in 2D systems up to 1024² spins for small length scales, while large Δ drives ferromagnetic behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the SWAP algorithm (originally for polydisperse hard spheres) to Ising spin-glasses with variable-length spins, and to test whether SWAP annealing can reliably find ground states and how the length scale Δ affects the glassy nature of disorder realizations.

Method: Introduce SWAP ensemble where spin lengths (scale Δ∈[0,2]) are exchanged like the SWAP algorithm; use slow annealing to find low-energy states; verify against exact ground states from graph matching algorithms; analyze zero-temperature domain-wall energies via exact ground states with periodic and anti-periodic boundary conditions.

Result: SWAP annealing finds true ground states with high probability for sufficiently slow schedules in 2D systems up to L²=1024² spins. For medium/large Δ (e.g., Δ=1), realizations become ferromagnetic and lose spin-glass property. For small Δ, realizations stay glassy but SWAP annealing struggles to find true ground states.

Conclusion: SWAP annealing reliably solves the variable-length Ising spin-glass ground state in 2D when annealing is slow, but the disorder's glassy character depends strongly on Δ: large Δ induces ferromagnetic ordering, small Δ retains glassiness at the cost of harder optimization.

Abstract: We study the recently introduced SWAP ensemble for Ising spin-glasses, where the spins obtain varying lengths, with length scale $Δ\in [0, 2]$. The lengths can be exchanged in the spirit of the SWAP algorithm for glassy poly-disperse hard-sphere systems. Using an annealing schedule, if the annealing is slow enough, ground states of the corresponding Ising Hamiltonian, where the spin lengths are incorporated into the bonds, can be obtained with high probability. We prove this statement for two-dimensional systems with sizes of up to $L^2=1024^2$ spins by comparison with exact ground states obtained by using graph matching algorithms. In particular, we analyze the nature of the obtained realizations of the disorder by calculating exact zero-temperature domain-wall energies, from exact ground state calculations using periodic and anti-periodic boundary conditions. For medium or large values of $Δ$, e.g. $Δ=1$, the realizations turn ferromagnetic if the annealing is slow enough, i.e., they lose their zero-temperature spin-glass property. If $Δ$ is small, the realizations remain glassy, but the SWAP annealing does not easily find a true ground state.

</details>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [276] [Explainable Molecular Structure Inference from GC--MS with Diffusion Models and LLM Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03066)
*Changlin Liu,Tianyu Yi,Chengchun Liu,Boxuan Zhao,Fanyang Mo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model, plus an LLM-based second-stage reasoning framework, enables de novo molecular structure elucidation from GC-EI-MS, even for compounds absent from reference libraries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional GC-EI-MS analysis relies on reference spectral library matching, which cannot identify compounds absent from libraries or infer full molecular structures from fragmentation data.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model, generates candidate molecular structures from input spectra; (2) an LLM validates, repairs, and reranks candidates using mass spectral information and provides interpretable fragment-ion peak analysis.

Result: On 13,696 NIST 20 test spectra, Acc@1 and Acc@10 reached 6.01% and 15.76%. On the ≤10 heavy-atom subset, LLM-assisted repair and reranking raised Acc@1 from 21.28% to 21.95%, Acc@10 from 46.91% to 47.99%, and candidate validity from 91.04% to 100%.

Conclusion: Spectrum-aware LLM postprocessing can correct generative model errors while providing auditable, traceable explanations, enabling structure elucidation for library-absent compounds.

Abstract: GC--EI--MS is an important technique for analyzing volatile and semivolatile compounds in complex samples. However, conventional methods rely heavily on reference spectral library matching, limiting their ability to identify compounds absent from these libraries and to infer complete molecular structures directly from fragmentation information. Here, we present DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model for de novo structure elucidation from GC--EI--MS, and further develop a framework that integrates DiffGCMS with second-stage reasoning by a large language model (LLM). In the first stage, DiffGCMS generates candidate molecular structures from input spectra; in the second stage, the LLM uses mass spectral information to validate, repair, and rerank the candidates and provides interpretable analysis of fragment-ion peaks. This framework can generate plausible molecular structures for compounds absent from reference spectral libraries and provide traceable evidence supporting its decisions. On a test set comprising 13,696 spectra from NIST 20, the generative model achieved Acc@1 and Acc@10 of 6.01\% and 15.76\%, respectively. On the test subset containing molecules with no more than 10 heavy atoms, LLM-assisted molecular graph repair and reranking increased Acc@1 from 21.28\% to 21.95\%, Acc@10 from 46.91\% to 47.99\%, and candidate validity from 91.04\% to 100\%. These results demonstrate that spectrum-aware postprocessing can correct errors produced by the generative model while providing auditable and traceable explanations for the final ranking.

</details>


### [277] [Preparation sequence controls oxygen partitioning between boron and lithium on tungsten](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02905)
*Predrag S Krstic,Meral Sharkass,Swarit Dwivedi,Dalia Sayed Ahmed,Yun Kyung Shin,Adri CT van Duin*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Reactive MD shows that the sequence of boron and oxygen introduction, not boron inventory, governs tungsten surface chemistry; lithium only alters the co-deposited case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how boron and lithium conditioning of tungsten surfaces controls oxygen capture and surface chemistry, guiding plasma-facing material design.

Method: Classical reactive molecular dynamics simulations of boron- and lithium-conditioned tungsten surfaces: thin engineered tungsten oxide, ~2 nm mixed WO3/WO2 film, and co-deposited W-B substrate later exposed to oxygen, with and without lithium.

Result: On thin tungsten oxide, boron captures 81% of oxygen and consumes the oxide. On mixed WO3/WO2 film, boron captures only 20%, leaving 52% of reaction-zone tungsten in WO3-like coordination. In co-deposited W-B later exposed to oxygen, boron captures only 28% despite tenfold larger inventory. Lithium leaves oxide-first cases unchanged but dominates in the co-deposited case (58% oxygen capture) forming a lithium-borate-glass-like network.

Conclusion: Introduction order, not boron inventory, controls surface chemistry; lithium selectively transforms the co-deposited case. Divergent responses to deuterium irradiation are predicted for boron-only vs boron-plus-lithium materials.

Abstract: Classical reactive molecular dynamics of boron- and lithium-conditioned tungsten surfaces show that the order in which boron and oxygen are introduced - not the boron inventory - controls surface chemistry. On a thin engineered tungsten oxide, boron captures 81% of oxygen and consumes the oxide; on an engineered ~2 nm mixed WO3/WO2 film, boron captures only 20% and leaves 52% of reaction-zone tungsten in WO3-like coordination; and in a co-deposited W-B substrate later exposed to oxygen, boron captures only 28% despite a tenfold larger inventory. Adding lithium leaves the oxide-first cases unchanged but transforms the co-deposited case, where lithium becomes the dominant oxygen captor (58%) and a lithium-borate-glass-like network form. We outline the expected, divergent responses of the boron-only and boron-plus-lithium materials to deuterium irradiation.

</details>


### [278] [Explainable Molecular Structure Inference from GC--MS with Diffusion Models and LLM Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03066)
*Changlin Liu,Tianyu Yi,Chengchun Liu,Boxuan Zhao,Fanyang Mo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model, performs de novo molecular structure elucidation from GC-EI-MS, enhanced by an LLM second stage for validation, repair, and reranking of candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional GC-EI-MS analysis relies heavily on reference spectral library matching, which limits identification of compounds absent from libraries and prevents direct inference of complete molecular structures from fragmentation data.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model generating candidate molecular structures from input spectra; (2) an LLM stage that validates, repairs, and reranks candidates using mass spectral information and provides interpretable fragment-ion peak analysis.

Result: On 13,696 NIST 20 spectra, the generative model achieved Acc@1 of 6.01% and Acc@10 of 15.76%. On the ≤10 heavy-atom subset, LLM-assisted repair and reranking raised Acc@1 from 21.28% to 21.95%, Acc@10 from 46.91% to 47.99%, and candidate validity from 91.04% to 100%.

Conclusion: Spectrum-aware postprocessing can correct generative model errors while providing auditable, traceable explanations for final rankings, enabling structure elucidation for compounds absent from reference libraries.

Abstract: GC--EI--MS is an important technique for analyzing volatile and semivolatile compounds in complex samples. However, conventional methods rely heavily on reference spectral library matching, limiting their ability to identify compounds absent from these libraries and to infer complete molecular structures directly from fragmentation information. Here, we present DiffGCMS, a spectrum-conditioned discrete graph diffusion model for de novo structure elucidation from GC--EI--MS, and further develop a framework that integrates DiffGCMS with second-stage reasoning by a large language model (LLM). In the first stage, DiffGCMS generates candidate molecular structures from input spectra; in the second stage, the LLM uses mass spectral information to validate, repair, and rerank the candidates and provides interpretable analysis of fragment-ion peaks. This framework can generate plausible molecular structures for compounds absent from reference spectral libraries and provide traceable evidence supporting its decisions. On a test set comprising 13,696 spectra from NIST 20, the generative model achieved Acc@1 and Acc@10 of 6.01\% and 15.76\%, respectively. On the test subset containing molecules with no more than 10 heavy atoms, LLM-assisted molecular graph repair and reranking increased Acc@1 from 21.28\% to 21.95\%, Acc@10 from 46.91\% to 47.99\%, and candidate validity from 91.04\% to 100\%. These results demonstrate that spectrum-aware postprocessing can correct errors produced by the generative model while providing auditable and traceable explanations for the final ranking.

</details>


### [279] [Preparation sequence controls oxygen partitioning between boron and lithium on tungsten](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02905)
*Predrag S Krstic,Meral Sharkass,Swarit Dwivedi,Dalia Sayed Ahmed,Yun Kyung Shin,Adri CT van Duin*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Reactive MD shows the sequence of boron and oxygen introduction, rather than boron inventory, governs tungsten surface chemistry; adding lithium only alters the co-deposited case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how boron and lithium condition tungsten surfaces is important for controlling oxygen capture and surface chemistry, with implications for plasma-facing materials under deuterium irradiation.

Method: Classical reactive molecular dynamics simulations of boron- and lithium-conditioned tungsten surfaces, comparing thin engineered tungsten oxide, a ~2 nm mixed WO3/WO2 film, and a co-deposited W-B substrate later exposed to oxygen.

Result: On the thin oxide, boron captures 81% of oxygen and consumes the oxide; on the mixed WO3/WO2 film, boron captures only 20% and leaves 52% of reaction-zone tungsten in WO3-like coordination; in the co-deposited W-B case, boron captures only 28% despite tenfold larger inventory. Lithium leaves oxide-first cases unchanged but dominates oxygen capture (58%) in the co-deposited case, forming a lithium-borate-glass-like network.

Conclusion: The order of boron and oxygen introduction controls surface chemistry, not boron inventory; lithium selectively transforms the co-deposited case, and divergent responses to deuterium irradiation are predicted.

Abstract: Classical reactive molecular dynamics of boron- and lithium-conditioned tungsten surfaces show that the order in which boron and oxygen are introduced - not the boron inventory - controls surface chemistry. On a thin engineered tungsten oxide, boron captures 81% of oxygen and consumes the oxide; on an engineered ~2 nm mixed WO3/WO2 film, boron captures only 20% and leaves 52% of reaction-zone tungsten in WO3-like coordination; and in a co-deposited W-B substrate later exposed to oxygen, boron captures only 28% despite a tenfold larger inventory. Adding lithium leaves the oxide-first cases unchanged but transforms the co-deposited case, where lithium becomes the dominant oxygen captor (58%) and a lithium-borate-glass-like network form. We outline the expected, divergent responses of the boron-only and boron-plus-lithium materials to deuterium irradiation.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [280] [Approximation Property of Dropout Neural Networks: Sobolev Rates and Confidence Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02253)
*Jia-He Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Studies approximation of the unit ball of W^{n,∞}([0,1]^d) by constant-depth ReLU dropout networks (edges kept i.i.d. with probability p), giving size bounds and matching lower bounds in the accuracy exponent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Universal approximation for dropout networks does not specify the network size needed for an accurate random realization, so size-dependent guarantees (not just existence) are required.

Method: Constructs networks of constant depth and size Õ_{n,d}(p^{-9} ε^{-max{d/n,2}} log(1/δ)) via bounded local subnetworks, localization on a successful approximation event, and a multiscale Taylor decomposition; derives lower bounds from Sobolev capacity (surviving edges) and an output-layer cost ((1-p)/p) ε^{-2} log(1/δ) for a fixed affine function.

Result: Uniform approximation over the domain holds with probability ≥1-δ for a single sampled network. For fixed p∈(0,1), δ<min{1/2,1-p}, upper and lower bounds match in the accuracy exponent under fixed or logarithmic depth; when d≤2n they also match in confidence up to log factors. Lower bounds extend to W^{n,r} targets with L^s error, distinguished from the W^{n,∞} upper bound.

Conclusion: Dropout network size for uniform Sobolev approximation is characterized up to the accuracy exponent (and confidence when d≤2n); optimal retention dependence and logarithmic factors remain open.

Abstract: The universal approximation property of dropout neural networks does not by itself describe the network size required for an accurate random realization. In this work, we study approximation of the unit ball of $W^{n,\infty}([0,1]^d)$ by ReLU networks whose edges are retained independently with probability $p$. The approximation error is measured uniformly over the input domain, and the guarantee holds with probability at least $1-δ$ for a single sampled network. We construct networks of constant depth and size $\widetilde O_{n,d}(p^{-9}\varepsilon^{-\max\{d/n,2\}} \log(1/δ))$. The construction combines bounded local subnetworks, localization on a successful approximation event, and a multiscale Taylor decomposition. Conversely, Sobolev capacity imposes a lower bound on the number of surviving edges, while approximation of a fixed affine function requires an output-layer cost of order $((1-p)/p)\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/δ)$ at sufficiently high confidence. For fixed $p\in(0,1)$ and $δ<\min\{1/2,1-p\}$, the upper and lower bounds match in the accuracy exponent under a fixed or logarithmic depth budget. When $d\leq2n$, they also match in confidence up to logarithms of accuracy. We extend the lower bounds to $W^{n,r}$ targets with $L^s$ error, and distinguish this extension from the upper bound for $W^{n,\infty}$. The optimal retention dependence and logarithmic factors remain open.

</details>


### [281] [Approximation Property of Dropout Neural Networks: Sobolev Rates and Confidence Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02253)
*Jia-He Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Studies approximation of the unit ball of $W^{n,\infty}([0,1]^d)$ by randomly-sparsified (dropout) ReLU networks, giving high-probability upper and lower bounds on size that nearly match in the accuracy exponent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Universal approximation only guarantees existence; it does not quantify the network size needed for an accurate single random realization of a dropout network. This work aims to characterize the size required for uniform approximation guarantees that hold with high probability for one sampled network.

Method: Constructs constant-depth ReLU networks where edges are retained independently with probability $p$, combining bounded local subnetworks, localization on a successful approximation event, and a multiscale Taylor decomposition. Lower bounds are derived via Sobolev capacity and output-layer cost analysis for affine functions.

Result: Upper bound size $\widetilde O_{n,d}(p^{-9}\varepsilon^{-\max\{d/n,2\}}\log(1/δ))$ with probability $\ge 1-δ$. Lower bounds: Sobolev capacity bounds surviving edges; approximating a fixed affine function needs output-layer cost $\Theta(((1-p)/p)\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/δ))$. Upper and lower bounds match in accuracy exponent for fixed $p\in(0,1)$, $δ<\min\{1/2,1-p\}$, under fixed or logarithmic depth. For $d\le 2n$, they match in confidence up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: Dropout network size for high-probability uniform Sobolev approximation is nearly characterized in the accuracy exponent. Optimal retention ($p$) dependence and logarithmic factors remain open. Lower bounds extend to $W^{n,r}$ targets with $L^s$ error.

Abstract: The universal approximation property of dropout neural networks does not by itself describe the network size required for an accurate random realization. In this work, we study approximation of the unit ball of $W^{n,\infty}([0,1]^d)$ by ReLU networks whose edges are retained independently with probability $p$. The approximation error is measured uniformly over the input domain, and the guarantee holds with probability at least $1-δ$ for a single sampled network. We construct networks of constant depth and size $\widetilde O_{n,d}(p^{-9}\varepsilon^{-\max\{d/n,2\}} \log(1/δ))$. The construction combines bounded local subnetworks, localization on a successful approximation event, and a multiscale Taylor decomposition. Conversely, Sobolev capacity imposes a lower bound on the number of surviving edges, while approximation of a fixed affine function requires an output-layer cost of order $((1-p)/p)\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/δ)$ at sufficiently high confidence. For fixed $p\in(0,1)$ and $δ<\min\{1/2,1-p\}$, the upper and lower bounds match in the accuracy exponent under a fixed or logarithmic depth budget. When $d\leq2n$, they also match in confidence up to logarithms of accuracy. We extend the lower bounds to $W^{n,r}$ targets with $L^s$ error, and distinguish this extension from the upper bound for $W^{n,\infty}$. The optimal retention dependence and logarithmic factors remain open.

</details>


### [282] [Physics-Augmented Graph Transformers for Patch-Antenna Forward and Inverse Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05004)
*Avi Epstein,Snir Nehemia,Haim Suchowski,Lior Wolf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A mesh-native, physics-augmented graph-learning framework predicts patch-antenna radiation patterns on irregular surface meshes, using physics-augmented intermediate supervision (PAIS) of surface currents, achieving strong forward accuracy and improved inverse design via diffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-wave EM simulation is accurate but computationally expensive for large-scale forward prediction and inverse design of patch antennas, motivating a fast learned surrogate.

Method: Treats radiation-pattern prediction as signal reconstruction on an irregular surface mesh. A GPS graph transformer is trained with PAIS, an auxiliary node-level objective predicting complex surface currents. Direction-conditioned decoding and a differentiable radiation-integral consistency loss further exploit physical structure.

Result: On an 80,000-sample CST benchmark, GPS+PAIS reaches MSE 0.17 / PSNR 19.67, generalizes to a PCA split, and transfers zero-shot to canonical patches. For inverse design, surrogate-filtered diffusion beats nearest-neighbor retrieval by 32% relative MSE.

Conclusion: Physics-augmented intermediate supervision improves multiple GNN backbones at no inference cost, with controls confirming gains stem from physical correspondence, yielding an effective EM surrogate for forward and inverse patch-antenna design.

Abstract: Full-wave electromagnetic (EM) simulation enables accurate patch-antenna analysis but is computationally expensive for large-scale forward prediction and inverse design. We present a mesh-native, physics-augmented graph-learning framework that treats radiation-pattern prediction as signal reconstruction on an irregular surface mesh. For the forward problem, a GPS graph transformer is trained with Physics-Augmented Intermediate Supervision (PAIS), an auxiliary node-level objective that predicts complex surface currents, the physical intermediate linking geometry to radiation. PAIS improves multiple GNN backbones at no inference-time cost, while shuffled-current and non-physical controls show the gain comes from physical correspondence. Direction-conditioned decoding and a differentiable radiation-integral consistency loss further exploit this structure. On an 80,000-sample CST benchmark, GPS+PAIS reaches MSE 0.17 / PSNR 19.67, generalizes to a PCA split, and transfers zero-shot to canonical patches. For inverse design, surrogate-filtered diffusion beats nearest-neighbor retrieval by 32% relative MSE.

</details>


### [283] [On architectural choices for interpretability and thermodynamic consistency in Physically Recurrent Neural Networks in the low-data regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04067)
*M. A. Maia,K. A. Meyer,A. M. C. M. van Gils,I. B. C. M. Rocha,F. P. van der Meer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how decoder architectures affect latent space interpretability in Physically Recurrent Neural Networks, introducing a weight normalization constraint for low-data training and an adjoint encoder-decoder design that guarantees thermodynamic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability of the latent space in Physically Recurrent Neural Networks depends on decoder architectural choices, yet these effects are not well understood. The work aims to improve data efficiency and embed physical/thermodynamic consistency through principled architecture design.

Method: The authors analyze different decoder architectures and introduce a new weight normalization constraint acting as regularization for robust training in low-data regimes. They enforce an adjoint encoder-decoder structure with positive scalar contributions, and adopt a multi-task approach requiring no extra parameters or larger training sets.

Result: The weight normalization constraint enables robust low-data training. Visual exploration shows the latent space aligns fictitious stress with the true RVE state without explicit training. The adjoint encoder-decoder structure with positive scalar contributions ensures energy consistency across scales and non-negative dissipation, further lowering training requirements.

Conclusion: Careful architectural choices rooted in the underlying physics improve interpretability, inductive bias, thermodynamic consistency, and data efficiency of Physically Recurrent Neural Networks.

Abstract: In this paper, we unravel the effect of different decoder architectures on the interpretability of the latent space of the Physically Recurrent Neural Network. Particular emphasis is given to a new weight normalization constraint, which acts as a regularization technique and enables robust training in the low-data regime. A brief visual exploration illustrates how these changes impact the latent space and how the fictitious stress can align with the true state of the RVE without explicit training. Reaping the benefits of a meaningful latent space, a case study illustrates how information from the microscopic level can be retrieved and incorporated into a multi-task approach that does not require extra parameters or larger training sets. Another key contribution shows that a specific architectural choice can naturally lead to a thermodynamically consistent formulation. By enforcing an adjoint encoder-decoder structure with positive scalar contributions, this modification ensures energy consistency across scales and non-negative dissipation, leading to even lower training requirements. This alternative completes the study on interpretability, inductive bias, and thermodynamic consistency, and demonstrates that data efficiency can be improved with careful architectural choices rooted in the underlying physics.

</details>


### [284] [Checkable NTK Positivity and Finite-Width Gradient Descent for Scalar- and Vector-Valued PINNs with Strong-Form, Weak-Form, and Nonlocal Linear Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04357)
*Zifan Lyu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides checkable positive-definiteness criteria for the limiting NTK of PINNs with linear constraints, plus finite-width gradient-descent guarantees, where positive definiteness reduces to a rank condition on a finite design matrix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs encode differential equations and other linear constraints into the loss, but their trainability depends on the positive definiteness of the limiting NTK. Prior work lacks general, checkable criteria covering diverse constraint types and finite-width guarantees.

Method: The authors treat constraints as strong-form differential rows of finite order (including coupled systems), linear initial/boundary conditions, weak-form residual and boundary functionals, and finite-measure nonlocal observations (integral, nonlocal-diffusion, Dirac data) in any dimension. For each class they prove that positive definiteness of the limiting NTK is equivalent to a rank condition on a finite coefficient, functional, or moment matrix computed from the fixed design—a pre-training certificate detecting structural zero modes. The analysis assumes an ordinary two-layer PINN with a smooth nonpolynomial activation and bounded symmetric initialization (e.g., tanh with uniform init). Given a certificate, explicit width and step-size conditions yield high-probability bounds on the empirical NTK and geometric loss decrease under full-batch GD.

Result: Positive definiteness of the limiting NTK is equivalent to a finite-matrix rank condition for every constraint class. Under explicit width and step-size conditions, the empirical NTK retains at least half the limiting gap with high probability, and full-batch gradient descent decreases the training loss geometrically at every iteration. Controlled experiments validate the certificates and the finite-width mechanism.

Conclusion: The work yields computable certificates for NTK positive definiteness and rigorous finite-width convergence guarantees for PINNs with broad linear constraints, unifying strong, weak, and nonlocal constraint types and enabling pre-training detection of structural degeneracies.

Abstract: We give checkable positive-definiteness criteria for the limiting neural tangent kernel (NTK) and high-probability finite-width gradient-descent guarantees for scalar- or vector-valued physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) with linear constraints. The constraints may be strong-form differential rows of any fixed finite order, including coupled systems, with any linear initial or boundary conditions; weak-form residual and boundary functionals; or finite-measure nonlocal observations such as integral, nonlocal-diffusion, and Dirac-data rows, all in any dimension. For each class, positive definiteness of the limiting NTK is equivalent to a rank condition on a finite coefficient, functional, or moment matrix of the fixed design: a certificate computed before training that detects structural zero modes. The model hypotheses are those of an ordinary two-layer PINN, a smooth nonpolynomial activation with bounded symmetric initialization, and are met by standard choices such as $\tanh$ with uniform initialization. Given a certificate, explicit width and step-size conditions ensure, with high probability, that the empirical NTK retains at least half the limiting gap and that full-batch gradient descent decreases the training loss geometrically at every iteration. Controlled experiments check the certificates and the finite-width mechanism.

</details>


### [285] [CEENs: Causality-enforced evolutional networks for solving time-dependent partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04405)
*Jeahan Jung,Heechang Kim,Hyomin Shin,Minseok Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causality-respecting PINN variant that splits the time domain into subintervals, assigns a network per subinterval, and trains sequentially with an integral-form loss, improving long-time PDE accuracy at lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Original PINNs lack temporal causality, causing bias toward satisfying PDEs at later times before learning initial conditions, which yields erroneous solutions in long-time integration.

Method: Inspired by classical numerical methods, the time domain is divided into nonoverlapping subintervals, each with a unique neural network. A loss based on the integral form of the PDE is constructed within subintervals, and networks are trained sequentially starting from the initial time step.

Result: Significant accuracy improvement for long-time simulations of various PDE problems where standard PINNs fail, with less computational cost and memory. A parallelization algorithm further speeds up solving time-dependent PDEs.

Conclusion: Integrating temporal causality via sequential subinterval training markedly enhances PINNs' long-time PDE applicability while reducing cost and enabling parallel efficiency.

Abstract: Despite the growing popularity of physics-informed neural networks (PINNs), their applicability in the long-time integration of partial differential equations (PDEs) remains constrained. We argue that this problem stems from the lack of consideration of temporal causality in the original PINN formulation, resulting in a bias towards satisfying governing equations at later times before learning the initial condition and hence leading to erroneous solutions. To this end, we propose a novel method that seamlessly integrates temporal causality into the training process. Drawing inspiration from classical numerical methods where the temporal causality is reflected, we divide the time domain into nonoverlapping subintervals, assign a unique neural network to each subinterval, and construct a loss function founded on the integral form of PDEs within these subintervals. The proposed networks undergo sequential training, beginning with the initial time step. Our method demonstrates significant improvement in accuracy for long-time simulations of various PDE problems where the original PINN method fails while it requires less computational cost and memory compared to the PINN method. A parallelization algorithm is provided to further enhance the computational efficiency, showing a significant speedup for solving time-dependent PDEs.

</details>


### [286] [The Numerical Linear Algebra of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04631)
*Abdelkader Baggag,Yousef Saad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey bridging Numerical Linear Algebra (NLA) and Machine Learning, focusing on Large Language Models (LLMs), aimed at making LLM concepts accessible to numerical methods specialists and highlighting NLA's contributions to LLM techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NLA has historically driven scientific progress (e.g., LR/QR algorithms for aerodynamics, sparse linear solvers for computational aerodynamics). The current ML wave, especially LLMs grounded in matrix/tensor methods, is disruptive and positioned to play a major role toward AGI. NLA practitioners struggle to adapt to this rapid change, motivating a survey that connects the two fields.

Method: A survey article that: (1) clarifies core concepts behind LLMs in a way accessible to numerical methods specialists, and (2) examines key NLA concepts employed by LLM techniques while highlighting recent significant NLA contributions to the field.

Result: The paper provides an accessible exposition of LLM fundamentals for NLA researchers and an analysis of the NLA concepts underlying LLM techniques, alongside a review of recent NLA contributions to ML/LLMs.

Conclusion: Matrix and tensor methods will play an increasingly significant role as AI advances toward AGI; the survey equips NLA practitioners to understand and engage with LLM-related developments.

Abstract: Numerical Linear Algebra (NLA) has consistently played a vital role in advancing science by providing tools to solve fundamental problems encountered in scientific and engineering applications. Over the decades, it has continually evolved to meet the demands driven by successive waves of scientific discovery. For instance, during the 1950s and 1960s, substantial efforts were devoted to developing methods for solving eigenvalue problems that emerged from the rapidly growing field of aerodynamics. This led to the discovery of the LR and QR algorithms. Later the attention turned to the solution of sparse linear systems that were common in applications like computational aerodynamics. Today we are experiencing yet another wave of major scientific advancement and NLA is once more at the heart of its development. This Machine Learning (ML) wave is proving to be utterly disruptive in science and engineering. Many tools in ML particularly Large Language Models (LLMs) are grounded in matrix and tensor methods. As we are approaching Artificial General Intelligence (AGI), it is clear that matrix methods will be called to play an even more significant role. For the numerical linear practitioner the speed of the current change makes it particularly challenging to adapt.
  This is a survey article that centers on machine learning techniques, with a particular focus on large language models. It has two main objectives. The first is to clarify the core concepts behind Large Language Models in a manner accessible to specialists in numerical methods. The second is to examine the key Numerical Linear Algebra concepts employed by LLM techniques, while also highlighting several significant recent contributions of NLA to the field.

</details>


### [287] [Inferring physical fields in coupled systems with unknown parameters from incomplete observations using physics-constrained attentive neural operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05723)
*Shilun Wei,Xiaoqiang Sun,Wei Li,Kejun Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework reconstructs full physical fields and identifies unknown parameters in coupled systems from sparse observations of a single field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring the full state of a coupled system from incomplete measurements of only one field is hard: multiple coupled fields must be reconstructed simultaneously while system parameters remain unknown.

Method: A cross-attention encoder maps sparse sensor observations onto a regular grid to build a sensor-conditioned latent representation; a Fourier neural operator (FNO) decoder captures global spatial dependencies to reconstruct all coupled fields. Network and physical parameters are jointly optimized using observation losses, governing equation residuals, and boundary/initial condition constraints.

Result: Validated on 2D/3D lid-driven cavity flows, a 2D cylinder wake, and a 2D non-ideal magnetohydrodynamics problem, showing recovery of unobserved fields and physical parameters from incomplete observations.

Conclusion: The approach enables full-field reconstruction and parameter identification of unknown coupled physical systems from sparse single-field observations.

Abstract: Given incomplete measurements of a single physical field in a coupled system with unknown parameters, can we infer its full physical state and identify the underlying parameters? This problem is challenging because multiple coupled fields must be reconstructed simultaneously from limited observations of only one, while the system parameters are unknown. In this work, we propose a machine learning framework for full-field reconstruction and parameter identification of unknown physical systems from sparse observations of a single physical field. Specifically, the cross-attention encoder propagates sparse sensor observations onto a regular grid to construct a sensor-conditioned latent representation, while a Fourier neural operator (FNO) decoder captures global spatial dependencies to reconstruct all coupled physical fields. The network parameters and unknown physical parameters are jointly optimized by minimizing observation losses, governing equation residuals, and boundary/initial condition constraints. The proposed approach is validated on two- and three-dimensional lid-driven cavity flows, a two-dimensional cylinder wake, and a two-dimensional non-ideal magnetohydrodynamics problem, demonstrating the recovery performance of unobserved fields and physical parameters from incomplete observations.

</details>


<div id='math.CA'></div>

# math.CA [[Back]](#toc)

### [288] [Weak reverse Hölder inequalities for doubling measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05320)
*Martí Prats*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper proves a local, quantitative Bennewitz–Lewis theorem for doubling Radon measures on the support of μ, showing that under a small-constant Bennewitz–Lewis condition ν is absolutely continuous w.r.t. μ with a reverse Hölder density. A dyadic toolkit and a unified bootstrapping lemma also yield a local Gehring lemma for enlarged balls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical Bennewitz–Lewis theorem relates a measure satisfying the Bennewitz–Lewis condition to absolute continuity, but a local and quantitative version in the general setting of a doubling Radon measure on a possibly irregular support X ⊂ R^d was missing. The authors aim to establish such a local result and develop the dyadic machinery needed for it.

Method: They work with a doubling Radon measure μ on X and assume a Radon measure ν satisfies the Bennewitz–Lewis condition on a bounded relatively open V ⊂ X with a sufficiently small constant. They build a dyadic toolkit: triple cubes defined via neighbours, the basis D ∪ 3D, a Whitney decomposition, and Muckenhoupt-type weight classes over that basis. Both main theorems are deduced from a single bootstrapping lemma covering p=1 and p>1.

Result: ν restricted to V is absolutely continuous with respect to μ restricted to V, i.e. ν|_V ≪ μ|_V, and its density satisfies a reverse Hölder inequality on every compact subset K ⊂ V. The same machinery also gives a local Gehring lemma for enlarged balls.

Conclusion: The authors establish a local quantitative Bennewitz–Lewis theorem for doubling Radon measures and, via a unified bootstrapping approach, a local Gehring lemma for enlarged balls, providing reusable dyadic tools and weight classes for this non-smooth setting.

Abstract: Let $μ$ be a doubling Radon measure in $\mathbb R^d$ with support $X$. We prove a local and quantitative form of the Bennewitz--Lewis theorem in this setting: if a Radon measure $ν$ satisfies the Bennewitz--Lewis condition on a bounded relatively open set $V\subset X$ with a small enough constant, then $ν|_V\llμ|_V$ and its density satisfies a reverse Hölder inequality on every compact subset $K\subset V$. Along the way we develop the dyadic toolkit these arguments run on --- triple cubes defined through neighbours, the basis $\mathcal{D}\cup3\mathcal{D}$, and a Whitney decomposition --- and we collect the Muckenhoupt-type classes of weights over that basis. The same machinery yields a local Gehring lemma for enlarged balls: both theorems are deduced from a single bootstrapping lemma, stated once for $p=1$ and $p>1$.

</details>


### [289] [Multilinear transference on product spaces and applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05973)
*Árpád Bényi,Tadahiro Oh*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The authors prove transference principles for multilinear Fourier multiplier operators from Euclidean spaces to mixed product spaces ℝ^{d₁}×𝕋^{d₂}, preserving Euclidean operator bounds and yielding applications to specific multilinear and PDE-related multiplier operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilinear Fourier multiplier theory is well developed on Euclidean spaces, but analogous results on mixed settings involving tori are less understood. This work aims to transfer known Euclidean bounds to product spaces, bridging the gap and enabling applications.

Method: Establish strong and weak-type transference results that move multilinear Fourier multiplier operators from Euclidean spaces to product spaces of the form ℝ^{d₁}×𝕋^{d₂}, while preserving the corresponding Euclidean operator bounds.

Result: Strong and weak-type transference principles are proven that retain Euclidean bounds. As applications, bounds are obtained for certain multilinear operators and for multiplier operators arising naturally in PDEs.

Conclusion: The transference framework extends multilinear Fourier multiplier theory to mixed Euclidean–torus product spaces and provides new boundedness results with PDE applications.

Abstract: We establish transference results for multilinear Fourier multiplier operators from Euclidean spaces to product spaces of the form $\mathbb R^{d_1}\times\mathbb T^{d_2}$. We prove strong and weak-type transference principles that preserve the corresponding Euclidean operator bounds. As applications, we obtain bounds for certain multilinear operators, as well as for some multiplier operators arising naturally in PDEs.

</details>


### [290] [A uniqueness result for two-step indefinite $p$-Laplacian equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06068)
*Alberto Cagnetta*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: The paper proves a conjecture by Boscaggin, Feltrin and Zanolin on uniqueness of positive solutions to u''+a(t)u^γ=0 with two-step indefinite weight, extends it to the log case γ=-1, and gives a full existence/uniqueness classification for the p-Laplacian version under Neumann/periodic conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Boscaggin, Feltrin and Zanolin conjectured that the Neumann and periodic problems for u''+a(t)u^γ=0 with a two-step indefinite weight have at most one positive solution for every γ in R\{-1,0,1}. The paper aims to settle this conjecture and broaden the theory, in particular to cover the logarithmic potential case.

Method: Neumann and periodic boundary value problems for the p-Laplacian equation (|u'|^{p-2}u')' + a(t)u^γ = 0, with a two-step indefinite weight, are studied for all γ in R\{0,p-1}, using the mean-value condition γ∫_0^T a(t)dt < 0; a numerical example examines non-constant internal weight profiles.

Result: The conjecture is proven, and the uniqueness picture is completed by including γ=-1. A complete existence and uniqueness classification is obtained for all γ in R\{0,p-1}. The necessary mean-value condition is sufficient for existence when γ≤-1 or γ>p-1, whereas in the intermediate range γ in (-1,p-1)\{0} an additional sharp condition on the mean of the weight is required. A numerical example shows multiple positive solutions for the Neumann problem with a single sign change but non-constant internal profile.

Conclusion: The conjecture holds, and the analysis extends to the p-Laplacian and logarithmic case with a full classification. The sharpness of the two-step weight assumption is highlighted by numerics indicating multiplicity when the internal profile is non-constant.

Abstract: Boscaggin, Feltrin and Zanolin conjectured that the Neumann and periodic problems associated with the equation
  \begin{equation*}
  u''+a(t)u^γ=0
  \end{equation*} with a two-step indefinite weight $a(t)$ have at most one positive solution for every $γ\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{-1,0,1\right\}$. We prove this conjecture and complete the uniqueness picture by also including the logarithmic potential case $γ=-1$. More generally, for every $p>1$, we consider the equation
  \begin{equation*}
  \left(\left\lvert u'\right\rvert^{p-2}u'\right)'+a(t)u^γ=0
  \end{equation*} under Neumann or periodic boundary conditions and establish a complete existence and uniqueness classification for all $γ\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0,p-1\right\}$. The necessary mean-value condition $γ\int_{0}^{T}a(t)\,\mathrm{d} t<0$ is also sufficient for existence when $γ\leq-1$ or $γ>p-1$, while an additional sharp condition on the mean of the weight is required in the intermediate range $γ\in(-1,p-1)\setminus\{0\}$. Finally, a numerical example provides evidence of multiple positive solutions for the Neumann problem whose weight has the same single change of sign but a non-constant internal profile, highlighting the role of the two-step assumption.

</details>


<div id='math.FA'></div>

# math.FA [[Back]](#toc)

### [291] [Another characterization of $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03426)
*Rupert L. Frank*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: Improves a characterization of the Sobolev space Ẇ^{1,p}(R^d) using ball oscillations, replacing sup-finiteness with the weaker liminf-finiteness, answering Brezis's question.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An earlier characterization of Ẇ^{1,p}(R^d) via oscillations on balls asked finiteness of a supremum. Brezis questioned whether this could be weakened to finiteness of the liminf.

Method: Analytic argument refining the ball-oscillation characterization: replace the supremum condition with the formally weaker liminf condition over balls of varying centers and radii.

Result: The characterization still holds under the weaker liminf-finiteness assumption, in contrast to prior work.

Conclusion: Brezis's question is answered affirmatively, giving an improved characterization of the homogeneous Sobolev space.

Abstract: We improve an earlier characterization of the Sobolev space $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$, $1<p<\infty$, in terms of the oscillations of a function on balls of varying centers and radii. In contrast to earlier work, finiteness of the supremum is replaced by the formally weaker assumption of finiteness of the liminf, thereby answering a question by Brezis.

</details>


### [292] [Another characterization of $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03426)
*Rupert L. Frank*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: This paper strengthens a ball-oscillation characterization of the Sobolev space $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$ by weakening the sup condition to a liminf condition, resolving a question of Brezis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Earlier characterizations of $\dot W^{1,p}$ via oscillations of a function on balls of varying centers and radii required a finiteness-of-supremum condition. Brezis asked whether the formally weaker liminf condition suffices; this paper addresses that question.

Method: The authors refine the earlier ball-oscillation characterization, replacing the finiteness of the supremum with the finiteness of the liminf over ball oscillations.

Result: The improved characterization holds: finiteness of the liminf (rather than the supremum) of oscillations still characterizes $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$ for $1<p<\infty$.

Conclusion: The formally weaker liminf assumption is sufficient to characterize $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$, thereby answering Brezis's question affirmatively.

Abstract: We improve an earlier characterization of the Sobolev space $\dot W^{1,p}(\mathbb R^d)$, $1<p<\infty$, in terms of the oscillations of a function on balls of varying centers and radii. In contrast to earlier work, finiteness of the supremum is replaced by the formally weaker assumption of finiteness of the liminf, thereby answering a question by Brezis.

</details>


### [293] [A Class of Function-Preserving Neural Network Operators with Applications to Modulated Signal Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05759)
*Sachin Saini*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: This paper introduces function-preserving neural network operators that preserve a prescribed strictly positive function, analyzes their approximation properties, and validates them via numerical experiments and a denoising application.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard neural network operators may not preserve desirable functions (e.g., exponential or logarithmic), which limits their use where such preservation is important. The authors aim to design operators that guarantee preservation of a prescribed strictly positive function.

Method: A class of function-preserving neural network operators is defined to preserve a given strictly positive function. The authors analyze their approximation behavior on continuous functions, proving positivity, linearity, well-definedness, and boundedness, and derive uniform convergence on compact intervals with quantitative error estimates via the modulus of continuity.

Result: The operators are shown to be positive, linear, well-defined, and bounded, with uniform convergence on compact intervals and quantitative error bounds. Numerical experiments on preserving exponential and logarithmic functions show strong performance versus existing neural network operators, and an application to denoising modulated signals demonstrates practical relevance.

Conclusion: The proposed function-preserving neural network operators offer a theoretically grounded and practically effective tool for approximation while preserving prescribed positive functions, outperforming existing operators and proving useful in signal denoising.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce a class of function-preserving neural network operators designed to preserve a prescribed strictly positive function. We examine the approximation behaviour of the proposed operators on the space of continuous functions and show that they are positive, linear, well-defined, and bounded. Specifically, we demonstrate uniform convergence on compact intervals and get quantitative estimates of the approximation error in terms of the modulus of continuity. To support the theoretical analysis, we present several numerical experiments focusing on the preservation of exponential and logarithmic functions. These examples show how well the proposed operators perform and compare them with existing neural network operators. Also, an application to denoising modulated signals is provided, demonstrating the practical relevance of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [294] [Quantitative $Γ$-Convergence: A Variational Extrapolation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06645)
*Oscar Dominguez,Mario Milman*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper builds a quantitative extrapolation framework in the Calculus of Variations by merging De Giorgi's Γ-convergence with Gagliardo's relative completions, using variational K-functionals to characterize Γ-limits and minimizer convergence rates, with applications to image-processing models like ROF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: De Giorgi's Γ-convergence lacks a robust quantitative theory giving explicit convergence rates; the paper aims to supply one by linking it to interpolation/completion ideas.

Method: Introduces extrapolation of variational K-functionals, associating a dynamic penalty method to any family of functionals that simultaneously characterizes Γ-limits and yields explicit minimizer convergence rates; proves compactness extrapolation theorems involving Gagliardo completions.

Result: Applies the technique to image-processing models (ROF, Chan–Esedoglu); contributes to the inverse regularity problem for discrete ROF approximations, characterizing the required image regularity via decay of variational K-functionals, expressed through Brezis–Van Schaftingen–Yung spaces; unifies and extends Bourgain–Brezis–Mironescu and Maz'ya–Shaposhnikova results (pointwise and Γ-versions), including BMO-type functionals, heat kernels, and Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces.

Conclusion: Bridging De Giorgi's variational convergence and Gagliardo's completion via variational K-functionals and compactness extrapolation establishes a quantitative approach to the Calculus of Variations.

Abstract: This paper introduces a new extrapolation framework within the Calculus of Variations. We develop a robust quantitative theory of the $Γ$-convergence approach of De Giorgi by combining it with Gagliardo's work on relative completions in interpolation theory. A central new tool is the extrapolation of \emph{variational $K$-functionals}, which associates to an arbitrary family of functionals a dynamic penalty method that simultaneously characterizes their $Γ$-limits and yields explicit rates of convergence for minimizers. As a technical foundation we prove compactness extrapolation theorems also involving Gagliardo completions. To illustrate the mechanism, we apply the technique to variational models arising in image processing, notably the Rudin--Osher--Fatemi (ROF) and Chan--Esedoglu models. Our main result in this setting is a contribution to the \emph{inverse regularity problem} associated to discrete approximations of the ROF model: we show that the required regularity of the observed image that guarantees a prescribed optimal rate of convergence is measured in terms of the decay of variational $K$-functionals. Surprisingly, our characterization is explicitly captured by using the recently introduced spaces of Brezis, Van Schaftingen, and Yung. Moreover, our methods unify, and considerably extend, classical pointwise convergence and $Γ$-versions of the Bourgain--Brezis--Mironescu and Maz'ya--Shaposhnikova formulae, as well as related results in a variety of settings that involve BMO-type functionals, heat kernels, Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces, ... By bridging De Giorgi's variational convergence with Gagliardo's completion through variational $K$-functionals and extrapolation of compactness, this work establishes a quantitative approach to the Calculus of Variations.

</details>


### [295] [Sharp Hardy inequalities for the Vladimirov--Taibleson operator on $\mathbb{Q}_p^n$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04463)
*Yaojia Sun*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: Sharp Hardy inequalities are proven on $\mathbb{Q}_p^n$ for the Vladimirov--Taibleson quadratic form, weighted Riesz potential $L^q$ norms, and a nonlinear Gagliardo difference energy, with exact constants and ground-state representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establish sharp Hardy inequalities on $p$-adic spaces, determining exact constants and the precise parameter regime where the inequalities hold on the full test-function space.

Method: Self-dual Fourier normalization; weighted Schur estimate for the Riesz potential; two-point Picone inequality; shell decomposition; annular truncations of critical powers as minimizing sequences.

Result: Sharp quadratic-form constant $[(1-p^{-(n-\alpha)/2})/(1-p^{-(n+\alpha)/2})]^2$; exact $L^q$ norm $[\Gamma_p^{(n)}(n/q'+s)/\Gamma_p^{(n)}(n/q')]^q$; difference energy constant $2(1-p^{-n})\sum_{m\ge1}p^{-sqm}(1-p^{-(n/q-s)m})^q$. Equality only for zero; $q=2$ unifies cases; $sq<n$ without $s<1$.

Conclusion: The inequalities hold on the full test-function space precisely when $sq<n$, with no restriction $s<1$, and equality is attained only by the zero function.

Abstract: We prove sharp Hardy inequalities on $\mathbb{Q}_p^n$ for the quadratic form of the Vladimirov--Taibleson operator, the $L^q$ norm of its fractional derivative, and a nonlinear difference energy. Under the self-dual Fourier normalization, the sharp quadratic-form constant for $0<α<n$ is $[(1-p^{-(n-α)/2})/(1-p^{-(n+α)/2})]^2$, with a remainder given by the ground state representation. For $1<q<\infty$, $q'=q/(q-1)$, and $0<s<n/q$, a weighted Schur estimate determines the exact $L^q$ norm of the weighted Riesz potential $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_p^{-s}I_s$, giving the sharp linear Hardy constant $[Γ_{p}^{(n)}(n/q'+s)/Γ_{p}^{(n)}(n/q')]^q$. The two-point Picone inequality and shell decomposition give the sharp constant $2(1-p^{-n})\sum_{m\ge1}p^{-sqm}(1-p^{-(n/q-s)m})^q$ for the Gagliardo-type energy with kernel $\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_p^{-n-sq}$. Annular truncations of the critical powers give explicit minimizing sequences with error estimates. Equality is attained only by the zero function in each stated class. For $q=2$, both $L^q$ inequalities reduce to the quadratic-form inequality. Uniform local constancy makes the difference energy finite for every Bruhat--Schwartz function and every $s>0$. The Hardy inequalities on the full test-function space therefore hold precisely when $sq<n$, without the restriction $s<1$.

</details>


### [296] [Sharp discrete Hardy constants in low dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05421)
*Natanael Alpay*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper proves that the sharp constant in the nearest-neighbor Hardy inequality on Z^d equals the continuous Hardy coefficient C_d=(d-2)^2/4 for dimensions 5≤d≤8.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed C_d is sharp for d=3,4 but not for d≥9, leaving dimensions 5–8 unresolved.

Method: The proof uses reciprocal edge fields and pointwise estimates for associated vertex weights: exterior estimates via polynomial inequalities, and near-origin weights via explicit estimates and local corrections.

Result: λ_d = C_d is established for all remaining cases 5≤d≤8, completing the classification of when the continuous Hardy coefficient is sharp.

Conclusion: Combined with earlier results, the sharp constant λ_d equals C_d for 3≤d≤8 and differs for d≥9.

Abstract: For $d\ge3$, let $λ_d$ denote the sharp constant in the nearest-neighbor Hardy inequality on $\mathbb Z^d$ with Euclidean inverse-square weight for functions vanishing at the origin. Recent work established that the continuous Hardy coefficient \[ C_d=\frac{(d-2)^2}{4} \] is sharp in dimensions three and four, while it is not sharp for $d\ge9$. We prove $λ_d=C_d$ for the remaining cases $5\le d\le8$. The proof uses reciprocal edge fields and pointwise estimates for the associated vertex weights. The exterior estimates follow from polynomial inequalities, and the weights near the origin are treated by explicit estimates and local corrections.

</details>


### [297] [Lower bounds and Poincaré inequality for the Brezis-Seeger-Van Schaftingen-Yung functionals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05929)
*Nicola Picenni*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper studies a three-parameter family (γ,p,λ) of non-local, non-convex functionals, proving a Gamma-liminf lower bound and a Poincaré-type inequality when γ<-p as λ→0+, showing the inequality fails for γ>0, and providing a modified version in that case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The functionals were introduced by Brezis, Seeger, Van Schaftingen and Yung, and several open questions raised by them and by H. M. Nguyen remained unanswered.

Method: Asymptotic analysis (λ→0+) via Gamma-convergence techniques, establishing lower bounds for the Gamma-liminf and deriving Poincaré-type inequalities.

Result: For γ<-p, a lower bound for the Gamma-liminf and a Poincaré-type inequality are proved; the inequality is shown to fail for γ>0.

Conclusion: A modified version of the inequality is established for γ>0, answering the open questions of the aforementioned authors.

Abstract: We consider a family of non-local and non-convex functionals depending on three real parameters $(γ,p,λ)$, which was introduced by H. Brezis, A. Seeger, J. Van Schaftingen and P. L. Yung. In the case $γ<-p$ we prove a lower bound for the Gamma-liminf of these functionals as $λ\to 0^+$ and a Poincaré-type inequality. We also show that such inequality cannot hold if $γ>0$, and we establish a modified version in this case.
  These results answer some open questions posed by these authors and by H. M. Nguyen.

</details>


### [298] [Characterization of the boundedness of the discrete Fractional maximal operator between weighted in Lorentz sequence spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06124)
*Amiran Gogatishvili,Nurzhan A. Bokayev,Nurgul K. Kuzeubayeva*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper fully characterizes the boundedness of the discrete fractional maximal operator $M_\gamma$ on $\mathbb{Z}^n$ between Lorentz sequence spaces $\lambda_p(v)$ and $\lambda_q(w)$.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The boundedness of the discrete fractional maximal operator between weighted Lorentz sequence spaces was not fully understood, and a complete characterization was needed.

Method: A sharp upper estimate for the nonincreasing rearrangement of $M_\gamma x$ is derived using a supremal operator involving a discrete Hardy operator.
