### [317] [The ALPS project release 3.0: open source software for strongly correlated systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03884)
*F. Alet,T. Chen,A. Feiguin,E. Gull,S. Iskakov,J. P. F. LeBlanc,F. Lin,A. Mirmira,G. Möller,L. Pollet,M. Rosales,V. W. Scarola,H. Shinaoka,H. Terletska,S. Todo,M. Troyer,M. Wallerberger,P. Werner,T. M. R. Wolf*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Release 3.0 of the ALPS open-source software project for simulating strongly correlated quantum lattice models, featuring modernized code, new distribution channels, and governance updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide libraries and application programs that enable both experts and non-experts to simulate strongly correlated quantum lattice models (quantum magnets, lattice bosons, strongly correlated fermions) using key numerical algorithms.

Method: Development centered on common data formats, code-simplifying libraries, and full-featured simulation programs covering classical/quantum Monte Carlo with non-local updates, extended-ensemble simulations, exact/full diagonalization, DMRG, and continuous-time QMC solvers for DMFT.

Result: Release 3.0 includes pip/Spack distribution, GitHub migration with CI/testing, MIT relicensing, rebuilt documentation with Jupyter tutorials, C++17 modernization, major DMRG update, removal of legacy components, Zenodo DOI archival, and an NSF POSE governance model.

Conclusion: ALPS 3.0 delivers a modernized, sustainably governed, openly licensed simulation package, available at https://alps.comp-phys.org.

Abstract: We present release 3.0 of the ALPS (Algorithms and Libraries for Physics Simulations) project, an open-source software project to develop libraries and application programs for the simulation of strongly correlated quantum lattice models such as quantum magnets, lattice bosons, and strongly correlated fermion systems. As in previous releases, development is centered on common data formats, on libraries to simplify and speed up code development, and on full-featured simulation programs that let non-experts carry out serial or parallel numerical simulations using the important algorithms for quantum lattice models: classical and quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) using non-local updates, extended-ensemble simulations, exact and full diagonalization (ED), the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG), and continuous-time QMC solvers for dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). Major changes in release 3.0 include distribution of the pyalps binary through the Python Package Index (pip install pyalps) and through Spack for HPC systems; migration of development to GitHub with continuous integration and automated testing; relicensing of the package under the permissive MIT license; a rebuilt documentation and tutorial website, including a set of Jupyter-notebook tutorials and localized content; a broad modernization of the C++ codebase (C++17 compliance, Boost and NumPy 2.0 compatibility, and warning and dead-code cleanup) together with a major DMRG update and associated reliability and build-compatibility fixes; the removal of legacy components (the VisTrails provenance integration and the TEBD, MPS, and directed-worm-algorithm application codes); archival of release 3.0.0 with a Zenodo DOI (10.5281/zenodo.22775899); and a formal governance and sustainability model developed under the US National Science Foundation (NSF) POSE program. The software is available at https://alps.comp-phys.org.

</details>


<div id='cs.CV'></div>

# cs.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [318] [A differentiable Lagrangian-coupled 3D Gaussian Splatting-SPH model for forward simulation and inverse analysis in solid mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04336)
*Tian Xu,Soroush Atashi,Tianju Xue*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A differentiable Lagrangian-coupled 3DGS-SPH model is proposed for forward simulation and inverse analysis of deformable solids reconstructed from images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative world models need digital replicas that reproduce both appearance and physical interaction response. While 3D reconstruction (e.g., 3D Gaussian Splatting) captures geometry and appearance well, extending these representations beyond plausible animation to mechanically interpretable models—covering constitutive behavior, boundary conditions, and inverse parameter identification—remains underexplored.

Method: The object is reconstructed from a multi-view calibrated dataset as a 3DGS rendering model. An envelope-based procedure generates an independent SPH support for the solid-mechanics model, avoiding direct use of rendering primitives as mechanical particles. A reference-configuration Lagrangian transfer maps SPH deformation to Gaussian positions and covariances, coupling the physical and image observation models while keeping a differentiable path. The SPH formulation supports linear elastic, hyperelastic, and Kelvin–Voigt viscoelastic responses with fixed, free, and Robin-type boundary conditions.

Result: Numerical studies validate the SPH response against finite-element results, assess accuracy/efficiency versus a conventional model using Gaussian centers as surface SPH particles, and demonstrate forward simulations on beam, bridge, and liver-shaped examples. Inverse analyses estimate constitutive and boundary parameters from rendered deformation observations, including noisy cases.

Conclusion: The proposed differentiable 3DGS-SPH model enables mechanics-based parameter identification from image data, showing feasibility for mechanically interpretable digital models.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative world models have increased interest in digital models that reproduce both the appearance of real objects and their response to physical interaction. Three-dimensional reconstruction techniques, including 3D Gaussian Splatting, capture detailed surface geometry and appearance from images and videos. However, extending these representations beyond plausible animation to mechanically interpretable models for constitutive behavior, boundary conditions, and inverse parameter identification remains less explored. In this work, a differentiable Lagrangian-coupled 3DGS-smoothed particle hydrodynamics (SPH) model is proposed for forward simulation and inverse analysis of deformable solids. The observed object is first reconstructed from multi-view calibrated visual dataset as a 3DGS rendering model. An envelope-based procedure then generates an independent SPH support for the solid-mechanics model, avoiding the direct use of rendering primitives as mechanical particles. A reference-configuration Lagrangian transfer maps SPH deformation to Gaussian positions and covariances, thereby coupling the physical model and the image observation model while preserving a differentiable computational path. The SPH formulation supports linear elastic, hyperelastic, and Kelvin--Voigt viscoelastic responses, together with fixed, free, and Robin-type boundary conditions. Numerical studies validate the SPH response against finite-element results, assess accuracy and efficiency against a conventional model using Gaussian centers as surface SPH particles, and demonstrate forward simulations on beam, bridge, and liver-shaped examples. Inverse analyses further estimate constitutive and boundary parameters from rendered deformation observations, including noisy cases, demonstrating the feasibility of the proposed model for mechanics-based parameter identification from image data.

</details>


<div id='math.DS'></div>

# math.DS [[Back]](#toc)

### [319] [On the Spread of a Virus and Traveling Waves, Part I](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04732)
*Yizhou Wang,Akif Ibragimov*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: A 1D model of a mobile viral component spreading through a spatially distributed susceptible population is developed, with analytic traveling-wave solutions proved and validated by a conservative implicit-explicit finite-difference scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Viral propagation through a spatially distributed susceptible population involves both random (diffusive) motion and directed transport driven by gradients in susceptible density. A tractable model capturing when directed transport dominates diffusion, along with verifiable closed-form solutions, was needed.

Method: The model combines diffusion from random viral motion with directed transport from the relative gradient of susceptible density. Existence of a positive entire traveling wave is proved when transport dominates diffusion, and the equal-coefficient case is constructed separately. A dimensionless transformation reduces the equation to a logistic equation, yielding closed-form profiles, the unique viral maximum, and far-field behavior. A conservative implicit-explicit finite-difference scheme (diffusion implicit, gradient flux explicit at interfaces, depletion pointwise) with exact traveling-wave initial and Dirichlet boundary data verifies the profiles.

Result: With 800 spatial subintervals, the computation reproduces the localized viral band and monotone susceptible front, with largest uniform errors about 1.70e-2 for viral density and 2.22e-3 for susceptible density.

Conclusion: Numerical results corroborate the analytical construction, showing the conservative discretization preserves and translates the traveling-wave profiles and captures the proposed propagation mechanism.

Abstract: This paper develops a one-dimensional model for a mobile viral component propagating through a spatially distributed susceptible population. Random viral motion produces diffusion, whereas a response to the relative gradient of susceptible density generates directed transport. The existence of a positive entire traveling wave is proved when directed transport dominates diffusion, and the limiting case of equal transport and diffusion coefficients is constructed separately. A dimensionless transformation then converts the remaining equation into a logistic equation and supplies closed-form profiles. These formulas identify the unique viral maximum and the far-field behavior. A conservative implicit-explicit finite-difference method provides an independent numerical check. Diffusion is evaluated at the new time level, the gradient-driven flux explicitly at cell interfaces, and susceptible depletion pointwise. Exact traveling-wave values specify the initial data and time-dependent Dirichlet conditions at both endpoints. Thus, the computation tests preservation and translation of the analytical profiles. A calculation with eight hundred spatial subintervals reproduces the localized viral band and the monotone susceptible front. The largest uniform errors are approximately $1.70\cdot10^{-2}$ for viral density and $2.22\cdot10^{-3}$ for susceptible density. The numerical results corroborate the analytical construction and show that the conservative discretization captures the proposed propagation mechanism.

</details>


### [320] [Symmetry-breaking in a discrete-choice LQG mean field game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05730)
*Ali Akbar Rezaei Lori,Piyush Grover*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: This paper proves symmetry-breaking in a finite-horizon, two-choice min-LQG mean-field game: as the social-conformity penalty grows, the symmetric (even-split) equilibrium loses stability and asymmetric consensus equilibria emerge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical studies suggested that increasing the penalty for social nonconformity destabilizes the symmetric equilibrium of a two-destination min-LQG mean-field game, but no rigorous proof existed.

Method: Analyze the linearized forward-backward PDE system via a scalar self-consistency map, exploiting its odd symmetry.

Result: Provides a proof of the loss of stability of the symmetric equilibrium, implying existence of symmetry-broken consensus equilibria on either destination.

Conclusion: Symmetry-breaking in the min-LQG mean-field game is rigorously established, confirming prior numerical findings of consensus equilibria.

Abstract: Mean-field games provide a continuum framework for modeling the dynamics of large, interacting populations of non-cooperative agents. This paper studies symmetry-breaking in a finite-horizon, two-choice min-LQG mean-field game in which identical agents with linear stochastic dynamics choose one of two equally desirable terminal destinations, while trading off control effort against social pressure to conform. The model has an odd symmetry between the two destinations and therefore always admits a symmetric, dynamic Nash equilibrium in which the population splits evenly between them, producing a deadlock collective state at final time. Numerical studies have suggested that, as the penalty for social nonconformity increases, this symmetric equilibrium loses stability as a fixed point of an associated scalar, self-consistency map, and asymmetric consensus Nash equilibria emerge, where most agents select the same destination. By analyzing the linearized forward-backward PDE system through the scalar map representation, we provide a proof of this loss of stability of the symmetric equilibrium. Together with the odd symmetry of the map, this implies the existence of symmetry-broken mean-field game equilibria corresponding to consensus on either destination.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [321] [Equivariant generative diffusion learns and generalizes the structural ensemble of amorphous oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03973)
*Jun Jiang,Ian Berry,James N. Fry,Hai-Ping Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: An SE(3)-equivariant denoising-diffusion model learns the configurational distribution of amorphous oxides from limited DFT data, enabling scalable generation and first-principles-guided structure discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Amorphous materials are statistical ensembles, but conventional DFT and machine-learned-potential simulations sample only a small part of that ensemble, limiting exploration of their configurational space.

Method: An SE(3)-equivariant denoising-diffusion model trained on 1,781 DFT configurations that acts as the structure database itself, with data-efficient learning and the ability to propose structures for first-principles relaxation.

Result: The model reproduces partial radial distribution functions, coordination statistics, and bond-angle distributions, generates models of over 3×10^5 atoms at low cost, and locates an amorphous Zr-Ta-O structure 36 meV/atom below the known minimum.

Conclusion: Generation extends to non-stoichiometric compositions, other densities, interfaces, and doping; first-principles verification shows generation can be steered to target energies, and denoising loss does not rank generative quality as they measure different things.

Abstract: Amorphous materials are statistical ensembles rather than definitive structures, and conventional density-functional (DFT) and machine-learned-potential simulations sample only a small part of that ensemble. We present an $SE(3)$-equivariant denoising-diffusion model that learns the configurational distribution of amorphous oxides, so the model itself is the structure database. The learning is data efficient. A model trained on $1{,}781$ DFT configurations suffices to reproduce partial radial distribution functions, coordination statistics and bond-angle distributions, and to generate models of over $3\times10^{5}$ atoms at a cost comparable to that of the cheapest classical pair potentials. The trained model can propose amorphous atomic structures for first-principles relaxation to explore the configuration space. For example, it locates an amorphous Zr-Ta-O structure $36$ meV/atom below the previously known minimum. Generation can also extend beyond trained conditions to non-stoichiometric compositions, other mass densities, interfaces, and doping. First-principles verification confirms that generation can be steered to a requested energy, and shows that the denoising training loss does not rank generative quality, because the two measure different things.

</details>


### [322] [Out-of-Plane Oscillating Electric Fields Unlock Low-Temperature Nonequilibrium Superionicity in Quasi-Two-Dimensional AgCrSe2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05941)
*Jia-Wen Li,Kun Yang,Sheng Meng,Xinghua Shi,Wei-Hai Fang,Jin Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A machine-learning MD study shows an out-of-plane oscillating electric field can induce superionic Ag+ transport in AgCrSe2 at 300 K, far below its ~475 K order-disorder transition, via field-driven dynamic disorder rather than thermal disordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superionic conductors offer fast ion transport but usually require temperatures above the order-disorder transition of the mobile-ion sublattice, limiting low-temperature operation. The paper seeks a nonequilibrium route to achieve superionic transport at lower temperatures.

Method: Electric-field-responsive machine-learning molecular dynamics simulations of quasi-2D AgCrSe2 under an out-of-plane oscillating electric field.

Result: At 300 K (well below the ~475 K transition), the field activates fast in-plane Ag+ transport with conductivity ~1.7 S/cm. This arises from field-driven dynamic disorder of Ag+ across two equivalent sublattices, not thermal disordering. As frequency increases, Ag occupation shifts from complete switching to a conductive dynamically disordered state, then to an ordered state with weak response. Regimes move to higher frequencies with higher temperature or field amplitude, reflecting competition between driving period and Ag-sublattice response time. Strong-field timescales match field-assisted thermal activation, while weak-field deviations imply extra dynamics beyond single-ion activation.

Conclusion: Timescale-matched periodic driving is proposed as a route to transport-active ionic disorder far below the equilibrium superionic transition.

Abstract: Superionic conductors enable exceptionally fast ion transport in solids and offer broad technological potential. However, their highly conductive states are typically accessed only above the order-disorder transition of the mobile-ion sublattice, which limits low-temperature operation. Here, electric-field-responsive machine-learning molecular dynamics reveals a nonequilibrium route to superionic transport in quasi-two-dimensional AgCrSe2 driven by an out-of-plane oscillating electric field. At 300 K, far below the transition temperature near 475 K, the field activates fast in-plane Ag+ transport with an ionic conductivity reaching ~1.7 S/cm. This originates from field-driven dynamic disorder of Ag+ across two equivalent sublattices rather than thermally induced disordering. With increasing frequency, the Ag occupation evolves from complete switching to a highly conductive dynamically disordered state and finally to an ordered state with weak field response. These regimes shift to higher frequencies with increasing temperature or field amplitude, consistent with competition between the driving period and Ag-sublattice response time. At strong fields, this timescale is consistent with field-assisted thermal activation, while weak-field deviations suggest additional dynamics beyond single-ion activation. These results suggest timescale-matched periodic driving as a route to transport-active ionic disorder far below an equilibrium superionic transition.

</details>


### [323] [First-Principles Investigation of Multimodal Toxic Gas Sensing in Carbon-Tuned hBN-Graphene Alloys: Chemiresistive, Work-Function, and Optical Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06283)
*Tanjuma Shikder Jhumu,Ahmed Zubair*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A DFT first-principles study shows hBN-graphene alloys (especially 22% and 33% carbon) are promising chemiresistive, work-function-based, and optical gas sensors for Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing demand for compact, reliable, cost-effective gas sensors in medical, chemical, food, agricultural, and industrial safety sectors requires fast, sensitive detection of hazardous gases, motivating the search for suitable sensing materials.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) first-principles study of the adsorption behavior of Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN on hexagonal boron nitride–graphene (hBN-Gr) alloys with varying carbon proportions (e.g., BNGr-2 with 22% C, BNGr-3 with 33% C).

Result: BNGr-2 (22% C) best senses Cl2, CO, CO2, and HCN; BNGr-3 (33% C) best interacts with NO and NO2. NOx showed highest chemiresistive sensitivity; other gases showed significant sensitivity and distinct selectivity. ~20% work function variation supports work-function sensing. Cl2 and NO2 were strongly physisorbed while others were weak-to-moderate, giving very short recovery times (10^-1 to 10^-6 s). Distinctive absorption spectra support optical sensing, and all gases desorb from BNGr-2 at 425 K.

Conclusion: hBN-Gr alloys are promising, tunable gas sensors for pollution auditing and hazardous gas monitoring.

Abstract: Compact, reliable, and cost-effective gas sensors have become a highly demanding subject for safety management in the medical sector, chemical manufacturing, food quality monitoring, agriculture, and industrial safety. Hazardous gas emissions need to be controlled and monitored with fast-responsive and highly sensitive sensing devices. A first-principles study employing density functional theory (DFT) was used to investigate the adsorption behavior of Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN gas molecules with our proposed alloys, which consisted of hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) and graphene (Gr). The alloy consisting of 22% carbon (BNGr-2) was found to be most competent for sensing Cl2, CO, CO2, and HCN with sufficient adsorption energy, charge transfer, and bandgap alteration. However, NO and NO2 gas molecules showed more engagement with 33% carbon-proportioned alloy (BNGr-3) in terms of adequate gas sensing properties. NOx gases exhibited the most chemiresistive sensitivity towards the adsorbents. Other gases also showed significant chemiresistive sensitivity and distinct selectivity ratios, which would facilitate these alloys as chemiresistive sensors. Besides, noticeable work function variation (~20%) of these systems manifested potential as work function based sensors. Cl2 and NO2 showed strong physical adsorption, while the rest of the gases were weakly to moderately physisorbed, resulting in very short recovery times (10-1 ~ 10-6 seconds). Additionally, the distinctive absorption spectra observed for the gas analyte systems highlighted the potential of the proposed alloys as optical gas sensors. Temperature variation revealed that all gas molecules can be freed from the adsorbent BNGr-2 at 425 K. These findings imply hBN-Gr alloys as promising gas sensors for pollution auditing.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [324] [MUTACO: Simulator-Efficient Response Matching in Stochastic Nonlinear Systems via Compatibility-Aware Sequential Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04619)
*Ege Karadeniz,İlter Onat Korkmaz,Umut Can Turhan,Cem Tekin,Aydın Cem Keser*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: MUTACO is an uncertainty-aware sequential-design method for response-matching inverse problems that relies only on noisy forward simulator evaluations, demonstrated on a disordered stochastic phase-oscillator network.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many physical inverse problems depend on expensive stochastic simulators lacking response gradients, adjoint sensitivities, or differentiable interfaces. Additionally, experimentally accessible observables often characterize ensemble statistics rather than a specific microscopic realization, making unique parameter recovery inappropriate.

Method: MUTACO (Multiple Target-based Confident Acquisition) learns a multicomponent response together with its predictive uncertainty using only noisy forward evaluations, and performs sequential design to find parameter configurations whose predicted rotation and synchronization responses match the target under multiple driving conditions.

Result: Under strictly limited simulation budgets, MUTACO achieves the lowest held-out response error among adaptive-search, space-filling, and simulation-based inference baselines, and also yields lower parameter-recovery error.

Conclusion: Response-targeted sequential design is a simulator-efficient approach to inverse modeling from finite noisy forward evaluations, with potential broader applicability to stochastic physical systems with correlated multicomponent responses and weakly constrained parameter directions.

Abstract: Many physical inverse problems are mediated by expensive stochastic simulators for which response gradients, adjoint sensitivities, or a differentiable simulator interface are unavailable. We introduce Multiple Target-based Confident Acquisition (MUTACO), an uncertainty-aware sequential-design method for response matching that requires only noisy forward evaluations and learns the multicomponent response together with its predictive uncertainty. We develop and evaluate MUTACO for a disordered network of stochastic phase oscillators. The inverse task is to infer statistical properties of the local potential and interactions from macroscopic rotation and synchronization curves under multiple driving conditions. Rather than requiring unique recovery of these statistical properties, MUTACO searches for parameter configurations whose predicted rotation and synchronization responses are compatible with the target response while accounting for predictive uncertainty. This response-centered formulation is appropriate when experimentally accessible observables characterize ensemble statistics rather than a particular microscopic realization. Under strictly limited simulation budgets, MUTACO achieves the lowest held-out response error among the tested adaptive-search, space-filling, and simulation-based inference methods, while also yielding lower parameter-recovery error than the compared methods. These results demonstrate that response-targeted sequential design is a simulator-efficient approach to inverse modeling from finite noisy forward evaluations and suggest broader applicability to stochastic physical systems with correlated multicomponent responses and weakly constrained parameter directions.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [325] [FlashSinkhorn 2: Block-Sparse Entropic Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02395)
*Felix X. -F. Ye,Yu Chin Fabian Lim,Naigang Wang,Davis Wertheimer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashSinkhorn 2 (FS2) is a two-stage streaming GPU solver for squared-Euclidean entropic optimal transport on large discrete problems, achieving massive scale on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming GPU solvers like FlashSinkhorn avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all n×m point pairs each Sinkhorn iteration, which is prohibitive for very large problems.

Method: A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts potentials to all points, and continues on centroids when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift, replacing most point-level updates. A block-sparse fine stage removes residual centroid error, using Morton-ordered blocks with screening and fused tensor-core execution; a block-mass threshold bounds each omitted tile's contribution per row/column.

Result: Reaches target residual on all 32 synthetic problems and GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, solves discrete EOT between two 1.34×10^8-particle cosmological N-body measures—at blur equal to mean interparticle distance, all-particle marginal residual below 0.01—in under 2.5 hours.

Conclusion: FS2 is, to the authors' knowledge, the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours. Open-source implementation released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Streaming GPU solvers for entropic optimal transport (EOT), such as FlashSinkhorn, avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all $n\times m$ point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration. We present \textbf{FlashSinkhorn~2} (FS2), a solver for squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds that solves large discrete EOT problems to a prescribed marginal residual on a single GPU by coupling two stages. A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts the potentials to every point and, when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift, continues on the centroids, replacing most point-level updates. A block-sparse fine stage then removes the centroid error that coarse updates cannot. Its Morton-ordered blocks support screening and fused tensor-core execution, and a threshold set by the block masses bounds each omitted tile's contribution to every row and column. On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 problems and GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, FS2 solves discrete EOT between two $1.34\times10^8$-particle measures from a cosmological $N$-body simulation, at an entropic blur equal to the mean interparticle distance, to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours. To our knowledge, it is the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours. For reproducibility, we release an open-source implementation at https://github.com/ot-triton-lab/flash-sinkhorn

</details>


### [326] [FlashSinkhorn 2: Block-Sparse Entropic Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02395)
*Felix X. -F. Ye,Yu Chin Fabian Lim,Naigang Wang,Davis Wertheimer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashSinkhorn 2 (FS2) is a streaming GPU solver for large discrete entropic optimal transport with squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds, combining a coarse centroid stage with a block-sparse fine stage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming GPU solvers like FlashSinkhorn avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all n×m point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration, which is prohibitively expensive for very large problems.

Method: A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts potentials to all points, and falls back to centroids when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift. A block-sparse fine stage removes residual centroid error, using Morton-ordered blocks for screening and fused tensor-core execution, with a mass-based threshold bounding omitted tile contributions per row/column.

Result: FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 synthetic problems and beats GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, it solves EOT between two 1.34×10^8-particle measures from a cosmological N-body simulation to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours.

Conclusion: FS2 is, to the authors' knowledge, the largest discrete EOT problem solved to such accuracy within hours; an open-source implementation is released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Streaming GPU solvers for entropic optimal transport (EOT), such as FlashSinkhorn, avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all $n\times m$ point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration. We present \textbf{FlashSinkhorn~2} (FS2), a solver for squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds that solves large discrete EOT problems to a prescribed marginal residual on a single GPU by coupling two stages. A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts the potentials to every point and, when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift, continues on the centroids, replacing most point-level updates. A block-sparse fine stage then removes the centroid error that coarse updates cannot. Its Morton-ordered blocks support screening and fused tensor-core execution, and a threshold set by the block masses bounds each omitted tile's contribution to every row and column. On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 problems and GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, FS2 solves discrete EOT between two $1.34\times10^8$-particle measures from a cosmological $N$-body simulation, at an entropic blur equal to the mean interparticle distance, to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours. To our knowledge, it is the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours. For reproducibility, we release an open-source implementation at https://github.com/ot-triton-lab/flash-sinkhorn

</details>


### [327] [Recursive Improvement of a Differentiable Scientific Software Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04561)
*Pengcheng Hou,Xiaojun Tan,Sihan Hu,Ruisi Wang,Shuo Chen,Lei Wang,Youjin Deng,Kun Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an agent-driven environment for evolving differentiable scientific software, connecting demand identification, development, and quality evaluation through a unified differentiation interface, validated across 20 packages in physical, chemical, and biological modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Differentiable programming connects scientific computation with gradient-based inference, learning, and design, but extending it across a heterogeneous software ecosystem requires specialized effort for derivative implementation, interface integration, and quality evaluation. AI coding agents can accelerate this, but translating their capabilities into useful scientific software requires identifying research needs and evaluating how well implementations meet them.

Method: The authors build an environment for agent-driven evolution of differentiable scientific software that links demand identification, development, and quality evaluation. A unified differentiation interface exposes reusable derivative rules alongside existing numerical routines (sharing capabilities across research tasks). Research requirements guide development, and implementations are assessed via independent derivative checks, workflow tests, and performance evaluation. Validated software, research programs, and tests become shared resources.

Result: Automatic differentiation extensions were constructed and validated across 20 packages spanning physical, chemical, and biological modeling, with workflows demonstrating reuse across tasks. Benchmarks show computational savings over finite differences in gradient evaluation and complete parameter estimation. Research-driven revisions made previously unsupported workflows differentiable, corrected derivatives of scientific observables, and eliminated redundant computation. Quantum-control and thermal-design studies revised objectives based on physical evaluation, improving designs while reusing derivatives.

Conclusion: The work offers a practical approach to expanding differentiable programming across established scientific software and organizing AI agents around the recursive improvement of a shared computational ecosystem.

Abstract: Differentiable programming connects scientific computation with gradient-based inference, learning and design. Extending these capabilities across a heterogeneous software ecosystem requires specialized effort to implement derivatives, integrate interfaces and evaluate quality. AI coding agents can accelerate this transformation, but translating their capabilities into useful scientific software requires identifying research needs and evaluating how well implementations meet them. We present an environment for agent-driven evolution of differentiable scientific software that connects demand identification, development and quality evaluation. A unified differentiation interface exposes reusable derivative rules alongside existing numerical routines, allowing research tasks to share these capabilities. Research requirements guide development, with implementations assessed through independent derivative checks, workflow tests and performance evaluation. Validated software, research programs and tests become shared resources for subsequent studies. We construct and validate automatic differentiation extensions across 20 packages spanning physical, chemical and biological modeling, with research workflows demonstrating reuse across tasks. Benchmarks demonstrate computational savings over finite differences in gradient evaluation and complete parameter estimation. Research-driven revisions make previously unsupported workflows differentiable, correct derivatives of scientific observables and eliminate redundant computation. Quantum-control and thermal-design studies revise objectives in response to physical evaluation, improving designs while reusing existing derivatives. This work provides a practical approach to expanding differentiable programming across established scientific software and organizing AI agents around the recursive improvement of a shared computational ecosystem.

</details>


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [328] [A hybrid CNN-adjoint optimization framework for reconstruction of viscoelastic tissue properties in magnetic resonance elastography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02634)
*Anwesa Dey,Johann Rudi,Elena Cherkaev*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: A hybrid framework combining CNN reconstruction with adjoint-based PDE-constrained optimization for MRE, where CNN provides initialization for faster, more accurate complex shear modulus recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering the complex-valued shear modulus from MRE displacement fields is a severely ill-posed inverse problem, especially under noise and limited boundary excitations. PDE-constrained optimization is accurate but highly sensitive to initialization.

Method: Adjoint-based optimization, CNN reconstruction, and a hybrid of both. Forward model is a scalar modified stationary Stokes system with complex shear modulus. Theory: well-posedness, existence of minimizers, first-order optimality conditions. Solver: nonlinear conjugate-gradient with Armijo line search. A 2D CNN maps complex displacement measurements to spatially varying complex shear modulus fields, initializing the adjoint optimization.

Result: CNN trained on individual perturbations generalizes to previously unseen combined configurations. Hybrid method yields faster convergence and improved accuracy versus adjoint-only reconstruction.

Conclusion: Combining data-driven initialization with physics-based optimization enables efficient and accurate MRE reconstruction.

Abstract: Magnetic resonance elastography (MRE) is a noninvasive imaging modality for quantifying the viscoelastic properties of soft tissues from shear wave propagation. Recovering the complex-valued shear modulus from measured displacement fields leads to a severely ill-posed inverse problem, particularly in the presence of noise and limited boundary excitations. We investigate adjoint-based optimization, convolutional neural network (CNN) reconstruction, and a hybrid framework combining both approaches. The forward model is based on a scalar form of the modified stationary Stokes system with a complex shear modulus. We establish well-posedness of the forward problem, existence of minimizers, and first-order optimality conditions for the adjoint-based formulation, and implement a nonlinear conjugate-gradient method with Armijo line search. While PDE-constrained optimization can accurately refine coefficient reconstructions, its performance depends strongly on initialization. We therefore construct a two-dimensional CNN that maps complex-valued displacement measurements to spatially varying complex shear modulus fields and provides rapid, informative initial reconstructions. The proposed hybrid method uses the CNN reconstruction to initialize the adjoint-based optimization, yielding faster convergence and improved accuracy. The CNN is trained on coefficient fields containing individual perturbations and tested on both individual and previously unseen combined configurations. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the CNN generalizes to these more challenging configurations, while subsequent PDE-constrained optimization further refines the reconstructed coefficient. These results demonstrate the potential of combining data-driven initialization with physics-based optimization for efficient and accurate MRE reconstruction.

</details>


### [329] [Quantitative Mean-Field Limits for Recent Variants of Consensus-Based Optimization Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02901)
*Konstantin Haug*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This thesis derives rigorous propagation of chaos results for three variants of consensus-based optimization (δ-CBO, consensus freezing, consensus hopping), showing the empirical measure of interacting particles converges to the mean-field equation as N→∞.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CBO is a robust zero-order, multi-particle method for high-dimensional global optimization, grounded in exploration-exploitation trade-off. Three recent variants (δ-CBO, CF, CH) lacked rigorous particle-to-mean-field convergence guarantees.

Method: Propagation of chaos via the coupling method, applied to the empirical measure of the interacting particle system in two regimes: (i) a high-probability set with uniformly bounded empirical measures; (ii) an unrestricted setting using moment bounds and careful treatment of inter-particle dependence without favorable-event conditioning.

Result: Quantitative propagation of chaos estimates for all three CBO variants, establishing convergence of the empirical particle measure to the corresponding mean-field equation as particle number N→∞.

Conclusion: The particle systems faithfully approximate the idealized mean-field dynamics in the large-particle limit for δ-CBO, consensus freezing, and consensus hopping schemes.

Abstract: Consensus-based optimization (CBO) is a robust and flexible zero-order, multi-particle class of methods devised to provide provable solutions to high-dimensional global optimization problems, including truly nonconvex and/or nonsmooth cases. The algorithmic framework is grounded in a principled trade-off between stochastic exploration of the search space and deterministic contraction toward a consensus point. This thesis is concerned with three recently established variants of CBO. The $δ$-CBO method is similar to the classic CBO method, with the difference that the diffusion function is constant. The consensus freezing (CF) scheme can be understood as a piecewise CBO method. The overall time horizon is divided into intervals in which the consensus point is fixed. The consensus hopping (CH) scheme can be understood as a deterministic iterative scheme that jumps at each iteration to a Gaussian distribution centered at the consensus point of the previous distribution. The central contribution of this thesis is the rigorous derivation of propagation of chaos results for all three schemes. For each variant, we establish that the empirical measure of the interacting particle system converges, as the number of particles $N$ approaches infinity, to the solution of the corresponding mean-field equation. Two regimes are treated: first, on a high-probability set on which the empirical measures are uniformly bounded; second, in the unrestricted setting, where moment bounds and a careful treatment of the inter-particle dependence yield convergence without imposing conditioning on favorable events. The propagation of chaos estimates are derived by the coupling method. Together, these results provide quantitative propagation of chaos estimates for all three CBO variants and establish that the particle systems faithfully approximate the idealized mean-field dynamics in the large-particle limit.

</details>


### [330] [A hybrid CNN-adjoint optimization framework for reconstruction of viscoelastic tissue properties in magnetic resonance elastography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02634)
*Anwesa Dey,Johann Rudi,Elena Cherkaev*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper develops a hybrid MRE reconstruction framework combining a CNN for fast initial coefficient estimation with adjoint-based PDE-constrained optimization for refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering the complex-valued shear modulus from MRE displacement fields is a severely ill-posed inverse problem, especially under noise and limited boundary excitations. Pure PDE-constrained optimization is accurate but highly sensitive to initialization.

Method: Uses a scalar modified stationary Stokes forward model with complex shear modulus. Establishes well-posedness, existence of minimizers, and first-order optimality conditions; implements nonlinear conjugate-gradient with Armijo line search. Trains a 2D CNN to map complex displacements to shear modulus fields, then uses the CNN output to initialize the adjoint-based optimization (hybrid framework).

Result: CNN trained on individual perturbation fields generalizes to unseen combined configurations. Hybrid method yields faster convergence and improved accuracy compared to adjoint-only reconstruction.

Conclusion: Combining data-driven initialization with physics-based optimization enables efficient and accurate MRE reconstruction, showing promise for solving ill-posed inverse problems in elastography.

Abstract: Magnetic resonance elastography (MRE) is a noninvasive imaging modality for quantifying the viscoelastic properties of soft tissues from shear wave propagation. Recovering the complex-valued shear modulus from measured displacement fields leads to a severely ill-posed inverse problem, particularly in the presence of noise and limited boundary excitations. We investigate adjoint-based optimization, convolutional neural network (CNN) reconstruction, and a hybrid framework combining both approaches. The forward model is based on a scalar form of the modified stationary Stokes system with a complex shear modulus. We establish well-posedness of the forward problem, existence of minimizers, and first-order optimality conditions for the adjoint-based formulation, and implement a nonlinear conjugate-gradient method with Armijo line search. While PDE-constrained optimization can accurately refine coefficient reconstructions, its performance depends strongly on initialization. We therefore construct a two-dimensional CNN that maps complex-valued displacement measurements to spatially varying complex shear modulus fields and provides rapid, informative initial reconstructions. The proposed hybrid method uses the CNN reconstruction to initialize the adjoint-based optimization, yielding faster convergence and improved accuracy. The CNN is trained on coefficient fields containing individual perturbations and tested on both individual and previously unseen combined configurations. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the CNN generalizes to these more challenging configurations, while subsequent PDE-constrained optimization further refines the reconstructed coefficient. These results demonstrate the potential of combining data-driven initialization with physics-based optimization for efficient and accurate MRE reconstruction.

</details>


### [331] [Quantitative Mean-Field Limits for Recent Variants of Consensus-Based Optimization Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02901)
*Konstantin Haug*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This thesis derives rigorous propagation of chaos results for three consensus-based optimization (CBO) variants—δ-CBO, consensus freezing (CF), and consensus hopping (CH)—showing the interacting particle system converges to the mean-field equation as N→∞.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CBO is a robust zero-order, multi-particle method for high-dimensional global optimization, balancing stochastic exploration and deterministic contraction to a consensus point. While several CBO variants exist, rigorous justification that their finite-particle systems faithfully approximate the idealized mean-field dynamics was lacking—especially without restrictive conditioning on favorable events.

Method: The thesis studies three CBO variants: δ-CBO (constant diffusion), consensus freezing (piecewise CBO with fixed consensus point over intervals), and consensus hopping (deterministic jumps to Gaussians centered at previous consensus points). Using the coupling method, it derives propagation of chaos estimates in two regimes: (1) a high-probability set where empirical measures are uniformly bounded, and (2) an unrestricted setting using moment bounds and careful treatment of inter-particle dependence.

Result: For all three variants, the empirical measure of the interacting particle system converges to the solution of the corresponding mean-field equation as N→∞, with quantitative propagation of chaos estimates obtained in both the restricted and unrestricted regimes.

Conclusion: The thesis provides rigorous, quantitative propagation of chaos results for all three CBO variants, establishing that the particle systems faithfully approximate the idealized mean-field dynamics in the large-particle limit.

Abstract: Consensus-based optimization (CBO) is a robust and flexible zero-order, multi-particle class of methods devised to provide provable solutions to high-dimensional global optimization problems, including truly nonconvex and/or nonsmooth cases. The algorithmic framework is grounded in a principled trade-off between stochastic exploration of the search space and deterministic contraction toward a consensus point. This thesis is concerned with three recently established variants of CBO. The $δ$-CBO method is similar to the classic CBO method, with the difference that the diffusion function is constant. The consensus freezing (CF) scheme can be understood as a piecewise CBO method. The overall time horizon is divided into intervals in which the consensus point is fixed. The consensus hopping (CH) scheme can be understood as a deterministic iterative scheme that jumps at each iteration to a Gaussian distribution centered at the consensus point of the previous distribution. The central contribution of this thesis is the rigorous derivation of propagation of chaos results for all three schemes. For each variant, we establish that the empirical measure of the interacting particle system converges, as the number of particles $N$ approaches infinity, to the solution of the corresponding mean-field equation. Two regimes are treated: first, on a high-probability set on which the empirical measures are uniformly bounded; second, in the unrestricted setting, where moment bounds and a careful treatment of the inter-particle dependence yield convergence without imposing conditioning on favorable events. The propagation of chaos estimates are derived by the coupling method. Together, these results provide quantitative propagation of chaos estimates for all three CBO variants and establish that the particle systems faithfully approximate the idealized mean-field dynamics in the large-particle limit.

</details>


### [332] [On randomized batch-sampling coordinate descent methods for solving linear least-squares problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04685)
*Dong-Yue Xie,Xi Yang*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper introduces a coordinate-level batch-sampling distribution characterization to refine randomized block coordinate descent, deriving column-scaling-invariant convergence bounds and a learnable sampling strategy that accelerates convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing randomized block coordinate descent methods rely on static sampling rules with convergence bounds that are not sharp and are sensitive to column scaling of data matrices. There is a need for a finer-grained framework with tighter, scaling-invariant guarantees and the ability to adapt sampling to problem-specific structure.

Method: The authors characterize batch-sampling at the coordinate level to build a fine-grained randomized batch-sampling coordinate descent framework applicable to any block coordinate descent method under arbitrary static stochastic sampling. They derive new linear convergence bounds invariant to column scaling, and propose a learning-based sampling strategy that parameterizes the batch-sampling distribution and optimizes its parameters via gradient-based training.

Result: The new bounds are significantly sharper than existing results and better align with practical convergence rates, validated by theory and numerical experiments. On CT reconstruction problems, the learned sampling method converges faster than fixed sampling variants and outperforms tested adaptive coordinate descent methods and full gradient descent in computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Coordinate-level batch-sampling characterization yields tighter, scaling-invariant convergence bounds, and learned sampling distributions can effectively capture problem-specific structure to accelerate convergence within the proposed framework.

Abstract: This paper refines randomized block coordinate descent by introducing a coordinate-level batch-sampling distribution characterization, yielding a fine-grained randomized batch-sampling coordinate descent framework, which applies to any block coordinate descent method under an arbitrary static stochastic sampling rule for solving large-scale linear least-squares problems. Based on this framework, we derive new linear convergence rate bounds that are invariant to the column scaling of data matrices. Theoretical analysis and numerical experiments validate that the new bounds are significantly sharper than existing results and better aligned with the practical convergence rate. Furthermore, we proposed a learning-based sampling strategy by parameterizing the batch-sampling distribution and optimizing its parameters through gradient-based training. Preliminary numerical results on computed tomography (CT) reconstruction problems demonstrate that the resulting method converges significantly faster than variants using other fixed sampling rules, and outperforms tested adaptive coordinate descent methods and full gradient descent in computational efficiency, confirming that learned sampling distributions can effectively capture problem-specific structure to accelerate convergence within the framework.

</details>


### [333] [From Inverse Problems to Adjoint Gradients: A Discrete, Implementation-Oriented Tutorial on PDE-Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04789)
*Solmaz S. Kia*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: A discrete, implementation-oriented tutorial on the adjoint method for PDE-constrained optimization, deriving adjoint gradients step by step with a vibrating-string inverse problem as a running example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The adjoint method is central to gradients in PDE-constrained optimization (parameter estimation, model calibration, optimal control), but conceptual understanding and implementation details are often unclear to students and practitioners.

Method: A discrete, implementation-oriented tutorial starting from a PDE-governed inverse problem, formulating the optimization and deriving the adjoint gradient via iterative descent. It makes state, parameters, objective, and constraint explicit, derives the adjoint equation from the gradient computation, and addresses discretization effects, backward-in-time adjoint recursion, and gradient verification.

Result: Provides explicit derivations, implementation details, and the link between the mathematical development and the computational algorithm, illustrated through a vibrating-string inverse problem covering the full workflow from model formulation to adjoint-based optimization.

Conclusion: The tutorial offers a step-by-step understanding of adjoint gradient computation for advanced undergraduates, graduate students, and practitioners.

Abstract: The adjoint method is a standard approach for computing gradients in PDE-constrained optimization and central to parameter estimation, model calibration, and optimal control. This tutorial presents a discrete, implementation-oriented introduction to the method with an emphasis on conceptual understanding. Starting from an inverse problem governed by a partial differential equation, it develops the corresponding optimization problem and derives the adjoint gradient from the perspective of iterative descent methods. The roles of the state, the parameters, the objective, and the constraint are made explicit, and the adjoint equation is obtained directly from the gradient computation rather than postulated. Particular attention is given to explicit derivations, implementation details, and the connection between the mathematical development and the resulting computational algorithm. The tutorial also examines the influence of discretization on the adjoint construction, explains the origin of the backward-in-time adjoint recursion, and presents practical procedures for gradient verification. A vibrating-string inverse problem serves as a running example, illustrating the complete workflow from model formulation to adjoint-based optimization. The presentation is intended for advanced undergraduate students, graduate students, and practitioners seeking a step-by-step understanding of adjoint gradient computation.

</details>


### [334] [Polynomial Acceleration of Alternating Projections: Chebyshev Acceleration, Convergence Rates, and Retractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06150)
*Dengyu Zheng,Shixiang Chen*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: FPMA is a framework for accelerating smooth pointwise-fixing maps near manifolds, providing local R-linear convergence and polynomial (e.g., Chebyshev) acceleration, with application to alternating projections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alternating projections near a clean intersection of smooth manifolds converge slowly when the Friedrichs angle is small, so faster local acceleration with preserved limit behavior is needed.

Method: Fixed-Point Manifold Acceleration (FPMA) framework uses regularity and uniform normal spectral stability to establish local R-linear convergence and a smooth limiting map inducing a local retraction; consistent polynomial acceleration with p(1)=1 preserves first-order normal response, and a curvature criterion characterizes second-order induced retractions.

Result: FPMA yields local R-linear convergence for fixed-degree Chebyshev-accelerated exact AP and second-order retractions; for uniform Friedrichs angle θ_F∈(0,π/2) it strictly improves the local rate per AP evaluation, predicting O(θ_F^{-1}) evaluations versus O(θ_F^{-2}) for ordinary AP as θ_F↓0.

Conclusion: FPMA provides convergence guarantees and polynomial acceleration for exact AP, and extends to inexact, relaxed, and generalized AP under suitable regularity and stability conditions.

Abstract: Alternating projections (AP) near a clean intersection of smooth manifolds can converge slowly when the Friedrichs angle is small. We develop the \emph{Fixed-Point Manifold Acceleration (FPMA) framework} for {local convergence and polynomial acceleration of} smooth maps that fix a manifold pointwise. {Under suitable regularity and uniform normal spectral stability, FPMA establishes local R-linear convergence and a smooth limiting map inducing a local retraction. At fixed points, consistent polynomial acceleration with $p(1)=1$ preserves the selected limit's first-order response to normal initial perturbations whenever the transformed normal blocks are uniformly spectrally stable. Under sufficient smoothness, we give a curvature criterion for second-order induced retractions and show that stable consistent polynomial acceleration preserves both the quadratic retraction expansion and this criterion. {As a special case, we establish new local R-linear convergence guarantees for fixed-degree Chebyshev-accelerated exact AP and second-order induced retractions. For uniform Friedrichs angle $θ_F\in(0,π/2)$, acceleration strictly improves the local convergence rate per AP evaluation. The large-degree limiting spectral factor at fixed $θ_F$ predicts $O(θ_F^{-1})$ evaluations for fixed relative error reduction as $θ_F\downarrow0$, versus $O(θ_F^{-2})$ for ordinary AP.}} {The framework also covers} inexact, relaxed, and generalized AP {under their respective regularity and stability conditions.}

</details>


### [335] [A Relaxed Maximum-Based Normal \texorpdfstring{$S$}{S}-Iteration Method for Generalized Absolute Value Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06154)
*Abhishek Kumar Singh Sengar,Bharat Kumar,Deepmala*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper proposes RMNSI, a relaxed maximum-based normal S-iteration method for solving generalized absolute value equations (GAVEs), with global and local convergence analysis and competitive numerical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving GAVEs efficiently is important, but existing methods often require auxiliary matrix selection or restrictive conditions. A simpler fixed-point scheme with a tunable relaxation parameter and reusable factorization is desirable.

Method: RMNSI combines a maximum-based fixed-point formulation with a constant relaxation parameter, avoiding auxiliary matrix selection. Both iteration stages use the same coefficient matrix A+B, so a single factorization is reused. Convergence analysis includes a spectral condition for unique solvability and global convergence, an admissible relaxation-parameter range, R-linear convergence and error estimates, plus a local analysis based on the solution sign pattern.

Result: Under non-singularity of A+B, a single spectral condition ensures unique solvability and global convergence from any initial vector. Numerical experiments on complementarity-derived, dense mixed-sign, and asymmetric ridge-regression problems show competitive efficiency and accuracy versus recent methods. The preferred relaxation parameter depends mainly on matrix structure and diagonal shift, with weak dependence on problem dimension.

Conclusion: RMNSI is an effective and theoretically supported solver for GAVEs, offering simple implementation, strong convergence guarantees, and competitive performance.

Abstract: A relaxed maximum-based normal $S$-iteration method (RMNSI) is proposed for solving generalized absolute value equations (GAVEs). The method combines a maximum-based fixed-point formulation with a constant relaxation parameter and avoids the selection of an auxiliary matrix. Assuming that $A+B$ is non-singular, both stages require linear systems with the same coefficient matrix $A+B$, allowing a single factorization to be reused throughout the iteration. A single spectral condition is established that guarantees unique solvability of the GAVE and global convergence from an arbitrary initial vector. An admissible range of the relaxation parameter is characterized, and $R$-linear convergence and error estimates are derived. Since the global condition can be conservative, a local convergence analysis based on the solution sign pattern is also presented. Numerical experiments on complementarity-derived, dense mixed-sign, and asymmetric ridge-regression problems are presented, and comparisons with several recent methods demonstrate the competitive efficiency and accuracy of RMNSI. The results further indicate that the preferred relaxation parameter depends mainly on the matrix structure and diagonal shift, while its dependence on the problem dimension is generally weak.

</details>


### [336] [Asymptotic analyis of an optimal control problem for a phase field tumor growth model with hyperbolic relaxation of the chemical potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04380)
*Pierluigi Colli,Elisabetta Rocca,Jürgen Sprekels*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper analyzes optimal control for a Cahn-Hilliard phase-field tumor growth model where the chemical potential's parabolic relaxation is replaced by a hyperbolic one (relaxation parameter α), studying the asymptotics as α→0 and relating optimal controls of relaxed and unrelaxed problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tumor growth models of Cahn-Hilliard type often assume a parabolic relaxation of the chemical potential. Replacing it with a hyperbolic relaxation (possibly singular potential) is more realistic, but the behavior as the relaxation parameter vanishes must be understood to connect the relaxed and unrelaxed regimes.

Method: Optimal control analysis of a Cahn-Hilliard phase-field tumor growth model with hyperbolic relaxation of the chemical potential and possibly singular potential. Studies the limit α→0, proving convergence of state and adjoint variables, and introduces an "adapted" cost functional to show fixed optimal controls of the unrelaxed problem are limits of relaxed minimizers.

Result: State and adjoint state variables of the relaxed problem converge to those of the unrelaxed regime (α=0). Weak limits of optimal controls of the relaxed problem are optimal for the unrelaxed case. Every fixed optimal control u* of the unrelaxed system is the limit of relaxed minimizers {u*_α}. The variational inequalities (first-order optimality conditions) of the relaxed problems converge to that of the unrelaxed problem.

Conclusion: The hyperbolic relaxation problem consistently approximates the unrelaxed optimal control problem as α→0, both in state/adjoint convergence and in optimality conditions, with the adapted cost functional bridging the two formulations.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the optimal control of a phase field tumor growth model of Cahn--Hilliard type with possibly singular potential in which the often assumed parabolic relaxation of the chemical potential is replaced by a hyperbolic one. We investigate the asymptotic behavior of the control problem as the relaxation parameter $\,α\,$ approaches zero. It is shown that the corresponding state and adjoint state variables of the relaxed problem converge in a suitable sense to their counterparts for the unrelaxed regime where $α=0$. Also, weak limits of optimal controls for the relaxed problem can be shown to be optimal controls for the unrelaxed case. Moreover, every fixed optimal control $\,u^*\,$ for the unrelaxed system turns out to be the limit of a family $\{u^*_α\}_{α>0}$ of minimizers of suitable optimal control problems for the relaxed systems, where the original cost functional is replaced by the so-called ``adapted'' cost functional. It is also shown that the variational inequalities satisfied by $u^*_α$, which constitute the first-order necessary optimality conditions of the relaxed problems with adapted cost functional, converge to the corresponding variational inequality fulfilled by $u^*$ for the unrelaxed problem with the original (nonadapted) cost functional.

</details>


### [337] [Observability from Measurable Sets for One-Dimensional Heat Equations with Bounded Spacetime Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05704)
*Xiaomin Zhu*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper proves an observability inequality for the Dirichlet heat equation with a space-time dependent bounded potential, from any measurable spacetime subset of positive measure, with uniform constants over bounded potentials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Observability and null controllability for heat equations are central in control theory. Existing results often require specific observation sets or lack uniform constants over potentials. This work addresses observability from general measurable subsets with uniformity over potentials.

Method: Combines observation estimates on finite-dimensional spaces transported by the evolution with decay estimates for the distance to those spaces.

Result: Establishes an observability inequality from every measurable spacetime subset of positive measure, with constants uniform over potentials with prescribed L-infinity bound. By duality, positive spacetime measure is equivalent to null controllability by L^2 distributed controls; controls can be chosen bounded in time with values in L^2. When almost every spatial slice has a fixed positive lower measure bound, the observability constant is bounded by C_0 e^{C_1/T}, uniform in slice locations.

Conclusion: Positive spacetime measure of the observation set characterizes null controllability for the Dirichlet heat equation with bounded space-time potentials, with uniform observability constants and control bounds.

Abstract: This paper study observability for the Dirichlet heat equation on a bounded interval with a real bounded potential depending on space and time. We prove an observability inequality from every measurable subset of spacetime with positive measure. The constant is uniform over potentials with a prescribed $L^\infty$ bound. By duality, positive spacetime measure is equivalent to null controllability by square-integrable distributed controls. Controls can also be chosen bounded in time with values in $L^2$. If almost every spatial slice of the observation set has a fixed positive lower bound on its measure, the observability constant is bounded above by $C_0e^{C_1/T}$, uniformly in the locations of the slices. The proof combines observation estimates on finite-dimensional spaces transported by the evolution with decay estimates for the distance to those spaces.

</details>

### [338] [A Renormalization-Group Hierarchy of Stochastic Effective Dynamics Learned through Path Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06099)
*Yuki Yasuda,Tobias Bischoff*

Main category: nlin.CD

TL;DR: This paper unifies stochastic renormalization group (RG) with a path-integral description of time evolution, yielding a multiscale path formulation where a neural predictor and score function are two aspects of the same object, enabling simulation, unconditional generation, and super-resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify spatial coarse-graining (RG) and temporal path descriptions of multiscale dynamics, connecting predictor-based simulation and score-based generation within a single framework.

Method: Combines stochastic RG (diffusion with scale-dependent Laplacian damping plus Gaussian noise) with path integrals via an Onsager–Machlup action per spatial scale. The predictor is optimized by minimizing KL divergence between RG and path-integral path distributions. The optimal predictor contains the cross-scale score. Computed by a neural network in two realizations: score via automatic differentiation, or as an extra denoising-trained output.

Result: Both realizations are validated on two multiscale systems: Kolmogorov flow and the two-timescale Lorenz-96 model. The second realization lowers computational cost while the first stays faithful to the formulation.

Conclusion: The predictor and score are two aspects of the same multiscale path formulation, unifying simulation, unconditional generation, and super-resolution, with practical neural implementations.

Abstract: This study combines a stochastic renormalization group (RG) in space with a path-integral description of the time evolution, giving a spatial hierarchy of coarse-grained dynamics together with the distribution over spatiotemporal paths. The RG is defined as a diffusion process that applies scale-dependent Laplacian damping together with additive Gaussian noise. The time evolution at each spatial scale is formulated as an Onsager--Machlup action, with a drift (i.e., the predictor) and white noise whose amplitude is fixed by the RG. The predictor of these dynamics is optimized by minimizing the Kullback--Leibler divergence between the path distributions from the RG and from the path-integral description. The optimal predictor contains the score function that connects the spatial scales, so the predictor and the score are two aspects of the same multiscale path formulation. The formulation unifies simulation using the predictor, unconditional generation using the score, and super-resolution using both. The predictor has no closed form, so it is computed by a neural network in two realizations that differ only in how the score is computed. The first realization obtains the score by automatic differentiation of the path distribution, remaining faithful to that formulation. The second obtains the score as an additional network output trained by denoising, at a lower computational cost. These two realizations are validated through numerical experiments on two representative multiscale systems, the Kolmogorov flow and the two-timescale Lorenz-96 model.

</details>


<div id='math-ph'></div>

# math-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [339] [Quantum Fluctuations in Bose--Einstein Condensates:Microscopic Correlations and Moment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06367)
*Mauris Chueng,Keheng Zhu,Hexiang Wang*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: The paper propagates higher moments of quantum fluctuations and microscopic correlation energy in the 3D Gross-Pitaevskii regime, identifying excitation-energy measures at scale N^2 and bounding fluctuation and energy moments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum fluctuations and correlation energy in the Gross-Pitaevskii regime require control beyond second moments; higher-moment bounds and spectral measures were not established to give uniform-in-N control.

Method: Uses exact many-body evolution with smooth compactly supported nonnegative radial interaction; propagates the third spectral moment of a positive correlated energy; analyzes excitation-energy measure at scale N^2 weighted by first energy; employs relative scattering operator -2Δ+v, dressed contact, and a finite cubic approximation.

Result: Third depletion-number moment and sixth collective fluctuation moments bounded uniformly in N; excitation-energy measure identified as spectral measure of -2Δ+v times a time-dependent dressed contact; contact strict positivity proven; fluctuation moments and energy moments of order p>1 bounded below by cN^{2p-2}; scaled interaction-energy moments determined for 1≤p<2, second moment O(N^2); cubic approximation errors O(N^{-3/16}) and O(N^{-5/4}).

Conclusion: Higher-moment and spectral-measure control extends Gross-Pitaevskii fluctuation theory, providing uniform-in-N bounds and positivity of the contact, with explicit approximation error rates.

Abstract: We study higher moments of quantum fluctuations and microscopic correlation energy in the three-dimensional Gross--Pitaevskii regime. For a smooth, compactly supported, nonnegative radial interaction and an $N$-independent normalized initial condensate wave function in $H^6$, we propagate the third spectral moment of a positive correlated energy along the exact many-body evolution. A uniform initial bound controls the third moment of the depletion number and sixth moments of collective one-particle fluctuations on every compact time interval, independently of $N$. For a nonzero interaction and a smooth, compactly supported initial condensate, we also identify the excitation-energy measure at scale $N^2$, weighted by the first energy. This limit holds for norm-convergent pure-state families with a uniform bound on the initial third correlated-energy moment. Its positive-energy part is a spectral measure of the relative scattering operator $-2Δ+v$, multiplied by a time-dependent dressed contact. We compute the radial density and prove strict positivity of the contact for nonzero references with finite kinetic energy and finite mean excitation number. The same evolving states have bounded physical fluctuation moments and excitation-energy moments of order $p>1$ bounded below by a positive multiple of $N^{2p-2}$, in the extended spectral sense. Inverse powers of the positive initial energy construct admissible pure and mixed families. For the norm-convergent pure-state class, we determine the scaled interaction-energy moments for $1\le p<2$ and bound the second moment by $O(N^2)$. A finite cubic approximation has norm error $O(N^{-3/16})$ on the energy form domain and $O(N^{-5/4})$ on a stronger initial domain of its generator.

</details>
