### [340] [Fast Nanophotonic Inverse Design using Precomputed Numerical Green Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02821)
*Thi Phuong Thao Nguyen,James Wang,Constantine Sideris*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This paper extends the Precomputed Numerical Green Function (PNGF) method to nanophotonic inverse design, achieving orders-of-magnitude speedup by avoiding expensive full-wave simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse design in nanophotonics is powerful for realizing high-performance, non-intuitive devices, but its main bottleneck is the reliance on time-consuming, computationally expensive full-wave electromagnetic simulations.

Method: The authors extend PNGF to nanophotonic inverse design. After a single, fully parallelizable precomputation step, the objective function and its gradient are evaluated via simple calculations scaling linearly with design-region size. A low-rank matrix update technique further reduces objective evaluation cost.

Result: Forward problem evaluation is accelerated by multiple orders of magnitude with no accuracy loss versus full-wave solutions. Objective evaluation reaches sub-millisecond times per iteration. Design examples using gradient-based level-set optimization and tile-flipping direct binary search achieve over three orders of magnitude speedup in design time.

Conclusion: The proposed approach yields an ultrafast photonic inverse design platform capable of designing new devices from scratch in seconds.

Abstract: Inverse design has been transformative in nanophotonics, providing an automated means of realizing high-performance, non-intuitive devices. However, the primary bottleneck of existing inverse design approaches is their reliance on time-consuming and computationally expensive full-wave electromagnetic simulations. In this work, we extend the Precomputed Numerical Green Function (PNGF) method for the first time to nanophotonic inverse design, accelerating evaluation of the forward problem by multiple orders of magnitude with no loss in accuracy compared to the full-wave solution. After a single, fully parallelizable precomputation step, both the objective function and its gradient can be evaluated through simple calculations whose cost scales linearly with the size of the design region. A low-rank matrix update technique further reduces the cost of objective function evaluation, yielding sub-millisecond computation times per iteration. Design examples using both gradient-based level-set optimization and tile-flipping direct binary search often achieve more than three orders of magnitude speedup in design time, resulting in an ultrafast photonic inverse design platform that can design new devices from scratch in seconds.

</details>


### [341] [Fast Nanophotonic Inverse Design using Precomputed Numerical Green Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02821)
*Thi Phuong Thao Nguyen,James Wang,Constantine Sideris*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This paper extends the Precomputed Numerical Green Function (PNGF) method to nanophotonic inverse design, achieving 3+ orders of magnitude speedup by avoiding full-wave simulations, enabling device design from scratch in seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing inverse design approaches in nanophotonics are bottlenecked by time-consuming, computationally expensive full-wave electromagnetic simulations.

Method: Extends PNGF to nanophotonic inverse design. After a single, fully parallelizable precomputation step, the objective function and its gradient are evaluated via simple calculations scaling linearly with design-region size. A low-rank matrix update technique further reduces objective evaluation cost. Demonstrated with gradient-based level-set optimization and tile-flipping direct binary search.

Result: Multiple orders of magnitude acceleration of forward-problem evaluation with no accuracy loss vs. full-wave solutions; sub-millisecond computation per iteration; design examples often achieve 3+ orders of magnitude speedup in design time.

Conclusion: PNGF yields an ultrafast photonic inverse design platform capable of designing new devices from scratch in seconds.

Abstract: Inverse design has been transformative in nanophotonics, providing an automated means of realizing high-performance, non-intuitive devices. However, the primary bottleneck of existing inverse design approaches is their reliance on time-consuming and computationally expensive full-wave electromagnetic simulations. In this work, we extend the Precomputed Numerical Green Function (PNGF) method for the first time to nanophotonic inverse design, accelerating evaluation of the forward problem by multiple orders of magnitude with no loss in accuracy compared to the full-wave solution. After a single, fully parallelizable precomputation step, both the objective function and its gradient can be evaluated through simple calculations whose cost scales linearly with the size of the design region. A low-rank matrix update technique further reduces the cost of objective function evaluation, yielding sub-millisecond computation times per iteration. Design examples using both gradient-based level-set optimization and tile-flipping direct binary search often achieve more than three orders of magnitude speedup in design time, resulting in an ultrafast photonic inverse design platform that can design new devices from scratch in seconds.

</details>


### [342] [Deep learning enables large-scale inverse design of free-form metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05021)
*Viktor A. Lilja,Khosro Zangeneh Kamali,Mikael Käll,Philippe Tassin*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A physics-tailored deep-learning surrogate model generalizes small-scale electromagnetic simulations to metasurfaces orders of magnitude larger, enabling fast, system-scale free-form inverse design validated by experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metasurfaces can control light with subwavelength structures, offering compact and unique optical functionalities, but inverse design over large areas is blocked by the massive computational cost of repeated electromagnetic simulations.

Method: The authors build a deep-learning surrogate model tailored to the physics of metasurfaces, exploiting locality and symmetries of electromagnetic interactions to generalize from small-scale simulations to much larger devices. It captures nonlocal interactions and predicts optical fields over four orders of magnitude faster than the conventional solver, enabling gradient-based topology optimization under arbitrary illumination.

Result: The approach was validated by inverse design and experimental realization of free-form metagratings with diffraction angles up to 75 degrees, and a millimeter-scale free-form holographic metasurface over 1,000 wavelengths across with 1.6 billion degrees of freedom.

Conclusion: By decoupling the scale of electromagnetic simulation from the scale of inverse design, this method enables system-scale free-form photonic devices while preserving nanoscale design freedom.

Abstract: Metasurfaces are ultrathin optical elements that control light through carefully engineered structures smaller than the wavelength of light, enabling compact devices with functionalities that are difficult to achieve with conventional optics. However, exploiting their full design freedom over large areas has been prohibited by the large computational cost of repeated electromagnetic simulations required for inverse design. Here we introduce a physics-tailored deep-learning surrogate model that exploits the locality and symmetries of electromagnetic interactions to generalize from small-scale simulations to metasurfaces orders of magnitude larger. The model captures nonlocal interactions while predicting optical fields more than four orders of magnitude faster than the conventional electromagnetic solver it is trained on, enabling gradient-based topology optimization under arbitrary illumination. We validate the approach through the inverse design and experimental realization of free-form metagratings with diffraction angles up to 75 degrees, as well as a millimeter-scale free-form holographic metasurface more than 1,000 wavelengths across and comprising 1.6 billion degrees of freedom. By decoupling the scale of electromagnetic simulation from the scale of inverse design, our approach enables system-scale free-form photonic devices that retain nanoscale design freedom.

</details>

### [343] [On solid angles and simplex shape measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05883)
*Martin W. Licht*

Main category: math.MG

TL;DR: The minimum solid angle of a simplex is proven to be a shape measure in any dimension, with new inequalities relating it to fullness that generalize the minimum angle condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This property was previously known only in dimensions two and three; a higher-dimensional generalization of the minimum solid angle as a shape measure was missing, motivating the extension of the minimum angle condition.

Method: The authors prove inequalities between the minimum solid angle of a non-degenerate simplex and its fullness, showing fullness is bounded above by the minimum solid angle and below by its square, up to dimension-dependent constants. They also review several simplex shape measures and their relationships.

Result: The minimum solid angle is established as a shape measure in any dimension, and the fullness bounds in terms of the minimum solid angle hold with exponents that are optimal for dimensions three and higher.

Conclusion: The work generalizes the minimum angle condition to arbitrary dimensions and clarifies relationships among simplex shape measures.

Abstract: The minimum solid angle of a simplex is shown to be a shape measure in any dimension. While this has been known in dimensions two and three, the higher-dimensional generalization is new. We prove inequalities between the minimum solid angle of a non-degenerate simplex and its fullness. Up to dimension-dependent constants, the fullness is bounded from above by the minimum solid angle and from below by the square of the minimum solid angle. The exponents are optimal for three and higher dimensions. This work therefore generalizes the minimum angle condition. We also review several simplex shape measures and their relationships.

</details>
